    THE CHAISE LONGUE THEOREM
 Conjecture 1.2 of Degtyarev–Shimada for the Fermat varieties of every degree in every
  even dimension, and the integral Hodge conjecture for those whose degree is prime,
                            equal to 4, or prime to (n+1)!

                                         Rafael Amichis Luengo
                      Madrid, Spain · tretoef@gmail.com · 4 October 2026 · version 12



Abstract
Let X ⊂ Pn+1 be the complex Fermat variety of degree m ≥ 3 and even dimension n = 2k, and let L(X) ⊆ Hn(X;
Z) be the subgroup generated by the classes of its standard k-dimensional linear subspaces. Degtyarev and
Shimada conjectured that Hn(X; Z)/L(X) is torsion free [DS, Conjecture 1.2]. We prove this conjecture for
every degree m ≥ 3 in every even dimension, modulo two topological results used by [DS]: Pham's theo‐
rem on the homology of the affine Fermat variety, and their intersection numbers. Its algebraic form — the
quotient Z[G]/(ψJ) of the group ring of [DS] is free abelian of rank m2k+1 − Qk(m) — is proved with no topol‐
ogy, and it has been verified for every degree by a complete proof in the proof assistant Lean 4 with the
Mathlib library, with no gap and no axiom beyond the three standard ones. The proof reduces the conjecture,
prime by prime, to dimension counts in truncated polynomial rings, proved by peeling off one variable at a
time. Consequences: the integral Hodge conjecture for X whenever m is prime, or m = 4, or m is prime to
(n+1)!, with the integral Hodge classes of middle degree generated by the standard linear subspaces — in
particular for the Fermat quartic of every even dimension; and the primitivity of the linear subspaces of the
partial Fermat varieties of [DS], which [DS] proved conditionally on the conjecture. This work has not been
refereed by a human expert.

1.1 The conjecture
We follow [DS]. Let n = 2k with k ≥ 0, let m ≥ 3, and let X ⊂ Pn+1 be the Fermat variety z0m + ⋯ + zn+1m = 0.
All homology is with coefficients in Z.
      𝒥 is the set of partitions of the index set {0, 1, …, n+1} into unordered pairs, written J = [[j0,k0], …,
      [jk,kk]] with ji < ki and j0 < j1 < ⋯ < jk; in particular j0 = 0. There are |𝒥| = (2k+1)!! of them.
      For J ∈ 𝒥 and β = (β0, …, βk) with βim = −1, the standard k-space LJ,β ⊂ X is given by zki = βi zji (i = 0,
      …, k).
      For ∅ ≠ K ⊆ 𝒥, LK(X) ⊆ Hn(X) is the subgroup generated by the classes [LJ,β] with J ∈ K; L(X) := L𝒥(X).
Conjecture 1.2 of [DS]. The group Hn(X)/L(X) is torsion free.
For k = 1 the conjecture is a theorem of Degtyarev [De14]; for m prime to 6 it implies that the Picard group of
the Fermat surface is generated by its lines. For k ≥ 2 it has been confirmed by computer for (n, m) = (4, m)
with 3 ≤ m ≤ 12, and for (6,3), (6,4), (6,5), (8,3) [DS, §5]. Aljovin, Movasati and Villaflor [AMV] compared the
lattice of linear cycles with the lattice of Hodge cycles by computing elementary divisors, for the Fermat sur‐
faces of degree 3 ≤ m ≤ 14 and the Fermat fourfolds of degree 3 ≤ m ≤ 6 [AMV, Table 1]. The conjecture is
restated as open in [De15, Conjecture 4.4]. Before the present work (versions 4 to 12 of this paper) we knew
of no proof for any k ≥ 2 beyond these cells; in particular it had not been decided for any odd degree that is
not a prime power when k ≥ 2 (the odd degrees in the table of [DS, §5] are prime powers). As [DS, §1] point
out, for prime m the rational Hodge classes of X are spanned by the classes of the standard spaces, so that the
conjecture would upgrade this to the integral Hodge classes; §12 carries this out, for prime m and for more
degrees.

1.2 The main theorem
Put N := 2k+2 = n+2, and for m ≥ 3 let
 Qk(m) := N!·[xN] I0(2x)(m−1)/2 (m odd), Qk(m) := N!·[xN] cosh(x)·I0(2x)(m−2)/2 (m even), I0(2x) = Σb≥0 x2b/(b!)2.
For every m, Qk(m) is the number |Γ𝒥| of [DS, Definition 1.3] (Lemma 2.2 for odd m, Lemma 9.1 for even m),
so that rank L(X) = Qk(m) + 1 by Theorem 0(c) below. In the algebraic statements of §1.5 and in §2–§7, Qk(q)
is always taken at an odd q.

     MAIN THEOREM. Let m ≥ 3 and k ≥ 0. Then H2k(X; Z)/L(X) is torsion free for the Fermat variety
     X of degree m and dimension 2k. Equivalently, L(X) is a primitive sublattice of H2k(X; Z), of rank
     Qk(m) + 1.

For k = 0 the statement is trivial: X is a set of m points and L(X) = H0(X). From now on k ≥ 1 whenever topol‐
ogy is involved.
The Main Theorem has an algebraic form, which is proved here with no topology at all (Corollary 7.7 for odd
m, Theorem 9.11 for even m). Let R := Z[t1, …, t2k+1]/(t1m − 1, …, t2k+1m − 1) be the integral group ring of G =
(Z/m)2k+1 and let ψJ ∈ R be the generators of [DS], recalled in §2.

     MAIN THEOREM′ (algebraic form). Let m ≥ 3 and k ≥ 1. Then R/(ψJ : J ∈ 𝒥) is a free abelian
     group of rank m2k+1 − Qk(m). Equivalently, dimFp Fp[G]/(ψ̄ J : J ∈ 𝒥) = m2k+1 − Qk(m) for every
     prime p.
By [DS, Theorem 1.1(a)] (Theorem 0(a) below) the torsion of R/(ψJ : J ∈ 𝒥) is that of H2k(X; Z)/L(X), so Main
Theorem′ implies the Main Theorem; the rank of L(X) is [DS, Theorem 1.4]. These two statements are the
only places where topology enters.
The proof has five parts.
      §2 (the translation). Using the results of [DS] collected in Theorem 0, torsion-freeness at m is equiva‐
      lent to a dimension count over Fp for each prime p | m (Proposition 2.1); for m = q = pv the count is (S)
      dimFp(DJ : J ∈ 𝒥)C = Qk(q) (Proposition 2.5).
      §5 (odd prime powers). Theorem B below proves (S).
      §6–§7 (odd degrees that are not prime powers). For odd m = q·r with q = pv, p ∤ r, r > 1, the count
      at p splits over the colourings of the coordinates by r-th roots of unity into a sum of products of block
      dimensions (Proposition 6.9). The blocks are instances of Theorem 5.3 and bipartite ideals, whose
      count is Theorem C (§7).
      §8 (the odd box). Theorem O below: the count of Theorem B at an odd box, on a grid with one zero. It
      is the root case of Theorem 8.11.
      §9 (even degrees). For m = 2v the ideal of leading forms of the ψ̄ J in characteristic 2 contains the
      product of s1 ⋯ s2k+1 with the ideal of Theorem O for the box 2v − 1, an isomorphic copy of it in anoth‐
      er ring (Proposition 9.2). For the other even m the colour reduction of §6 applies at every prime; the
      block of the colour −1 at an odd prime is the ideal of Theorem O for the box pv, the block of the colour
      1 at the prime 2 is the problem for the degree 2v, and the pair blocks are bipartite ideals, at the prime 2
      with an even box (Theorem 9.11).
Sections 5–9 prove lower bounds for the dimensions of the ideals: Theorems 5.3, 7.6 and 8.11. The matching
upper bound is the inequality of Proposition 2.1, and it is algebraic too: it is [DS, Claim 4.3] (Theorem 0(b)), a
count of points by the Chinese remainder theorem over a field of characteristic 0 (Appendix B, Step 6). So
Main Theorem′ is proved without topology; topology enters only through Theorem 0(a) and 0(c), which iden‐
tify the torsion of R/(ψJ) with that of H2k(X; Z)/L(X) and give the rank of L(X). Theorems 5.9 and 5.9′, §4, §10
and §11 are not on the path of the proof, and neither is the equality in Theorem O (Corollary 8.13): the proof
uses only its inequality ≥. Corollaries 7.8 and 9.12 are consequences of the proof. Main Theorem′ has been
proved again, for every degree, in the proof assistant Lean 4 (§14.8), and so has Theorem O.
The cubic case q = 3 has a shorter proof with an explicit basis given by ballot paths (§4, Theorem 4.1), and so
has the box r = 3, which is all that the quartics need (§8.8, Theorem 8.15).

1.3 The integral Hodge conjecture
For a smooth complex projective variety Y, the integral Hodge conjecture in degree 2j asks whether every
class in H2j(Y; Z) ∩ Hj,j(Y) is the class of an algebraic cycle with integer coefficients. It is false in general [AH],
[Kol], and it remains open for many varieties for which the rational Hodge conjecture is known; for Fermat
varieties it is the question studied in [AMV]. Write Hdg(X) ⊆ Hn(X; Z) for the group of integral Hodge classes
of middle degree (§12.1).

      COROLLARY H. Let m ≥ 3, k ≥ 1, and let X be the Fermat variety of degree m and dimension 2k.
        (i) If m is prime, or m = 4, or every prime factor of m is at least 2k + 3, then Hdg(X) = L(X):
        every integral Hodge class of middle degree is an integral linear combination of classes of stan‐
        dard k-spaces. In particular the integral Hodge conjecture holds for X in every degree.
        (ii) For every m, L(X) is a primitive sublattice of Hdg(X) and Hdg(X)/L(X) is free of rank |𝔅| −
        Qk(m), where 𝔅 is the set of Hodge characters of §12.1. It is zero if and only if m is prime, or m =
          4, or every prime factor of m is at least 2k + 3.

For odd m, «every prime factor of m is at least 2k + 3» is the same as the condition gcd(m, (n+1)!) = 1 of
[AMV, §1], which we use in the title. (Version 11 wrote gcd(m, (n+2)!) = 1. It is the same condition: a prime
that divides n + 2 = 2(k + 1) and m is at most k + 1, so it divides (n+1)! as well.) For even m the only degree
in (i) is m = 4: the integral Hodge conjecture holds for the Fermat quartic of every even dimension,
and its integral Hodge classes of middle degree are generated by the standard linear subspaces. The
proof is in §12. It combines the Main Theorem with the classical description of the rational Hodge classes of
X by characters [SK], [Shi79], [Ran] ((H3) of §12.1), and with the determination, due to Aoki [Aok, Theorem
A], of the degrees in which every Hodge character is of pair type; for prime m and for m = 4 we give short
proofs of this last fact (§12.1, (H4) and (H4′)). The new input is the primitivity of L(X). The classical fact that
the rational Hodge classes of pair type are spanned by linear subspaces [Ran, Theorem 4.9] is not used: over
Z it follows from the Main Theorem.
What was known. For k = 1 the integral Hodge conjecture is the Lefschetz (1,1) theorem, and (i) says that the
lines generate the Néron–Severi group, which was known when gcd(m, 6) = 1 [SSvL], [De14], and classically
for the cubic surface; for odd m these are exactly the degrees of (i) at k = 1. For m = 4: at k = 1 it is the state‐
ment that the lines of the Fermat quartic surface generate its Néron–Severi group, of rank 20, which is a re‐
sult of Mizukami [Miz] (1975; [SSvL, §3 and §6] record it, attribute it to Mizukami and Inose and give anoth‐
er proof; we have not read [Miz]), and which also follows from [De14]; at k = 2 it was verified by computer in
[AMV], whose abstract states the integral Hodge conjecture for the quartic and quintic Fermat fourfolds; at k
= 2, 3 the primitivity of L(X) was confirmed by computer in [DS, §5], so that (i) already followed there by the
argument of §12.3. For m = 4 and k ≥ 4 it is new, to our knowledge. For (k, m) = (2, 3) the integral Hodge
conjecture itself follows from Voisin's theorem on cubic fourfolds [Voi, Theorem 2.11]; as [AMV, §1] note, the
stronger statement Hdg(X) = L(X) does not follow from it. The cells (2, 3) and (2, 5) were verified by comput‐
er in [AMV]. In each prime cell where [DS, §5] confirmed the primitivity of L(X) by computer, namely (k, m)
= (2, 3), (2, 5), (2, 7), (2, 11), (3, 3), (3, 5), (4, 3), (i) already followed from that computation and the classical
facts by the argument of §12.3; [AMV, §1] make this observation for the cells of their table. The author had
also verified the primitivity of L(X) by computer, before the present proof, in the prime cells (k, m) = (2, 13),
(2, 17), (2, 19), (3, 7), (4, 5), (5, 3) [Rep]; three of them, (2, 13), (3, 7) and (5, 3), are re-run in §14.1. What is
new, for k ≥ 2, is the uniform statement: the quartics of dimension at least 8, every other cell with m prime,
and every cell of (i) with m not prime, for instance (k, m) = (2, 49), (2, 77), (3, 121), (3, 143); to our knowledge
no cell of (i) with m not prime and k ≥ 2 had been decided before. Part (ii) is new for k ≥ 2, to our knowledge;
for k = 1 it follows from [De14] and the rank |𝔅| + 1 of (H3). Recent work on the rational Hodge conjecture
for Fermat varieties is complementary to ours: Jumagulov [Jum] gave a computer-assisted proof for the
Fermat fourfolds of odd degree at most 199; Miranda, Movasati, Rufino and Villaflor [MMRV] prove it for the
Fermat varieties of every dimension and every degree d < 65 other than 44, 51, 52, by reducing the lengths of
Aoki's exceptional tuples; and Favero and Kelly [FK] prove it for the Fermat fourfold of degree 33, through an
explicit formula for Chern characters. These statements are rational; ours concern the lattice L(X) over Z.

1.4 Partial Fermat varieties
Let 0 ≤ s ≤ d. Following [DS, §1], a partial Fermat variety is a smooth hypersurface Ws ⊂ P2d+1 given by
f0(z0, z1) + f1(z2, z3) + ⋯ + fd(z2d, z2d+1) = 0, where the fi are binary forms of degree m, f0 = ⋯ = fs = xm + ym,
and fs+1, …, fd are generic, pairwise distinct and distinct from xm + ym. [DS, §1] exhibit (2s+1)!!·md+1 linear d-
spaces on it.

      COROLLARY W. Let m ≥ 3 and 0 ≤ s ≤ d.
         (i) The classes of the (2s+1)!!·md+1 linear d-spaces of [DS, §1] on Ws generate a primitive sublat‐
         tice of H2d(Ws; Z).
         (ii) On the Fermat variety X of dimension 2d, let 𝒥(s, d) ⊆ 𝒥 be the set of matchings that contain
         the pairs {2i, 2i+1} for s < i ≤ d. Then L𝒥(s,d)(X) is primitive in H2d(X; Z), of rank Qs(m)(m−1)d−s
         + 1.

[DS, Corollary 1.7] states (i) under the assumption that Conjecture 1.2 holds for the Fermat varieties of di‐
mension 2s and degree m, and proves it for s = 0, 1; [De15, §4.2–§4.3] reduces (i) and (ii) to the same assump‐
tion and proves them in the same cases (in [De15] the index is the dimension 2s). The Main Theorem re‐
moves the assumption. The proof is in §13, where (ii) is proved directly.

1.5 The algebraic statements
Let F be a field and q ≥ 3 odd. Put CF := F[y1, …, y2k+1]/(y1q−1, …, y2k+1q−1) and define D(a, b) and DJ as in the
abstract: D is the polynomial of §2.3, which has integer coefficients.

     THEOREM B. For every field F, every odd q ≥ 3 and every k ≥ 0,
                                           dimF (DJ : J ∈ 𝒥)·CF = Qk(q).
     Moreover, with CZ := Z[y1, …, y2k+1]/(y1q−1, …, y2k+1q−1), the group CZ/(DJ : J ∈ 𝒥)CZ is free abelian
     of rank (q−1)2k+1 − Qk(q), and the dimension of each graded piece of (DJ : J ∈ 𝒥)CF does not depend
     on F.

Theorem B is the root case of a more general statement about down-sets of partitions (Theorem 5.3 for ≥;
Theorems 5.9 and 5.9′ for = and for the integral statement). Neither the topology nor [DS] enters its proof;
they enter only in the translation of §2. With Theorem B and Proposition 2.5, the Main Theorem follows for
m = pv (Corollary 5.4).
The degrees that are not prime powers need a second count, in two sets of variables x1, …, xα and z1, …, zβ
with the box (xiq, zlq), for q = pv (p any prime; the box q is even when p = 2): the balanced bipartite ideal
Ibala, generated by Πi=1a(xi − zσ(i))q−1 over all σ ∈ Sa (α = β = a), and the phantom bipartite ideal Ipha, gener‐
ated by the same products over all bijections from a of the a + 1 variables xi onto the zl (α = a + 1, β = a).
Theorem C (§7) says that their dimensions are at least the numbers of pairs (ξ, η) of tuples in a set of q ele‐
ments, of lengths (a, a) and (a+1, a), such that the multiset of η is equal to, respectively contained in, the
multiset of ξ. Over F̄ p these are equalities (Corollaries 7.8 and 9.12), and the dimensions do depend on the
characteristic.
The even degrees need a third count, at an odd box. Let r ≥ 3 be odd, Dr(a, b) := Σu=0r−1 (−1)u au br−1−u = (ar
+ br)/(a + b), and for J ∈ 𝒥 let Dr,J be the product of the Dr(ya, yb) over the pairs {a, b} of J that avoid 0.

     THEOREM O (the odd box). For every field F, every odd r ≥ 3 and every k ≥ 0,
                 dimF (Dr,J : J ∈ 𝒥)·F[y1, …, y2k+1]/(y1r, …, y2k+1r) = N!·[xN] cosh(x)·I0(2x)(r−1)/2.
     Moreover the quotient of Z[y1, …, y2k+1]/(yir) by the ideal generated by the Dr,J is a free abelian group.

The right-hand side is the number of N-tuples, in a set of r elements with an involution that has one fixed
point, which split into pairs of opposite entries; for r = m − 1 it is Qk(m) for the even number m. Theorem O
is the root case of Theorem 8.11, a statement about interlaced pairs of down-sets of partitions. In characteris‐
tic p and for r = pv, Dr(a, b) = (a + b)r−1. Theorem O was stated as an open problem in version 10 of this pa‐
per (there: problem 7 of §13), and proved in version 11.

1.6 A different count: Theorem A
Write E := (e1, e3, …, e2k+1) ⊆ Fp[x0, …, x2k+1] for the ideal of the odd elementary symmetric polynomials.
Over an algebraic closure its zero set is the union of the linear spaces xa + xb = 0 ({a,b} ∈ J), over all J ∈ 𝒥.

      THEOREM A. Let F be a field of characteristic ≠ 2, let q ≥ 1 be odd and let n ≥ 0. Then dimF F[x1,
      …, xn]/(e1, e3, e5, …; x1q, …, xnq) = n!·[yn] ey I0(2y)(q−1)/2.

With F = Fp, q = pv and n = N, Theorem A says that Ak(q) := dim Fp[x0, …, x2k+1]/(E + (xiq)) equals the num‐
ber Pk(q) of Fq-points of the union of the matching spaces (Corollary 10.1). This is not the Main Theorem,
and it does not imply it. Theorem A is a sum statement at the odd box q on the grid with 0; the Main
Theorem for m = q is equivalent to an intersection statement at the even box q − 1 on the grid without 0
(Proposition 2.6), and §11 shows that the two are independent for families of matchings. The numbers differ
as well: P2(3) = 141 against Q2(3) = 20. As a count, Theorem A is not new: in characteristic 0 it is a conse‐
quence of a classical theorem of Kostant [Kos], and in odd characteristic of a proposition of Bezrukavnikov,
Riche and Rider [BRR] on modules with a good filtration, through the dictionary of Remark 10.4. The proof
of Appendix A is elementary and independent of both.

1.7 The idea of the proofs
Write the ideal of Theorem B as V ⊆ C2k+1 and the point set it should count as Z ⊆ T2k+1, T = Fq×. Peel off the
variable y1: the ideal splits into q − 1 slices W0 ⊆ ⋯ ⊆ Wq−2, ideals in the remaining variables, and the point
set splits into q − 1 layers according to how many values of the first coordinate complete a given tail. If every
layer is the point set of an ideal contained in the matching slice, induction on the number of variables gives
dim V ≥ |Z|. To make this work we enlarge the family: to every down-set Λ of partitions under weak domi‐
nance we attach an ideal VΛ, generated by products of divided differences D and Vandermonde determinants
Δ, and a point set ZΛ, the tuples whose «unpaired part» has shape in Λ. Three facts then carry the induction.
The number of completions of a tail depends only on the shape μ of the tail (P1). The q − 1 possible new
shapes form a chain for weak dominance, so the completions in Λ are an initial segment of the chain, and
the layers are again down-sets (P2). And each generator of a layer can be lifted to the right slice by one of
three explicit constructions, with no room to spare (P3). The full family of matchings enters only once, at the
root: the down-set generated by the partition (1) has as ideal exactly the ideal of all the DJ.
For m = q·r with q = pv, p ∤ r and r > 1, tm − 1 = Πζ ∈ μr(t − ζ)q over F̄ p, so the group algebra is a product of lo‐
cal rings, one for each colouring c of the coordinates by r-th roots of unity. On the factor of c, the generator ψJ
vanishes unless every pair of J has colours ζ, ζ−1; the surviving generators are pure tensors along the colour
classes, and the ideal is a tensor product of one ideal per class of colour 1 and one per pair of inverse colours.
The class of colour 1 is an instance of Theorem 5.3. A pair of inverse colours becomes, after the change of
variables x = ζ−1t − 1, z = (ζt)−1 − 1, a bipartite ideal, and the colour of the index 0, which is determined by
the others, decides whether it is balanced or phantom. The count of the points factors over the colourings in
the same way. For the bipartite ideals, the tail of a point now has two residues, one for each set of variables,
and its shape is a pair of partitions: adding a value to the x-side removes a box from the z-residue, adds a box
to the x-residue, or starts a new part, and these q options again form a chain, now in the product of two weak
dominance orders. The proof of Theorem B then goes through with the box q in place of q − 1 and the bino‐
mial coefficients C(q − 1, t) ≡ (−1)t in place of the signs of D.
For even m the same reduction applies at each prime, but two blocks are new. At an odd prime the colour −1
is its own inverse, and after the substitution u = −t its block is the ideal generated by the products of the (ya
+ yb)q−1 in the box (yiq). At the prime 2 the block of the colour 1 is the problem for the degree 2v itself; there
we replace each generator by its form of lowest degree in the variables s = t + 1, which is s1 ⋯ s2k+1 times a
product of the polynomials Σw aw bq−2−w, and the ideal of the leading forms of an ideal has the same dimen‐
sion as the ideal. Both are instances of one statement, Theorem O: the count of Theorem B on a grid T with
an odd number r of values, one of which, 0, is its own opposite. A tuple may then have one unpaired zero, so
the shape of a tuple is a partition together with a mark δ ∈ {0, 1}, and the new coordinate has one more op‐
tion, the zero, which changes the mark. The r options still form a chain, provided the set of shapes Λ is an in‐
terlaced pair: a down-set of unmarked shapes and a down-set of marked shapes such that removing a box
and switching the mark stays in Λ. The layers of an interlaced pair are interlaced pairs. On the side of the
ideals the unmarked shapes carry the generators of §5, with the symmetric polynomial Dr in the place of the
divided difference, and a marked shape carries, in the place of its first Vandermonde determinant, the
Pfaffian of the antisymmetric polynomials Dr−1(yi, yj) bordered by a Vandermonde matrix. Of the seven lift‐
ings of generators to slices, six are explicit, by expansions of Pfaffians; the seventh needs the membership of
the bordered Pfaffian in the ideal generated by the products of a Vandermonde determinant and of polyno‐
mials Dr (Lemma 8.7). It follows from one fact: the generating function Σσ ζσ ω2σ+1(a, b) of the antisymmetric
polynomials that interpolate between Dr−1 and the multiples of Dr is Ev(a)·Od(b) − Ev(b)·Od(a), a matrix of
rank two. A rank-two update of a bordered Pfaffian (Lemma 8.8) then expresses the Pfaffian of Dr−1 through
bordered Pfaffians whose entries are multiples of Dr, and a Laplace expansion finishes the proof.

1.8 A note on the history
For several months the project that produced this paper took the count Ak(q) = Pk(q) of Theorem A to be the
algebraic form of Conjecture 1.2. Two earlier notes of the author, on the cases k = 2 (The Sofa Theorem
[Sofa]) and k = 3 (The Hammock Theorem [Ham]), were written under that identification and stated that
they established Conjecture 1.2 in those dimensions. On 23 September 2026, re-deriving the reduction from
the original [DS], we found that the identification had never been proved, and that it is false for families of
matchings (§11). The conclusions of those notes about Conjecture 1.2 in dimensions 4 and 6 are nevertheless
true: for odd m they are the cases k = 2, 3 of the Main Theorem, proved here by a different route. Versions 4
and 5 of this paper proved the Main Theorem for odd prime powers and stated the colour reduction for the
degrees that are not prime powers as an open problem; the reduction and the bipartite count (§6–§7), added
in version 6, complete it. Version 8 added the machine-checked proof of Main Theorem′ (§14.8), recorded
that the repository [Rep] is public, stated the status of the Double Ladder theorem more precisely (Remark
12.2), and added one clause to the proof of Lemma 6.8; no statement and no proof of version 7 changed.
Version 9 corrected the attribution of Theorem A: in characteristic 0 it follows from a theorem of Kostant,
which the earlier versions did not cite (Remark 10.4). It also recorded that the Watermark and Double Ladder
theorems are now proved for every prime and verified in Lean [WDL] (Remark 12.2). Again no statement
and no proof changed. Version 10 corrects the attribution of Theorem A a second time: version 9 said that we
knew no version of Kostant's theorem in positive characteristic that gives the count, and there is one, [BRR,
Proposition 2.12], which we had not read (Remark 10.4(3)). It also adds the reading of Theorem B in the
same language (Proposition 10.5, Corollary 10.6, Remark 10.7) and the equality for the ideals of Appendix A
(Corollary A.17), restates problem 6 of §15 and adds problem 7. A supplementary note [Note], deposited with
this version, presents the dictionary on its own, with a statistic on walks that gives the Hilbert series of the
ring of Theorem A. No statement and no proof of the earlier versions changes. Version 11 proved the conjec‐
ture for the even degrees, which version 10 listed as its first open problem, and with it the odd box, which
was its seventh: §8 (Theorem O) and §9 (Theorem 9.11) are new. Sections 8 to 14 of version 10 are now sec‐
tions 10 to 16, with the same internal numbering (Proposition 8.5 of version 10 is Proposition 10.5 here, §12.8
is §14.8, and so on). §7 is now stated for every integer q ≥ 2, because §9 uses it for q = 2v; its proofs are un‐
changed. The Main Theorem, Main Theorem′ and Corollaries H and W are now stated for every m ≥ 3; for
odd m they are the statements of version 10. New in §10 and §12: Corollary 10.8 and (H4′). The even degrees
were found on 2 October 2026 by replacing the generators at m = 2v by their leading forms, which had been
measured to generate an ideal of the right dimension in seven cells, and then proving the count for that ideal.
Version 11 was not made public; everything in it is in the present version. Version 12 (the present one)
changes no statement of version 11. Main Theorem′ and Theorem O are now proved in Lean 4 for every de‐
gree (§14.8). Two proofs are printed in the form that was formalized, which is shorter: Lemma 8.7 by a rank-
two update of bordered Pfaffians, in place of the exterior algebra with divided powers of version 11 (§8.5),
which also gives the membership for other border sets (Lemma 8.7′); and the upper bound of Corollary 8.13
by a pairing of the box ring, so that §8 no longer uses §10. The signs of §8.3 are given explicitly, and its prop‐
erties are attributed to the classical sources. The version also adds the summary of §1.0 and the literature of
§1.10; incorporates the notes of the cold reading of the printed text of version 11 (§14.6); corrects the place of
Mizukami's theorem (§1.3); and writes the condition of the title in the form (n+1)! of [AMV], which is the
same condition as the (n+2)! of version 11.

1.9 Notation
q is odd in §2–§6 and §10–§11. In §2, §6 and §11, p is an odd prime and q = pv. §5 is stated for every odd q
over an arbitrary field. §7 is stated for every integer q ≥ 2 over a field in which the binomial coefficients C(q
− 1, t) are non-zero, and is applied with q = pv for every prime p, 2 included. In §8, r is odd and the box is r.
