At both primes the sum is Q1(15), as Lemma 6.8 says; the literal computation of §14.3 gives the same 546. (At
k = 1 the Main Theorem is Degtyarev's theorem [De14]; the example only shows the bookkeeping.)

7. The bipartite count
This section proves the hypothesis of Proposition 6.9, and so completes the proof of the Main Theorem for
odd m. The method is that of §5, with two changes: the box is q instead of q − 1, and the single partition of §5
is replaced by a pair of partitions, one for each of the two sets of variables. Throughout, q ≥ 2 is an integer,
odd or even, and F is a field in which the binomial coefficients C(q − 1, t), 0 ≤ t ≤ q − 1, are non-zero: for in‐
stance char F = 0, or char F = p and q = pv for a prime p, the prime 2 included (then C(q−1, t) ≡ (−1)t (mod
p), because (1 + u)q−1 = (1 + uq)(1 + u)−1 in Fp[[u]]). The parity of q plays no role in this section: q enters
only as the size of the box and of the set Ω, and the field only through the binomial coefficients, in case (γ) of
Proposition 7.5. (Versions 6 to 10 of this paper stated the section for odd q, which is what §6 needs; §9 needs
it for q = 2v in characteristic 2. The proofs are unchanged.) Let Ω be a set of q elements. For finite index sets
A and B put α := |A|, β := |B| and
                                           RA,B := F[xi (i ∈ A), zl (l ∈ B)]/(xiq, zlq).
(The symbols A, B, RA,B are local to this section.) Points are pairs (ξ, η) ∈ ΩA × ΩB.

      THEOREM C (the bipartite count). For every a ≥ 0:
      dimF Ibala ≥ Nbal(a, q) and dimF Ipha ≥ Nph(a, q) = Nbal(a + 1, q),
      where Ibala ⊆ R[a],[a] and Ipha ⊆ R[a+1],[a] are the ideals of Lemma 6.7(iii) and Nbal, Nph are the counts
      of §6.6.

Theorem C is the root case of Theorem 7.6 below. With Proposition 6.9 it gives the Main Theorem for m not a
prime power (Corollary 7.7). Over F̄ p both inequalities are equalities (Corollary 7.8); in characteristic 0 they
are strict in general (Remarks 7.9).

7.1 Peeling with the box q
Write RA,B = RA ∖ {i1}, B[w]/(wq) with w := xi1 for some i1 ∈ A. For an ideal V ⊆ RA,B and 0 ≤ j ≤ q − 1 put V≤j :=
{f ∈ V : degw f ≤ j} and Wj(V) := {[wj]f : f ∈ V≤j} ⊆ RA ∖ {i1}, B.
Lemma 7.1 (peeling). (i) Wj(V) is an ideal. (ii) Wj(V) ⊆ Wj+1(V) for 0 ≤ j < q − 1. (iii) dim V = Σj=0q−1 dim
Wj(V). (iv) For Z ⊆ ΩA × ΩB and a tail M' (the coordinates other than i1) let F(M') := {u ∈ Ω : (u, M') ∈ Z} and
Z>i := {M' : |F(M')| > i}; then |Z| = Σi=0q−1 |Z>i|.
Proof. As for Lemma 5.1, with q in place of q − 1: in (ii) one uses wj+1 ≠ 0, i.e. j + 1 ≤ q − 1, and in (iv) |F(M')|
≤ q. ∎

7.2 Pairs of partitions and the bipartite down-set family
We use the partial sums St and the weak dominance order ≼ of §5.2 (defined also between partitions of dif‐
ferent sizes), and the rows r̄j, r̲ j of §5.5.
       Residue shape. For (ξ, η), let X and Y be the multisets of values of ξ and η. Let R+ keep, for each value
       u with multX(u) > multY(u), the difference multX(u) − multY(u) of copies of u, and let R− be defined
       symmetrically. The shape of (ξ, η) is λ(ξ, η) := (λ+, λ−), the multiplicity partitions of R+ and R−. Their
       supports are disjoint subsets of Ω, so ℓ(λ+) + ℓ(λ−) ≤ q; moreover |λ+| − |λ−| = α − β and |λ+| + |λ−| ≤
       α + β.
       BParq(α, β) is the set of pairs (λ+, λ−) of partitions with |λ+| − |λ−| = α − β, |λ+| + |λ−| ≤ α + β and
       ℓ(λ+) + ℓ(λ−) ≤ q. It is ordered by the product order: (λ+, λ−) ≼ (μ+, μ−) iff λ+ ≼ μ+ and λ− ≼ μ−. Every
       element of BParq(α, β) is the shape of some point (give the ℓ(λ+) + ℓ(λ−) ≤ q rows pairwise distinct val‐
       ues and the pairs arbitrary values); this is not used below.
       For a ≼-down-set Λ ⊆ BParq(α, β) put ZΛ := {(ξ, η) ∈ ΩA × ΩB : λ(ξ, η) ∈ Λ}.
       Tight patterns. A tight pattern of λ = (λ+, λ−) ∈ BParq(α, β) on (A, B) consists of a set P of α − |λ+| = β
       − |λ−| pairwise disjoint pairs (i, l) ∈ A × B, of blocks B+1, …, B+λ+,1 partitioning A ∖ A(P) with |B+c| =
       (λ+)'c, and of blocks B−1, …, B−λ−,1 partitioning B ∖ B(P) with |B−c| = (λ−)'c. Its product is
                                      Π(i,l) ∈ P(xi − zl)q−1 · Πc Δ(xB+c) · Πc Δ(zB−c),
with Δ the Vandermonde determinant of §5.2.
       VΛ ⊆ RA,B is the ideal generated by the products of all tight patterns on (A, B) of all elements of Λ. For
       α = β = 0 the empty product is 1.
Lemma 7.2 (the roots). (i) {(∅, ∅)} is a down-set of BParq(a, a), V{(∅,∅)} = Ibala, and |Z{(∅,∅)}| = Nbal(a, q). (ii)
{((1), ∅)} is a down-set of BParq(a+1, a), V{((1),∅)} = Ipha, and |Z{((1),∅)}| = Nph(a, q). (iii) Nph(a, q) = Nbal(a + 1,
q).
Proof. (i) λ± ≼ ∅ forces λ± = ∅. A tight pattern of (∅, ∅) is a perfect matching of A with B, i.e. a bijection σ,
with product Πi (xi − zσ(i))q−1; and the shape is (∅, ∅) iff X = Y. (ii) In BParq(a+1, a), |λ+| = |λ−| + 1; λ− ≼ ∅
forces λ− = ∅ and then λ+ ≼ (1) with |λ+| = 1 forces λ+ = (1). A tight pattern has a pairs and one x-block of
size 1 (with Δ = 1), so its product is a generator of Ipha, and every generator arises. The shape is ((1), ∅) iff Y
⊆ X. (iii) Given (ξ, η) ∈ Ωa+1 × Ωa with Y ⊆ X, append to η the one element of X − Y; given a balanced point
in Ωa+1 × Ωa+1, delete the last coordinate of η. These maps are mutually inverse. ∎
For instance, Nbal(a, 3) = 3, 15, 93, 639, 4653 and Nbal(2, q) = 15, 45, 91, 153, 231 for a = 1, …, 5 and q = 3, 5, 7,
9, 11.
Lemma 7.2′ (§5.5 and §5.6 for arbitrary partitions). Let λ ≼ λ̃ be partitions of arbitrary sizes and lengths.
Then the identities of §5.5, the chain of Lemma 5.5 for the removals and the additions, (T) of §5.6, the inequality
(5.2) of §5.6 (if ℓ(λ) < t ≤ ℓ(λ̃), then St(λ̃) ≥ |λ| + (t − ℓ(λ))) and the comparisons (a), (b), (c), (e2) of §5.6, re‐
called in the proof of Proposition 7.4, hold for λ and λ̃.
Proof. The identities of §5.5 and the chain are statements about one partition. The proofs of (T), (5.2), (a), (b),
(c) and (e2) in §5.6 compare the partial sums St of the two partitions term by term and use only λ ≼ λ̃. In §5.6
the parity of |μ| is used only in case (d1), and the cap ℓ ≤ h only in the layout of the positions and in case
(e1); none of these is borrowed here. ∎
7.3 (P1) Fibres and (Chain)
Fix a down-set Λ ⊆ BParq(α, β) with α ≥ 1, and peel w = xi1. Let M' be a tail with shape μ = (μ+, μ−) ∈ BParq(α
− 1, β), residual values u1, …, u ℓ + of R+ (uj with multiplicity μ+,j) and v1, …, v ℓ − of R−. Adding the value u at
the index i1 changes the shape as follows:
       u = vj: (μ+, μ− − ej) (one box removed from row j of μ−, re-sorted);
       u = uj: (μ+ + ej, μ−);
       u any of the q − ℓ+ − ℓ− other values (the values whose multiplicities in the two coordinate sets of the
       tail agree, possibly 0 = 0): (μ+ ⊔ 1, μ−).
These are q = |Ω| values, and every resulting shape lies in BParq(α, β): one value is added to the x-side, and a
middle value exists only when ℓ+ + ℓ− < q, which gives ℓ(μ+ ⊔ 1) + ℓ(μ−) ≤ q. List them by position p = 1, …,
q: optp(μ) := (μ+, μ− − ep) for p ≤ ℓ −; (μ+ ⊔ 1, μ−) for ℓ − < p ≤ q − ℓ + (the middle positions); (μ+ + eq+1−p,
μ−) for p > q − ℓ+. Hence |F(M')| = FΛ(μ) := #{p : optp(μ) ∈ Λ}, and Z>i = ZΛi with Λi := {μ ∈ BParq(α − 1, β) :
FΛ(μ) > i}.
Lemma 7.3 (chain). opt1(μ) ≼ opt2(μ) ≼ ⋯ ≼ optq(μ). Hence the options lying in Λ form an initial segment, of
length FΛ(μ), and
                             FΛ(μ) = r + (q − ℓ+ − ℓ−)·ι + (ℓ+ − j0 + 1)·[j0 exists], (7.1)
where r = 0 or r is the last row of μ− of its length (the removals in Λ are those at positions ≤ r), ι := [(μ+ ⊔ 1, μ−)
∈ Λ] (ι := 0 if ℓ+ + ℓ− = q), and j0, if it exists, is the first row of μ+ of its length such that (μ+ + ej, μ−) ∈ Λ exact‐
ly for j0 ≤ j ≤ ℓ+. If j0 exists, then r = ℓ−, and either ι = 1 or ℓ+ + ℓ− = q; if ι = 1, then r = ℓ−.
Proof. By the identities of §5.5 (Lemma 7.2′), μ− − e1 ≼ ⋯ ≼ μ− − eℓ− ≼ μ− and μ+ ≼ μ+ ⊔ 1 ≼ μ+ + eℓ+ ≼ ⋯ ≼
μ+ + e1. Along the removals the first component is μ+, and along the middle and the additions the second
component is μ−; so in the product order the list is weakly increasing, including the step from the last re‐
moval to the first middle or addition option. Since Λ is a down-set, the options in Λ form an initial segment.
Rows of equal length give equal options, which gives the description of r and j0. ∎
In particular, with Φ := FΛ(μ): Φ = q − j0 + 1 if j0 exists, Φ = q − ℓ + if j0 does not exist and ι = 1, and Φ = r
otherwise.

7.4 (P2) The layers are down-sets
Proposition 7.4 (inequality of pairs of partitions). If μ, μ̃ ∈ BParq(α − 1, β) and μ ≼ μ̃, then optp(μ) ≼
optp(μ̃) for every p. Hence FΛ(μ) ≥ FΛ(μ̃) and every Λi is a down-set.
Proof. The second sentence follows from the first as in Proposition 5.6. Write ℓ± := ℓ(μ±), ℓ̃ ± := ℓ(μ̃ ±). Since |
μ+| − |μ−| = |μ̃ +| − |μ̃ −|, any strict inequality |μ̃ −| > |μ−| gives |μ̃ +| > |μ+|. We use (T) of §5.6 and the inequali‐
ty (5.2) (Lemma 7.2′): if λ ≼ λ̃ and ℓ(λ) < t ≤ ℓ(λ̃), then St(λ̃) ≥ |λ| + (t − ℓ(λ)). We also use four comparisons
of §5.6, valid for arbitrary partitions by Lemma 7.2′: (a) λ − ep ≼ λ̃ − ep for p ≤ min(ℓ(λ), ℓ(λ̃)); (b) λ + ej ≼ λ̃
+ ej for j ≤ min(ℓ(λ), ℓ(λ̃)); (c) λ ⊔ 1 ≼ λ̃ ⊔ 1; (e2) λ + ej ≼ λ̃ ⊔ 1 for ℓ(λ̃) < j ≤ ℓ(λ). Each position p is a re‐
moval (R), a middle position (M) or an addition (A) for μ, and likewise for μ̃, which gives nine cases; put j :=
q + 1 − p when p is an addition.
     case             the position p is             option of μ        option of μ̃                       used
      RR             a removal for both             (μ+, μ− − ep)     (μ̃ +, μ̃ − − ep)               μ+ ≼ μ̃ +; (a)
      AA            an addition for both            (μ+ + ej, μ−)      (μ̃ + + ej, μ̃ −)              (b); μ− ≼ μ̃ −
     MM                middle for both              (μ+ ⊔ 1, μ−)       (μ̃ + ⊔ 1, μ̃ −)               (c); μ− ≼ μ̃ −
   RM, RA              a removal for μ              (μ+, μ− − ep)   middle or addition         the chain through μ ≼ μ̃
     AM        an addition for μ, middle for μ̃     (μ+ + ej, μ−)      (μ̃ + ⊔ 1, μ̃ −)              (e2); μ− ≼ μ̃ −
     MA        middle for μ, an addition for μ̃     (μ+ ⊔ 1, μ−)       (μ̃ + + ej, μ̃ −)   dominance, (5.2), sizes; μ− ≼ μ̃ −
     MR         middle for μ, a removal for μ̃      (μ+ ⊔ 1, μ−)      (μ̃ +, μ̃ − − ep)      (5.2) and the transfer of sizes
      AR      an addition for μ, a removal for μ̃   (μ+ + ej, μ−)     (μ̃ +, μ̃ − − ep)    (T), (5.2) and the transfer of sizes

The «transfer of sizes» is the one genuinely bipartite step: since |μ+| − |μ−| = |μ̃ +| − |μ̃ −|, a strict inequality on
the z-side gives one on the x-side.
      RR (p ≤ min(ℓ−, ℓ̃ −)): first components μ+ ≼ μ̃ +; second components by (a).
      AA (j ≤ min(ℓ+, ℓ̃ +)): (b), and μ− ≼ μ̃ −.
      MM: (c), and μ− ≼ μ̃ −.
      RM: (μ+, μ− − ep) ≼ μ ≼ μ̃ ≼ (μ̃ + ⊔ 1, μ̃ −).
      RA: (μ+, μ− − ep) ≼ μ ≼ μ̃ ≼ (μ̃ + + ej, μ̃ −).
      AM (j ≤ ℓ+ and ℓ̃ + < j): (e2), and μ− ≼ μ̃ −.
      MA (p middle for μ, so j > ℓ+; addition for μ̃, so j ≤ ℓ̃ +): we need μ+ ⊔ 1 ≼ μ̃ + + ej. For t ≤ ℓ+, St(μ+ ⊔ 1)
      = St(μ+) ≤ St(μ̃ +) ≤ St(μ̃ + + ej). For ℓ+ < t ≤ ℓ̃ +, St(μ̃ +) ≥ |μ+| + 1 = St(μ+ ⊔ 1) by (5.2). For t > ℓ̃ + ≥ j ≥
      r̲ j(μ̃ +), St(μ̃ + + ej) = |μ̃ +| + 1 ≥ |μ+| + 1. The second components are μ− ≼ μ̃ −.
      MR (p middle for μ, so p > ℓ−; removal for μ̃, so p ≤ ℓ̃ −): by (5.2) at t = ℓ̃ −, |μ̃ −| ≥ |μ−| + 1, hence |μ̃ +| ≥
      |μ+| + 1. First components: μ+ ⊔ 1 ≼ μ̃ +: for t ≤ ℓ+ this is dominance; for t > ℓ+ we need St(μ̃ +) ≥ |μ+| +
      1, which holds by (5.2) if t < ℓ̃ + and by |μ̃ +| ≥ |μ+| + 1 if t ≥ ℓ̃ +. Second components: μ− ≼ μ̃ − − ep: for t
      < r̄p(μ̃ −) this is dominance; for t ≥ r̄p(μ̃ −) ≥ p > ℓ−, St(μ−) = |μ−|, while the rows ℓ− + 1, …, p of μ̃ − are
      non-empty, so St(μ̃ −) ≥ Sℓ−(μ̃ −) + 1 ≥ |μ−| + 1.
      AR (p addition for μ, so j ≤ ℓ+; removal for μ̃, so p ≤ ℓ̃ −): since ℓ+ + ℓ− ≤ q and ℓ̃ + + ℓ̃ − ≤ q, we have
      ℓ− ≤ q − ℓ+ < p and ℓ̃ + ≤ q − p = j − 1. Second components: exactly as in MR, μ− ≼ μ̃ − − ep and |μ̃ −| ≥ |
      μ−| + 1, so |μ̃ +| ≥ |μ+| + 1. First components: we need St(μ+) + [t ≥ r̲ j(μ+)] ≤ St(μ̃ +). This is dominance
      for t < r̲ j(μ+), and it follows from |μ̃ +| ≥ |μ+| + 1 for t ≥ ℓ̃ +. It remains to show St(μ+) < St(μ̃ +) for r̲ j(μ+)
      ≤ t < ℓ̃ +. Suppose t is tight. The rows r̲ j(μ+), …, j of μ+ have the same length c := μ+,j ≥ 1, and t < t + 1
      ≤ ℓ̃ + ≤ j − 1, so μ+,t = μ+,t+1 = c. By (T), c = μ+,t ≥ μ̃ +,t ≥ μ̃ +,t+1 ≥ μ+,t+1 = c, so every row of μ̃ + after the
      t-th has length ≤ c. As μ̃ + has only ℓ̃ + ≤ j − 1 rows, Sj(μ̃ +) ≤ St(μ+) + (j − 1 − t)c < St(μ+) + (j − t)c =
      Sj(μ+), contradicting μ+ ≼ μ̃ +.
For each of μ and μ̃ the three types partition {1, …, q}, so the nine cases cover every position. ∎

7.5 (P3) Every tight pattern of a layer lies in the right slice
Proposition 7.5. For every i ∈ {0, …, q − 1}, VΛi ⊆ Wq−1−i(VΛ), where VΛi ⊆ RA ∖ {i1}, B.
Proof. It suffices to treat a generator G: the product of a tight pattern (P, (B+c), (B−c)) of some μ ∈ Λi, so that Φ
:= FΛ(μ) ≥ i + 1. By Lemma 7.1(ii) it is enough to find f ∈ VΛ with degw f ≤ q − Φ and [wq−Φ]f = ±κ·G for a
non-zero constant κ; then G ∈ Wq−Φ(VΛ) ⊆ Wq−1−i(VΛ). By (7.1) exactly one of the following holds.
      (α) j0 exists and λ := (μ+ + ej0, μ−) ∈ Λ, Φ = q − j0 + 1. The column c* := μ+,j0 + 1 of μ+ has height j0 −
      1, because j0 is the first row of its length; let S := B+c* (if j0 = 1, c* is a new column and S = ∅). Putting
      i1 into S gives a tight pattern of λ: column c* of λ+ has height j0, and the number of pairs α − |λ+| = (α
      − 1) − |μ+| is unchanged. Its product lies in VΛ; by the factorisation Δ(xS ∪ i1) = ±Πs ∈ S(w − xs)·Δ(xS) it
      has w-degree |S| = j0 − 1 = q − Φ ≤ q − 1 (so w|S| ≠ 0 in the box) and top coefficient ±G.
      (β) j0 does not exist, ι = 1, λ := (μ+ ⊔ 1, μ−) ∈ Λ, Φ = q − ℓ+. Put i1 into B+1, of height ℓ+ (empty if μ+ =
      ∅): this is a tight pattern of λ, whose product has w-degree ℓ + = q − Φ ≤ q − 1 (a middle value exists,
      so ℓ+ < q) and top coefficient ±G.
      (γ) j0 does not exist, ι = 0, and Φ = r ≥ 1, λ := (μ+, μ− − er) ∈ Λ. The column c0 := μ−,r of μ− has height
      r, because r is the last row of its length; let S := B−c0 and write G = Δ(zS)·G'. For b ∈ S, the pairs P ∪ {(i1,
      b)}, the blocks B+c, the blocks B−c (c ≠ c0) and S ∖ {b} form a tight pattern of λ: column c0 of λ− has
      height r − 1, and the number of pairs grows by one, as α − |λ+| = ((α − 1) − |μ+|) + 1. So, with the
      signs εb of Lemma 5.7(ii),
                                     f := Σb ∈ S εb Δ(zS∖b)·(w − zb)q−1·G' ∈ VΛ.
Expanding, [wq−1−t] f = C(q−1, t)(−1)t·(Σb∈S εb Δ(zS∖b) zbt)·G', which is 0 for t ≤ r − 2 and ±C(q−1, r−1)·G for
t = r − 1, by Lemma 5.7(ii) (an identity over Z, valid in RA ∖ {i1}, B). So degw f ≤ q − r = q − Φ, and the top coef‐
ficient is ±C(q−1, r−1)·G with C(q−1, r−1) ≠ 0 in F (here r ≤ ℓ− ≤ q). ∎
The three constructions land exactly in the slice q − Φ, and in general the slice cannot be lowered: the state‐
ment with Wq−2−i in place of Wq−1−i fails at the cells of §14.4, for some generators G.

7.6 The induction
Theorem 7.6 (bipartite down-set theorem). For all α, β ≥ 0 and every ≼-down-set Λ ⊆ BParq(α, β), dimF
VΛ ≥ |ZΛ|.
Proof. Induction on α + β. Base: BParq(0, 0) = {(∅, ∅)}; for Λ = ∅ both sides are 0, and for Λ = {(∅, ∅)}, VΛ =
F and ZΛ is one point. Symmetry: the isomorphism RA,B ≅ RB,A that exchanges the names x and z carries
tight-pattern products of (λ+, λ−) on (A, B) to tight-pattern products of (λ−, λ+) on (B, A) (as (x − z)q−1 =
(−1)q−1(z − x)q−1, and a sign does not change the ideal), and (ξ, η) ↦ (η, ξ) exchanges λ+ and λ−. A relabelling
of A or B changes each product by a sign and preserves |ZΛ|. So we may assume α ≥ 1 and peel any x-variable.
Step: by Lemma 7.1(iii), Proposition 7.5, the induction hypothesis (each Λi is a down-set of BParq(α − 1, β)
by Proposition 7.4), §7.3 and Lemma 7.1(iv),
dim VΛ = Σi=0q−1 dim Wq−1−i(VΛ) ≥ Σi=0q−1 dim VΛi ≥ Σi=0q−1 |ZΛi| = Σi=0q−1 |Z>i| = |ZΛ|. ∎
Proof of Theorem C. Lemma 7.2 and Theorem 7.6. ∎

7.7 Consequences
Corollary 7.7 (Main Theorem′ and the Main Theorem, odd degrees). For odd m ≥ 3, Main Theorem′
holds, and Conjecture 1.2 of [DS] holds in every even dimension.
Proof. Let m be odd and k ≥ 1. For every prime p | m, dimFp Fp[G]/(ψ̄ J : J ∈ 𝒥) = mn+1 − |Γ𝒥|: for m = pv by
Corollary 5.4 and Proposition 2.5; otherwise by Proposition 6.9, whose hypothesis is Theorem C over F = F̄ p,
q = pv. For p ∤ m the same equality is Theorem 0(b) with Kp = F̄ p. The rank of R/(ψJ : J ∈ 𝒥) is mn+1 − |Γ𝒥|
(Theorem 0(b) with p = 0), and |Γ𝒥| = Qk(m) (Lemma 2.2, valid for odd m); so R/(ψJ : J ∈ 𝒥) has no torsion,
which is Main Theorem′. No step of this uses topology. With Theorem 0(a) (Proposition 2.1) it gives the Main
Theorem; for k = 0 the Main Theorem is trivial. ∎
Corollary 7.8 (equality over F̄p). Let q = pv with p odd. Then dimF̄p Ibala = Nbal(a, q) and dimF̄p Ipha = Nph(a,
q) for every a ≥ 0. (For p = 2 see Corollary 9.12.)
Proof. For a = 0, Ibal0 = F and Iph0 = F[x1]/(x1q) have dimensions 1 = Nbal(0, q) and q = Nph(0, q). Let a ≥ 1,
choose an odd prime r ≠ p and put m := qr and n := 2a, so that k = a ≥ 1 and (6.1) applies. Colour a coordi‐
nates by a primitive r-th root ζ, a coordinates by ζ−1, and one coordinate s by 1; then c0 = 1, the block 𝒞1 = {0,
s} contributes dim Ic,1 = q − 1 = N1(c), and the pair block contributes dim Ibala. If dim Ibala > Nbal(a, q), this
colouring has dim πc(I) > N1(c)·Nbal(a, q), while every other compatible colouring has dim πc(I) ≥ N1(c)·Πζ
Nζ(c) (Proposition 6.5, Lemmas 6.6 and 6.7, and Theorem C for every a ≤ n). Summing, dimF I > |Γ𝒥| by
Lemma 6.8, contradicting (6.1). For Ipha, take n := 2a, colour a + 1 coordinates by ζ and a coordinates by ζ−1;
then c0 = ζ−1, 𝒞1 = ∅, and the only block is Ipha. ∎
Remarks 7.9. (1) Corollary 7.8 is an algebraic statement with an algebraic proof: the upper bound (6.1) is
Theorem 0(b), a Chinese-remainder count. The proof is global, through the sum over the colourings of F[G];
we do not know a direct proof of ≤ on one block: the evaluation argument of Theorem 5.9 fails, because (x −
z)q−1 vanishes at x = z rather than off it. (2) In characteristic 0 the inequalities of Theorem C are strict in gen‐
eral: dimQ ((x − z)2) = 4 > 3 = Nbal(1, 3) in Q[x, z]/(x3, z3), and dimQ Ibal2 = 24 > 15 at q = 3 (§14). So, unlike
Theorem 5.9′, in these cells (where Corollary 7.8 gives dimF3 Ibala = Nbal(a, 3) < dimQ Ibala) the quotient of the
integral box ring by the bipartite ideal has 3-torsion: the bipartite ideals are genuinely of characteristic p. (3)
By Lemma 7.2(iii) the phantom and balanced counts are related. On the ideal side, if σ ∈ Sa+1 and i0 :=
σ−1(a+1), the generator Πi(xi − zσ(i))q−1 of Ibala+1 has za+1-degree q − 1, with top coefficient the generator of
Ipha indexed by (i0, σ|), so the top slice Wq−1(Ibala+1) (peeling za+1, that is, Lemma 7.1 with the names x and z
exchanged, as in the symmetry step of Theorem 7.6) contains Ipha. With Corollary 7.8, Wj(Ibala+1) = 0 for j < q
− 1, and f ↦ [za+1q−1] f is an isomorphism of graded vector spaces Ibala+1 → Ipha of degree −(q−1) over F̄ p.

8. The odd box: Theorem O
Theorem B is a count at the even box q − 1, on a grid without zero. The even degrees need the same count at
an odd box, on a grid with one zero. This is problem 7 of version 10 of this paper; this section proves it.
Throughout the section, r = 2h + 1 ≥ 3 is odd, F is an arbitrary field, and
                                         Cm := F[y1, …, ym]/(y1r, …, ymr)
(in this section the box is r; in §5 the ring Cm had the box q − 1). T is a set of r elements with an involution u
↦ −u that has exactly one fixed point, written 0; so T ∖ {0} consists of h classes {u, −u}. As in §5, the field car‐
ries the ideals and T carries the point sets, and the two are compared only through dimensions and cardinali‐
ties. Put
                     D(a, b) := Σu=0r−1 (−1)u au br−1−u, D−(a, b) := Σu=0r−2 (−1)u au br−2−u,
two polynomials with integer coefficients: D is the Dr of Proposition 10.5, and D− is the D of §2.3 for the odd
number q = r. For a matching J ∈ 𝒥, DJ := Π D(ya, yb), the product over the k pairs of J that avoid 0; and, as in
§10,
                                            Nr(n) := n!·[xn] ex·I0(2x)h,
the number of n-tuples in T in which, for every class {u, −u}, the values u and −u occur equally often (the ze‐
ros are free). For even n the number of zeros of such a tuple is even, and ex may be replaced by cosh x.

     THEOREM O (the odd box). For every field F, every odd r ≥ 3 and every k ≥ 0,
                                       dimF (DJ : J ∈ 𝒥)·C2k+1 = Nr(2k + 2).
      Moreover the quotient of Z[y1, …, y2k+1]/(yir) by the ideal generated by the DJ is a free abelian group,
      and the dimension of each graded piece of (DJ : J ∈ 𝒥)C2k+1 does not depend on F.

For k = 0 the ideal is the whole ring F[y1]/(y1r), of dimension r = Nr(2). The inequality ≥ is the root case of
Theorem 8.11 below, a statement about a family of ideals indexed by «interlaced pairs» of down-sets, proved
by peeling one variable as in §5. The inequality ≤ is elementary (Corollary 8.13). The plan is that of §5, with
two changes. First, a point may have one unpaired zero, so a shape is a partition together with a mark δ ∈ {0,
1}, and the down-sets of §5 become pairs of down-sets that must be interlaced. Second, the generators at‐
tached to a marked shape are not products of divided differences and Vandermonde determinants any more:
one of their factors is a bordered Pfaffian, and the place of Lemma 5.7(ii) is taken, in one of the seven cas‐
es, by a membership lemma (Lemma 8.7), which is proved by a rank-two update of bordered Pfaffians (§8.5).

8.1 Elementary identities and peeling
In Z[a, b]:
      (8.1) D(b, a) = D(a, b) and D−(b, a) = −D−(a, b) (substitute w = r − 1 − u, respectively w = r − 2 − u; r
      − 1 is even and r − 2 is odd).
      (8.2) D(a, b) = br−1 − a·D−(a, b). (Indeed −a·D−(a, b) = Σw=1r−1 (−1)w aw br−1−w.)
      (8.3) (a + b)·D(a, b) = ar + br and (a + b)·D−(a, b) = br−1 − ar−1 (telescoping).
      (8.4) For 0 ≤ u ≤ r − 1, the coefficient of ar−1−u in D(a, b) is (−1)u bu. For 0 ≤ u ≤ r − 2, the coefficient
      of ar−2−u in D−(a, b) is −(−1)u bu.
For a set P of pairwise disjoint pairs of indices, DP := Π(a,b) ∈ P D(ya, yb); by (8.1) it does not depend on the or‐
der inside the pairs. For a finite set S of indices, Δ(S) is the Vandermonde determinant of §5.2 (Δ(∅) = Δ({s})
= 1).
Peeling. Write Cm = Cm−1[y1]/(y1r), with Cm−1 in the variables y2, …, ym. For an ideal V ⊆ Cm and 0 ≤ j ≤ r −
1 put Wj(V) := {[y1j] f : f ∈ V, degy1 f ≤ j}. By Lemma 7.1 (with one set of variables, the box r and Ω = T): Wj(V)
is an ideal of Cm−1, Wj(V) ⊆ Wj+1(V), dim V = Σj=0r−1 dim Wj(V), and for Z ⊆ Tm, with Z>i the set of the tails
M' ∈ Tm−1 that have more than i completions in Z, |Z| = Σi=0r−1 |Z>i|.

8.2 Points: shapes, interlaced pairs, layers
Shapes. Let 𝒫n := Parn(h), the set of partitions λ with at most h parts, |λ| ≤ n and |λ| ≡ n (mod 2) (§5.2; 𝒫n =
∅ for n < 0). For M ∈ Tm: in each class with multiplicities a, ā keep the number |a − ā|; λ(M) is the partition
formed by the non-zero ones, and δ(M) ∈ {0, 1} is the parity of the number of entries of M equal to 0. The
shape of M is (λ(M), δ(M)). Since m is twice the number of disjoint pairs {u, −u}, {0, 0} of M plus |λ(M)| +
δ(M), the shape lies in
                                   Shm := {(λ, 0) : λ ∈ 𝒫m} ∪ {(λ, 1) : λ ∈ 𝒫m−1}.
We call δ the mark; a shape (λ, 1) is marked. ≼, λ − ej, λ + ej, λ ⊔ 1 and ℓ = ℓ(λ) are as in §5.2 and §5.4. A
row of a partition is a labelled part: lowering or raising two different rows commutes, so (λ − ei) − ej = (λ −
ej) − ei, and so on, as partitions.
Lemma 8.1 (options; this is (P1)). Let M' ∈ Tm−1 have shape (μ, δ), ℓ = ℓ(μ), with residual values u1, …, uℓ
(uj of multiplicity μj). The r values y ∈ T give the following shapes of (y, M'):
      y = −uj: (μ − ej, δ); y = uj: (μ + ej, δ) (j = 1, …, ℓ);
      y ≠ 0 in one of the h − ℓ classes absent from the residue: (μ ⊔ 1, δ) (2(h − ℓ) = r − 1 − 2ℓ values);
      y = 0: (μ, 1 − δ).
All of them lie in Shm.
Proof. For y ≠ 0 this is §5.4; a new zero changes the parity of the number of zeros and nothing else. The count
is 2ℓ + 2(h − ℓ) + 1 = r. For the last sentence: if δ = 0 then μ ∈ 𝒫m−1, so μ ± ej, μ ⊔ 1 ∈ 𝒫m (the last one exists
only if ℓ < h) and (μ, 1) ∈ Shm; if δ = 1 then μ ∈ 𝒫m−2, so μ ± ej, μ ⊔ 1 ∈ 𝒫m−1 and μ ∈ 𝒫m. ∎
For Λ ⊆ Shm put Λ0 := {λ : (λ, 0) ∈ Λ} ⊆ 𝒫m, Λ1 := {λ : (λ, 1) ∈ Λ} ⊆ 𝒫m−1 and ZΛ := {M ∈ Tm : the shape of M
lies in Λ}. For σ = (μ, δ) ∈ Shm−1 let FΛ(σ) be the number of y ∈ T such that the shape of (y, M') lies in Λ, for
any M' of shape σ; it is well defined by Lemma 8.1, and
                                        FΛ(μ, δ) = F*Λδ(μ) + [μ ∈ Λ1−δ], (8.5)
                     F*Λ'(μ) := #{j : μ − ej ∈ Λ'} + #{j : μ + ej ∈ Λ'} + (r − 1 − 2ℓ)·[μ ⊔ 1 ∈ Λ'].
Here F*Λ' is exactly the function FΛ' of §5.4 for the odd number q = r (there T has q − 1 = r − 1 elements and
h classes).
Definition 8.2. Λ ⊆ Shm is an interlaced pair if
      *(D1) Λ0 is a ≼-down-set of 𝒫m and Λ1 is a ≼-down-set of 𝒫m−1;*
      *(D2) if (ν, δ) ∈ Λ and 1 ≤ j ≤ ℓ(ν), then (ν − ej, 1 − δ) ∈ Λ.*
((ν − ej, 1 − δ) is a shape of level m: its size has the required parity.) In words: removing one box from a shape
of Λ and switching the mark stays in Λ. On points, (D2) says: replacing one unpaired non-zero value by 0
stays in ZΛ.
Lemma 8.3 (chain). Let Λ be an interlaced pair of level m and (μ, δ) ∈ Shm−1, ℓ = ℓ(μ). List the r options of
Lemma 8.1 as
           (μ − e1, δ), …, (μ − eℓ, δ); (μ, 1 − δ); (μ ⊔ 1, δ) [r − 1 − 2ℓ times]; (μ + eℓ, δ), …, (μ + e1, δ).
Then the options lying in Λ form an initial segment of this list, of length FΛ(μ, δ).
Proof. Among the removals, among the additions, and from the middle option to the additions, this is Lemma
5.5 applied to the down-set Λδ: μ − ej ≼ μ − ej+1, μ ⊔ 1 ≼ μ + eℓ and μ + ej+1 ≼ μ + ej. If (μ, 1 − δ) ∈ Λ, then by
(D2) every (μ − ej, δ) ∈ Λ. If (μ ⊔ 1, δ) ∈ Λ, then by (D2), removing the new row, (μ, 1 − δ) ∈ Λ. If (μ + ej, δ) ∈
Λ, then by (D2), removing the box just added, (μ, 1 − δ) ∈ Λ. ∎
As in §5.5, rows of equal length give equal options. So the removals in Λ are those of the rows 1, …, ρ, where
ρ = 0 or ρ is the last row of its length, and the additions in Λ are those of the rows j0, …, ℓ, where j0 is the first
row of its length.
Lemma 8.4 (layers; this is (P2)). Let Λ be an interlaced pair of level m ≥ 1, and for 0 ≤ i ≤ r − 1 let Λi := {σ
∈ Shm−1 : FΛ(σ) > i}. Then Λi is an interlaced pair of level m − 1, and (ZΛ)>i = ZΛi.
Proof. The last assertion is Lemma 8.1.
(D1). Let μ ≼ μ̃ in 𝒫m−1. By Proposition 5.6 (for q = r, the down-set Λ0 ⊆ Parm(h), tails in Parm−1(h)), F*Λ0(μ) ≥
F*Λ0(μ̃); and [μ ∈ Λ1] ≥ [μ̃ ∈ Λ1] because Λ1 is a down-set of 𝒫m−1. By (8.5), FΛ(μ, 0) ≥ FΛ(μ̃, 0). In the same
way, for μ ≼ μ̃ in 𝒫m−2: Proposition 5.6 for the down-set Λ1 ⊆ Parm−1(h), with tails in Parm−2(h), gives F*Λ1(μ) ≥
F*Λ1(μ̃), and [μ ∈ Λ0] ≥ [μ̃ ∈ Λ0] because 𝒫m−2 ⊆ 𝒫m and Λ0 is a down-set of 𝒫m; so FΛ(μ, 1) ≥ FΛ(μ̃, 1). Hence
both components of Λi are down-sets.
(D2). Let (ν, δ) ∈ Shm−1, 1 ≤ j ≤ ℓ := ℓ(ν), ν' := ν − ej, and write A := Λδ, A' := Λ1−δ. We must show FΛ(ν', 1 −
δ) ≥ FΛ(ν, δ), that is, by (8.5),
                                        F*A'(ν') + [ν' ∈ A] ≥ F*A(ν) + [ν ∈ A'].
By (D2) for Λ: if a partition κ lies in A, then κ minus a box in any of its rows lies in A'; and symmetrically with
A and A' exchanged.
Case νj ≥ 2. The rows of ν' are the rows of ν, with the same labels. If ν − ei ∈ A, lower the row j (it is still non-
empty, also when i = j): (ν − ei) − ej = ν' − ei ∈ A'. If ν + ei ∈ A, lower the row j: (ν + ei) − ej = ν' + ei ∈ A'. If ν
⊔ 1 ∈ A, lower the row j: ν' ⊔ 1 ∈ A'; and ℓ(ν') = ℓ, so the middle options of ν and ν' have the same multiplici‐
ty. So F*A'(ν') ≥ F*A(ν). Finally ν ∈ A' implies ν' = ν − ej ∈ A.
Case νj = 1. Then ν' has the rows of ν other than j, ℓ(ν') = ℓ − 1, and ν' ⊔ 1 = ν. For i ≠ j: ν − ei ∈ A implies ν'
− ei ∈ A', and ν + ei ∈ A implies ν' + ei ∈ A', as before. The removal of the row j of ν is ν', and [ν − ej ∈ A] = [ν'
∈ A]. What remains on the right is (r − 1 − 2ℓ)[ν ⊔ 1 ∈ A] + [ν + ej ∈ A] + [ν ∈ A'], and what remains on the
left is the middle term of ν', namely (r − 1 − 2(ℓ − 1))[ν' ⊔ 1 ∈ A'] = (r + 1 − 2ℓ)[ν ∈ A']. If ν ∈ A', the right-
hand remainder is at most (r − 1 − 2ℓ) + 1 + 1. If ν ∉ A', it is 0: ν ⊔ 1 ∈ A would give, removing the new row,
ν ∈ A'; and ν + ej ∈ A would give, removing the box just added, ν ∈ A'. ∎
Lemma 8.5 (the root; the only place where the full family enters). For m = 2k + 1, Λroot := {((1), 0), (∅,
1)} is an interlaced pair, and |ZΛroot| = Nr(2k + 2). For m = 2k + 2, {(∅, 0)} is an interlaced pair, and |Z{(∅,0)}| =
Nr(2k + 2).
Proof. {(1)} is a down-set of 𝒫2k+1 (Lemma 5.2), {∅} is a down-set of 𝒫2k and of 𝒫2k+2, and (1) − e1 = ∅. ZΛroot
is the set of the (2k+1)-tuples with exactly one unpaired value, zero or not. Appending the opposite of that
value is a bijection onto the set of the (2k+2)-tuples that split into pairs {u, −u} and {0, 0}: the inverse deletes
the last entry. The latter set is Z{(∅,0)} at level 2k + 2, and it has Nr(2k + 2) elements. ∎

8.3 Bordered Pfaffians
Let A = (axy) be an alternating matrix (axx = 0, ayx = −axy) over a commutative ring, indexed by a totally or‐
dered finite set X, and write px ∈ {0, 1, 2, …} for the position of x ∈ X, counted from 0. The Pfaffian of A is
Pf(A) := Σπ sgn(π)·Π{x<y} ∈ π axy, the sum over the perfect matchings π of X, where sgn(π) is the sign of the
permutation that lists the pairs of π one after the other, each pair in increasing order (it does not depend on
the order of the pairs, and it is (−1) to the number of crossings of π). So Pf(A) = 1 for X = ∅ and Pf(A) = 0 for
|X| odd. Three properties follow from the definition [Knu, (0.1), (0.4), (2.0)], [Oka, §2.1]:
       (i) a simultaneous permutation of rows and columns multiplies Pf by the sign of the permutation;
       (ii) if two indices x ≠ x' have equal rows, then Pf(A) = 0: exchanging x and x' is an involution of the
       matchings that do not pair them, which changes the sign and keeps the product, and a matching that
       pairs them has the factor axx' = ax'x' = 0;
       (iii) for a fixed index x, Pf(A) = Σz ≠ x ε(x, z)·axz·Pf(Ax,z), where Ax,z is A without the indices x, z, and ε(x,
       z) = (−1)px+pz+1 if x < z, ε(x, z) = (−1)px+pz if z < x. In particular Pf is additive in the row-and-column of
       x.
None of them needs a division or a hypothesis on 2-torsion: they hold over every commutative ring, charac‐
teristic 2 included. All the rings below are polynomial rings over Z and their quotients, and the identities are
proved there and then reduced to any field.
Definition. Let a be an alternating matrix indexed by a finite ordered set N of n indices, and c = (c1, …, cs) a
list of «border columns», each a function from N to the ring. Pf(a; c) is the Pfaffian of the alternating matrix in‐
dexed by N ⊔ {1, …, s} (first N, then the borders) whose block on N is a, whose entry in the row of i ∈ N and the
column of the border k is ck(i), and whose block on the borders is 0. For a set N of indices of variables, aN := (D−
(yi, yj))i,j ∈ N (alternating, by (8.1)), Pf(N; c) := Pf(aN; c), and, for a finite set E = {e1 < ⋯ < es} of exponents,
PfE(N) := Pf(N; ye1, …, yes), where ye is the column i ↦ yie.
In (F1)–(F7), n = |N|, s is the number of borders, and a(b) is a without the index b, the columns being restrict‐
ed to N ∖ b.
      (F1) Pf(a; c) changes sign under a transposition of two indices of N together with the corresponding
      entries of the borders, and under a transposition of two border columns (by (i)).
      (F2) Pf(a; c) is additive and homogeneous in each border column, and it vanishes if two border col‐
      umns are equal (by (iii) and (ii)). Expanding along the last border: Pf(a; c1, …, cs) = Σb ∈ N
      ηb·cs(b)·Pf(a(b); c1, …, cs−1) with ηb := (−1)n+s+pb.
      (F3) If n = s, then Pf(a; c) = (−1)s(s−1)/2·det(ck(i))i ∈ N, k ≤ s (rows indexed by N in its order); it does not
      depend on a. In particular Pf{0, 1, …, s−1}(N) = (−1)s(s−1)/2·Δ(N) for |N| = s. (A matching with a non-zero
      product pairs every border with an element of N, so it is a bijection from N to the borders.)
      (F4) Expansion along an index x ∈ N. Split the row-and-column of x into its entries against N ∖ x and
      its entries against the borders. By (iii), Pf(a; c) = ϑ·Pf(a(x); c, ax) + Σk=1s (−1)px+n+k·ck(x)·Pf(a(x); c without
      ck), ϑ := (−1)px+n+s, where ax is the border column b ↦ axb on N ∖ x; for a = aN it is D−(yx, ·), the col‐
      umn b ↦ D−(yx, yb). (In the first part, x is moved to the last place and becomes a border.)
      (F5) By (8.4) and (F2), for a set E of exponents: Pf(N'; yE, D−(yx, ·)) = −Σu=0r−2 (−1)u yxr−2−u·Pf(N'; yE,
      yu), Pf(N'; yE, D(yx, ·)) = Σu=0r−1 (−1)u yxr−1−u·Pf(N'; yE, yu), and Pf(N'; yE, yu) = 0 if u ∈ E, while it is
      (−1)#{e ∈ E : e > u}·PfE ∪ {u}(N') if u ∉ E. By (8.2), Pf(N'; yE, D(yx, ·)) = Pf(N'; yE, yr−1) − yx·Pf(N'; yE, D−(yx, ·)).
      (F6) (Lemma 5.7(ii).) For S = {b1 < ⋯ < bρ} and εbc := (−1)ρ+c: Σb ∈ S εb Δ(S ∖ b) ybu is 0 for 0 ≤ u ≤ ρ − 2
      and Δ(S) for u = ρ − 1.
      (F7) Laplace. For an alternating matrix a on N and n = s + 2κ, Pf(a; c) = (−1)s(s−1)/2·ΣS ⊆ N, |S| = s
      sgn(S)·det(ck(i))i ∈ S, k ≤ s·Pf(a|N∖S), where sgn(S) = (−1)Σi ∈ S pi − s(s−1)/2 is the sign of the shuffle that puts S
      before N ∖ S, and a|N∖S is the restriction of a. This is the case of [Oka, Proposition 2.3] in which the
      block on the borders is 0. (A matching with a non-zero product consists of a bijection from a subset S
      of N onto the borders and of a perfect matching of N ∖ S.)
The signs in (iii) and (F2)–(F7) are those of the Lean proof (§14.8); only their form matters below, except
where the text says otherwise.

8.4 Patterns and the ideals VΛ
For a partition λ, λ'c is the length of its c-th column (λ'1 = ℓ(λ)). For ℓ ≥ 0 put
E0 := {r − 1}, Eℓ := {0, 1, …, ℓ − 2} for ℓ ≥ 1 (so E1 = ∅).
Definition 8.6 (patterns). Let I be a set of m indices and (λ, δ) ∈ Shm, ℓ = ℓ(λ).
      δ = 0: a pattern consists of a set P of (m − |λ|)/2 pairwise disjoint pairs of I and of pairwise disjoint blocks
      B1, …, Bλ1 with |Bc| = λ'c covering the rest. Its product is DP·Πc Δ(Bc). (These are the tight patterns of §5.2,
      with D in the place of the divided difference of §2.3.)
      δ = 1: a pattern consists of a set P of pairwise disjoint pairs, of pairwise disjoint blocks B2, …, Bλ1 with |Bc|
      = λ'c, and of a marked block B0 with |B0| = ℓ + 1 + 2t for some t ≥ 0, which together partition I. Its
      product is DP·PfEℓ(B0)·Πc ≥ 2 Δ(Bc).
On the side of the points, the marked block carries the unpaired zero, the ℓ values of the first column of λ,
and t «absorbed» pairs. (Some of these products are 0, by §8.5, Remark (2); this does no harm.) The sizes are
consistent: a pattern of (λ, 1) uses 2|P| + (|λ| − ℓ) + (ℓ + 1 + 2t) indices, so |P| + t = (m − 1 − |λ|)/2, an inte‐
ger because λ ∈ 𝒫m−1; and the matrix of PfE ℓ (B0) has (ℓ + 1 + 2t) + |E ℓ | indices, an even number in both
cases.
For Λ ⊆ Shm, VΛ ⊆ Cm is the ideal generated by the products of all the patterns on {1, …, m} of all the shapes
of Λ. All these products have integer coefficients. A permutation of the indices maps every product to ± a
product of a pattern of the same shape (by (8.1), (F1) and the antisymmetry of Δ), so VΛ is stable under
relabelling.
Examples. (∅, 1) on one index: yr−1. (∅, 1) on three indices: ycr−1D(ya, yb) (t = 0) and Pf{r−1}({1,2,3}) = ±
(y3r−1D−(y1, y2) − y2r−1D−(y1, y3) + y1r−1D−(y2, y3)) (t = 1). ((1), 1) on two indices: D−(y1, y2). ((1,1), 1) on three
indices: ±(D−(y1, y2) − D−(y1, y3) + D−(y2, y3)).

8.5 The membership lemma
Lemma 8.7. Let ℓ ≥ 0, t ≥ 0, and let B0 be a set of ℓ + 1 + 2t indices. In Z[yi : i ∈ B0]/(yir),
               PfEℓ(B0) ∈ 𝔘ℓ(B0) := ( Δ(S)·DQ : S ⊆ B0, |S| = ℓ + 1, Q a perfect matching of B0 ∖ S ).
We prove a more general statement, in which the borders are arbitrary powers. For a finite set N of indices,
𝔘ℓ(N) is defined in the same way, with |S| = ℓ + 1.
Lemma 8.7′ (the membership lemma, any exponents). Let N be a set of n indices, t ≥ 0, and e1, …, es ≥ 0
any exponents. In Z[yi : i ∈ N]/(yir):
      (a) if n = s + 2(t + 1), then Pf(N; ye1, …, yes) ∈ 𝔘s+1(N);
      (b) if n = s + 1 + 2t, then Pf(N; ye1, …, yes, yr−1) ∈ 𝔘s(N).
Lemma 8.7 is the case s = ℓ − 1, (e1, …, es) = (0, 1, …, ℓ − 2) of (a) for ℓ ≥ 1, and the case s = 0 of (b) for ℓ = 0.
The proof takes the rest of this subsection; the symbols ωs, Ev, Od, Wσ, H and the variable ζ are local to it.
(a) Three polynomials. For odd s, 1 ≤ s ≤ 2r − 3, put ωs(a, b) := Σ (−1)u au bs−u, the sum over the u with 0 ≤
u ≤ r − 1 and 0 ≤ s − u ≤ r − 1. The set of these u is stable under u ↦ s − u, which exchanges the terms of
ωs(a, b) and of ωs(b, a) and changes the sign (−1)u, because s is odd; so ωs(b, a) = −ωs(a, b), and ωs(a, a) = 0
(the terms u and s − u ≠ u cancel; no division by 2 is involved).
      ωr−2 = D− (for s = r − 2 the conditions are 0 ≤ u ≤ r − 2).
      For odd i, 1 ≤ i ≤ r − 2: ωr−1+i(a, b) = bi·D(a, b) modulo (ar, br). (In bi D(a, b) = Σu (−1)u au br−1−u+i the
      terms with u < i have b-exponent at least r; the others are the terms of ωr−1+i.)
      In Z[a, b][ζ], with Ev(a) := Σα=0h ζα a2α and Od(a) := Σβ=0h−1 ζβ a2β+1:
                            Σσ=02h−1 ζσ ω2σ+1(a, b) = Ev(a)·Od(b) − Ev(b)·Od(a). (8.6)
Indeed, in ω2σ+1(a, b) the terms with u = 2α even have b-exponent 2β + 1 with α + β = σ, and the sign +; the
terms with u = 2β + 1 odd have b-exponent 2α, and the sign −. Every pair (α, β) with 0 ≤ α ≤ h, 0 ≤ β ≤ h − 1
occurs exactly once, with σ = α + β.
So the matrix (Σσ ζσ ω2σ+1(yi, yj))i,j has rank two: it is Ev·Odᵀ − Od·Evᵀ for the columns Ev = (Ev(yi))i and Od =
(Od(yi))i. This is the one fact behind the lemma.
Lemma 8.8 (two operations on bordered Pfaffians). Let a be an alternating matrix on N, c = (c1, …, cs)
border columns, and E, O two more columns, over a commutative ring.
      (i) (border shift) For every function λ on N and every k ≤ s: Pf(a'; c) = Pf(a; c), where a'ij := aij + λi ck(j) −
      λj ck(i).
      (ii) (rank-two update) Pf(a + E·Oᵀ − O·Eᵀ; c) = Pf(a; c) − Pf(a; c, E, O).
Proof. We use one elementary operation: for two indices x ≠ w of an alternating matrix A and an element λ,
adding λ times the row of x to the row of w, and λ times the column of x to the column of w, does not change
Pf(A). Indeed, by the additivity of (iii) in the row-and-column of w, the Pfaffian changes by λ·Pf(A''), where
A'' is A with the row and the column of w replaced by those of x (and A''ww = 0); the rows of w and x in A'' are
equal, so Pf(A'') = 0 by (ii).
(i) In the bordered matrix of (a; c), the row of the border k is −ck on N and 0 on the borders. For each w ∈ N,
add −λw times the row and the column of the border k to those of w: the entry (w, j), j ∈ N, becomes awj + λw
ck(j), the entry (i, w) becomes aiw − λw ck(i), and the border columns do not change. After all w the matrix is
the bordered matrix of (a'; c).
(ii) Let Xθ be the bordered matrix of (a; c, E, O) with the entry θ in the row of the border E and the column of
the border O (and −θ in the symmetric place). Expanding along the index O by (iii), the only term that con‐
tains θ is the one of z = E, with ε(O, E) = −1 (the borders E and O are the last two indices), and it is θ·Pf(a; c);
so Pf(Xθ) = Pf(a; c, E, O) + θ·Pf(a; c). Now take θ = 1 and, for each w ∈ N, add Ew times the row and the col‐
umn of O to those of w. The row of O is −O on N, 0 on the borders of c and −1 at E; so the block on N be‐
comes a − E·Oᵀ + O·Eᵀ, the entry of w in the column of E becomes Ew − Ew = 0, and nothing else changes.
Then the row of E is 0 except for the entry 1 at O; for each w ∈ N, adding −Ow times the row and the column
of E to those of w clears the column of O on N. The final matrix is the bordered matrix of (a − E·Oᵀ + O·Eᵀ; c)
together with the block [[0, 1], [−1, 0]] on the last two indices, and its Pfaffian, expanded along O, is Pf(a −
E·Oᵀ + O·Eᵀ; c). Hence Pf(a − E·Oᵀ + O·Eᵀ; c) = Pf(a; c) + Pf(a; c, E, O); replacing O by −O and using (F2)
gives (ii). ∎
There is no sign that depends on n or s. Two more facts follow at once from the definition of the Pfaffian as a
sum over matchings, in which a matching with a non-zero product uses one entry of each border and κ := (n
− s)/2 entries of a: (homogeneity) Pf(τ·a; c) = τκ·Pf(a; c) for every scalar τ; and (top coefficients) if a and c
have entries in a polynomial ring A[ζ], those of a of degree at most d and those of the border ck of degree at
most dk, then Pf(a; c) has degree at most κd + Σk dk in ζ, and its coefficient there is Pf(atop; ctop), the bordered
Pfaffian of the coefficients of the degrees d and dk.
Corollary 8.9 (the closed forms). Let A be a commutative ring, yi ∈ A (i ∈ N), Wσ := (ω2σ+1(yi, yj))i,j ∈ N, H
:= Στ=0h−1 ζτ Wh+τ over A[ζ], and Ev, Od the columns of (8.6) at the yi. For border columns c = (c1, …, cs) over
A:
      (a) if n = s + 2(t + 1), then Pf(N; c) = (−1)t+1·[ζh−1−t] Pf(H; c, Ev, Od) for t ≤ h − 1, and Pf(N; c) = 0 for
      t ≥ h;
      (b) if n = s + 1 + 2t, then Pf(N; c, yr−1) = (−1)t·[ζh−t] Pf(H; c, Ev) for t ≤ h, and Pf(N; c, yr−1) = 0 for t >
      h.
Proof. Put L := Σσ=0h−1 ζσ Wσ. By (8.6), L + ζh H = Ev·Odᵀ − Od·Evᵀ, entry by entry. The entries of L have de‐
gree at most h − 1 in ζ, with top coefficient Wh−1 = aN. (a) By Lemma 8.8(ii) with a := −ζh H and
homogeneity,
            Pf(L; c) = Pf(−ζh H; c) − Pf(−ζh H; c, Ev, Od) = (−ζh)t+1·Pf(H; c) − (−ζh)t·Pf(H; c, Ev, Od).
Take the coefficient of ζ(h−1)(t+1): on the left it is Pf(aN; c) = Pf(N; c), by the top coefficients; the first term on
the right is a multiple of ζh(t+1), so it contributes 0; the second contributes (−1)t+1·[ζh−1−t] Pf(H; c, Ev, Od) if ht
≤ (h−1)(t+1), i.e. t ≤ h − 1, and 0 otherwise. (b) By Lemma 8.8(i), applied to a := −ζh H, the last border Ev
and λ := −Od, Pf(−ζh H; c, Ev) = Pf(−ζh H + Ev·Odᵀ − Od·Evᵀ; c, Ev) = Pf(L; c, Ev); and Pf(−ζh H; c, Ev) =
(−ζh)t·Pf(H; c, Ev). The border Ev has degree h with top coefficient y2h = yr−1; take the coefficient of ζ(h−1)t + h:
on the left it is Pf(N; c, yr−1), and on the right (−1)t·[ζh−t] Pf(H; c, Ev) if t ≤ h, and 0 otherwise. ∎
Proof of Lemma 8.7′. Work in A := Z[yi : i ∈ N]/(yir). Since 2(h + τ) + 1 = (r − 1) + (2τ + 1), the second item of
(a) gives Hij = Στ ζτ yj2τ+1 D(yi, yj) = D(yi, yj)·Od(yj) in A[ζ]. By the definition of the Pfaffian as a sum over
matchings, Pf(H|N') therefore lies, for every subset N', in the ideal of A[ζ] generated by the products DQ, Q a
perfect matching of N'. (a) Expand Pf(H; ye1, …, yes, Ev, Od) by (F7). Its s + 2 borders are values Pk(yi) of poly‐
nomials Pk ∈ Z[ζ][T] in one variable; so for each S ⊆ N with |S| = s + 2, det(Pk(yi))i ∈ S is an alternating poly‐
nomial in the variables yi, i ∈ S, and hence a multiple of Δ(S) in Z[ζ][yi : i ∈ S]. So every coefficient of every
power of ζ in Pf(H; ye1, …, yes, Ev, Od) is a combination of products Δ(S)·DQ, with |S| = s + 2 and Q a perfect
matching of N ∖ S: it lies in 𝔘s+1(N). By Corollary 8.9(a), so does Pf(N; ye1, …, yes). (b) The same with the s + 1
borders ye1, …, yes, Ev and Corollary 8.9(b). ∎
Remarks. (1) The smallest non-trivial case of Lemma 8.7, (ℓ, t) = (0, 1), says y3r−1D−(y1, y2) − y2r−1D−(y1, y3) +
y1r−1D−(y2, y3) ∈ (D(y1, y2), D(y1, y3), D(y2, y3)); for r = 3 it reads Δ(y1, y2, y3) = y1D(y1, y2) − y1D(y1, y3) +
y2D(y2, y3) modulo the cubes. (2) Some patterns are zero. By Corollary 8.9, PfEℓ(B0) = 0 when ℓ ≥ 1 and t ≥ h,
and when ℓ = 0 and t > h; such patterns of Definition 8.6 contribute nothing, and do no harm. (3) The border
yr−1 in (b) cannot be dropped: at r = 5, n = 3, Pf(N; y2) does not lie in 𝔘0(N) (§14.9). (4) Version 11 proved
Lemma 8.7 in the exterior algebra on a free module with basis v0, …, vr−1, with divided powers of 2-forms:
there (8.6) says that the generating function of the 2-forms of the ωs is the product of two 1-forms, and
Lemma 8.8(ii) is the formula γκ(φ + E ∧ O) = γκ(φ) + γκ−1(φ) ∧ E ∧ O for the divided powers γκ of a 2-form φ
and two 1-forms E, O. The proof printed here is the one that was formalized (§14.8); it uses no exterior alge‐
bra, no divided powers and no torsion-freeness, and it proves Lemma 8.7′, the statement for other border sets
that problem 7(c) of version 11 asked for.

8.6 (P3) Every pattern of a layer lies in the right slice
Proposition 8.10. Let Λ be an interlaced pair of level m ≥ 1 and 0 ≤ i ≤ r − 1. Then VΛi ⊆ Wr−1−i(VΛ), where
VΛi ⊆ Cm−1 is built on the indices 2, …, m.
Proof. Wr−1−i(VΛ) is an ideal, so it suffices to treat a generator: let σ = (μ, δ) ∈ Λi, ℓ = ℓ(μ), Φ := FΛ(σ) ≥ i + 1,
and let G be the product of a pattern of σ on {2, …, m}. Since the slices are nested and r − Φ ≤ r − 1 − i, it is
enough to find f ∈ VΛ with degy1 f ≤ r − Φ and [y1r−Φ] f = ±G, or to show otherwise that G ∈ Wr−Φ(VΛ). Write
G = DP·Δ(B1)·R if δ = 0 and G = DP·PfEℓ(B0)·R if δ = 1, where R := Πc ≥ 2 Δ(Bc). By Lemma 8.3 the options in Λ
are an initial segment of the chain, of length Φ; we split according to the last one.
(A) The last option in Λ is an addition. Let j0 be the smallest j with (μ + ej, δ) ∈ Λ; it is the first row of its
length, and Φ = ℓ + 1 + (r − 1 − 2ℓ) + (ℓ − j0 + 1) = r − j0 + 1. The column c* := μj0 + 1 ≥ 2 of μ has height j0
− 1; put the index 1 into its block Bc* (a new block if c* is a new column). This is a pattern of (μ + ej0, δ): the
number of pairs is unchanged, and for δ = 1 the first column, hence ℓ, E ℓ and the marked block, are un‐
touched because c* ≥ 2. By the factorisation Δ(B ∪ {1}) = ±Πc ∈ B(yc − y1)·Δ(B) of the Vandermonde determi‐
nant, its product is ±G·Πc ∈ Bc (yc − y1): its y1-degree is j0 − 1 = r − Φ (and y1j0−1 ≠ 0 in the box, as j0 − 1 < r),
                               *

and its top coefficient is ±G.
(M) The last option in Λ is the middle one (so ℓ < h and Φ = ℓ + 1 + (r − 1 − 2ℓ) = r − ℓ).
      δ = 0: put the index 1 into B1 (of height ℓ): a pattern of (μ ⊔ 1, 0), whose product has y1-degree ℓ = r −
      Φ and top coefficient ±G.
      δ = 1: here (μ ⊔ 1, 1) ∈ Λ and (μ, 0) ∈ Λ (the zero option precedes the middle one). Put E+ := Eℓ+1 = {0,

      …, ℓ − 1} and f := ( y1·PfE+(B0 ∪ {1}) + ϑ·Pf(B0; yE , D(y1, ·)) )·DP·R, with ϑ = ±1 the sign of (F4) for N =
      B0 ∪ {1}, x = 1. f ∈ VΛ. PfE+(B0 ∪ {1})·DP·R is the product of a pattern of (μ ⊔ 1, 1): its marked block B0 ∪
      {1} has size (ℓ + 1) + 1 + 2t, its borders are Eℓ+1, and the columns c ≥ 2 and the pairs are those of μ. By
