      = r − Φ and the top coefficient is ±G.
(Z) The last option in Λ is the zero option (Φ = ℓ + 1).
      δ = 0: here (μ, 1) ∈ Λ. If ℓ = 0, f := y1r−1·G is the product of a pattern of (∅, 1) with marked block {1}
      (Pf{r−1}({1}) = y1r−1), of y1-degree r − 1 = r − Φ. If ℓ ≥ 1, f := PfEℓ(B1 ∪ {1})·DP·R is the product of a pat‐
      tern of (μ, 1) with marked block B1 ∪ {1} (of size ℓ + 1, so t = 0). By (F4) and (F5), PfE ℓ (B1 ∪ {1}) = ∓
      Σu=0r−2 (−1)u y1r−2−u Pf(B1; yEℓ, yu) + Σe ∈ Eℓ ± y1e PfEℓ ∖ e(B1). Pf(B1; yEℓ, yu) = 0 for u ≤ ℓ − 2, and for u =
      ℓ − 1 it is ±Pf{0, …, ℓ−1}(B1) = ±Δ(B1) by (F3). So the first sum has y1-degree r − 2 − (ℓ − 1) = r − ℓ − 1,
      with top coefficient ±Δ(B1); the second has y1-degree at most ℓ − 2 < r − ℓ − 1 (as ℓ ≤ h). So degy1 f = r
      − ℓ − 1 = r − Φ, with top coefficient ±G.
      δ = 1: here (μ, 0) ∈ Λ. For S ⊆ B0 with |S| = ℓ + 1 and a perfect matching Q of B0 ∖ S put, with the signs
      of (F6), fS,Q := Σb ∈ S εb Δ(S ∖ b)·D(y1, yb)·DQ·DP·R. Each summand is the product of a pattern of (μ, 0):
      first block S ∖ b (of size ℓ), blocks Bc (c ≥ 2), pairs P ∪ Q ∪ {(1, b)} (their number is |P| + t + 1 = (m − |
      μ|)/2). So fS,Q ∈ VΛ. By (8.4) and (F6), [y1r−1−u] fS,Q = (−1)u (Σb εb Δ(S ∖ b) ybu)·DQ DP R vanishes for u ≤
      ℓ − 1 and is ±Δ(S)·DQ·DP R for u = ℓ. Hence Δ(S) DQ·DP R ∈ Wr−1−ℓ(VΛ) for all S, Q. As Wr−1−ℓ(VΛ) is
      an ideal, it contains the image of 𝔘ℓ(B0) in Cm−1 times DP R, and by Lemma 8.7 — a membership over
      Z, which therefore holds for the images in Cm−1 over every field — it contains PfEℓ(B0)·DP R = G. And r
      − 1 − ℓ = r − Φ.
(R) The last option in Λ is a removal. Then the removals in Λ are those of the rows 1, …, ρ, with ρ ≥ 1 the
last row of its length, and Φ = ρ.
      δ = 0, or δ = 1 and μρ ≥ 2: the column c0 := μρ of μ has height ρ, and its block S := Bc0 is an ordinary
      block (c0 ≥ 2 if δ = 1). Write G = Δ(S)·G'. As in case (γ) of Proposition 5.8, f := Σb ∈ S εb Δ(S ∖ b)·D(y1,
      yb)·G' is a sum of products of patterns of (μ − eρ, δ) (pairs P ∪ {(1, b)}, the block of the column c0 re‐
      placed by S ∖ b; for δ = 1 the first column, hence the marked block, is unchanged). By (8.4) and (F6),
      [y1r−1−u] f vanishes for u ≤ ρ − 2 and is ±G for u = ρ − 1: degy1 f = r − ρ = r − Φ.
      δ = 1 and μρ = 1: then ρ = ℓ. Put E' := Eℓ−1 and f := PfE'(B0 ∪ {1})·DP·R. It is the product of a pattern of
      (μ − eℓ, 1): the first column of μ − eℓ has ℓ − 1 rows, the marked block B0 ∪ {1} has size ((ℓ − 1) + 1) +
      2(t + 1), and the other columns are those of μ. By (F4) and (F5), PfE'(B0 ∪ {1}) = ∓ Σu=0r−2 (−1)u y1r−2−u
      Pf(B0; yE', yu) + Σe ∈ E' ± y1e PfE' ∖ e(B0). If ℓ ≥ 2: E' = {0, …, ℓ − 3}; the first sum vanishes for u ≤ ℓ − 3,
      and for u = ℓ − 2 it gives ±y1r−ℓ PfEℓ(B0); the second sum has y1-degree at most ℓ − 3 < r − ℓ. If ℓ = 1:
      E' = {r − 1}; the first sum has y1-degree at most r − 2, and the second is ±y1r−1 Pf∅(B0) = ±y1r−1
      PfE1(B0). In both cases degy1 f = r − ℓ = r − Φ, with top coefficient ±G.
The four kinds of last option exhaust Φ ≥ 1. ∎
In every case the construction lands exactly in the slice r − Φ. The absorbed pairs (t ≥ 1) are produced by the
second case of (R): a removal that empties the first column of a marked tail turns a pair D outside the
marked block into a pair D− inside it.

8.7 The theorem
Theorem 8.11 (interlaced down-set theorem). For every field F, every odd r ≥ 3, every m ≥ 0 and every in‐
terlaced pair Λ ⊆ Shm, dimF VΛ ≥ |ZΛ|.
Proof. Induction on m. For m = 0, Sh0 = {(∅, 0)}: if Λ = ∅ both sides are 0; otherwise VΛ = F (the empty prod‐
uct) and ZΛ is one point. Let m ≥ 1. By the peeling lemma, Proposition 8.10, the induction hypothesis applied
to the interlaced pairs Λi (Lemma 8.4; VΛi is stable under relabelling, so the variables y2, …, ym may be used),
and Lemma 8.4 again,
dim VΛ = Σi=0r−1 dim Wr−1−i(VΛ) ≥ Σi dim VΛi ≥ Σi |ZΛi| = Σi |(ZΛ)>i| = |ZΛ|. ∎
Proof of Theorem O, the inequality ≥. Take m = 2k + 1 and Λ = Λroot (Lemma 8.5). The patterns of ((1), 0) con‐
sist of k disjoint pairs and one block of one element; their products are the DP, that is, the DJ, J ∈ 𝒥 (D is sym‐
metric). The patterns of (∅, 1) have products Pf{r−1}(B0)·DP' with |B0| = 1 + 2t; by Lemma 8.7 with ℓ = 0,
Pf{r−1}(B0) lies in the ideal generated by the DQ, Q a perfect matching of B0 minus one index (here Δ(S) = 1, as
|S| = 1); since |Q| + |P'| = t + (k − t) = k, the product lies in (DJ : J ∈ 𝒥). Hence VΛroot = (DJ : J ∈ 𝒥)·C2k+1, and
Theorem 8.11 gives dim ≥ |ZΛroot| = Nr(2k + 2). ∎
Lemma 8.12 (the two forms). Let N = 2k + 2, write the variables as x0, y1, …, y2k+1, and put ℳ := ΣP DP·CN,
the sum over the perfect matchings P of the N variables (the ideal V{(∅,0)} of level N). Then θ : ℳ → C2k+1, f ↦
[x0r−1] f, is injective, and its image is (DJ : J ∈ 𝒥)C2k+1.
Proof. By (8.3), (xa + xb)·D(xa, xb) = 0 in the box, so every DP is killed by e1 = x0 + s, s := Σi yi (sum over the
pairs of P). Write f = Σu=0r−1 x0u fu with fu ∈ C2k+1; comparing the coefficients of x0u in x0 f = −s f gives fu−1 =
−s fu for u ≥ 1. So f is determined by fr−1, and θ is injective. If k0 is the partner of 0 in P, the coefficient of
x0r−1 in D(x0, yk0) is 1, so θ(DP·g) = DJ·g for g ∈ C2k+1, where J is P read as a matching of {0, …, 2k+1}.
Moreover x0·DP = −yk0DP, so ℳ = ΣP DP·C2k+1. Hence θ(ℳ) = (DJ : J ∈ 𝒥)C2k+1. ∎
(The same proof works for every r ≥ 2, with (−1)r−1 in the place of 1; for the even box it is the proof of
Corollary 10.6(i).)
Corollary 8.13 (equality; freeness over Z). For every field F, every odd r ≥ 3 and every k ≥ 0: dimF ΣP
DP·C2k+2 = Nr(2k + 2) and dimF (DJ : J ∈ 𝒥)·C2k+1 = Nr(2k + 2). Both quotients of the integral box rings are
free abelian groups, and the dimensions of the graded pieces of the two ideals do not depend on F. In particular
Theorem O holds.
Proof. By Lemma 8.12 the two dimensions are equal, and each is at least Nr(2k + 2): the second by the part of
Theorem O already proved, the first by Theorem 8.11 for the interlaced pair {(∅, 0)} of level 2k + 2 (Lemma
8.5), whose patterns are the perfect matchings. For the upper bound, let first F = Q and T := {−h, …, h} ⊂ Q,
and put IP := (xa + xb : {a, b} ∈ P) and ℳ := ΣP DP·CN. On CN let ⟨f, g⟩ be the coefficient of Πi xir−1 in fg; as in
the proof of Proposition 2.6 the pairing is perfect, so dim V + dim V⊥ = dim CN for every subspace V, and the
annihilator of an ideal is contained in its orthogonal. By (8.3), (xa + xb)·D(xa, xb) = 0 in the box, so IP·CN anni‐
hilates DP, and ⋂P IP·CN annihilates ℳ. Hence dim ℳ ≤ dim CN − dim ⋂P IP·CN = dim Q[x]/⋂P (IP + (xir)).
Let Γ ⊆ TN be the set of the points of ⋃P V(IP) in TN, that is, the set of the N-tuples that split into pairs {u,
−u}; |Γ| = Nr(N). With g(x) := Πu ∈ T(x − u), an odd monic polynomial of degree r, the ideal of the finite set
V(IP) ∩ TN is IP + (g(xi) : i), and its ideal of top-degree forms is IP + (xir) (it contains it, and both quotients
have dimension rk+1; Lemma 5.8′). Since I(Γ) = ⋂P I(V(IP) ∩ TN) and gr of an intersection is contained in the
intersection of the gr's, gr(I(Γ)) ⊆ ⋂P (IP + (xir)), whence dim Q[x]/⋂P(IP + (xir)) ≤ dim Q[x]/gr(I(Γ)) = |Γ|. So
dimQ ΣP DP·CN ≤ Nr(N). Over an arbitrary field F, the ideal ΣP DP·CN is spanned by the images of the vectors
DP·xα, which have integer coordinates, and the rank of an integer matrix over F is at most its rank over Q.
This gives the two equalities over every field. The integral statement follows as in the proof of Theorem 5.9′:
the quotient M of the integral box ring by the ideal generated by the DP (respectively the DJ) is a graded, fi‐
nitely generated abelian group, M ⊗ F is the quotient over F, and the dimension of M ⊗ F is the same for F =
Q and for every F = Fp; so no graded piece of M has torsion. ∎
Remarks 8.14. (1) Where the full family enters. Only at the root (Lemma 8.5 and the proof of Theorem O), as
in §5. With one matching removed the dimension drops in every cell we computed, also in characteristic 2
(§14.9). (2) No slack. In Proposition 8.10 the slice r − 1 − i cannot be lowered: with r − 2 − i the containment
fails in every cell tested (§14.9). (3) Equality for every interlaced pair is measured, not proved: dim VΛ = |ZΛ|
held for every interlaced pair of every cell computed, and for pairs of down-sets that satisfy (D1) but not (D2)
the two numbers differ (§14.9). (4) The marked shapes are needed. Without the bordered Pfaffians — with the
patterns t = 0 only — the equality of (3) fails for 15 of the 163 interlaced pairs of the auditor’s run (§14.9): the
absorbed pairs are part of the ideal. (5) Characteristic. Unlike the bipartite ideals of §7, the ideals VΛ of this
section are defined over Z, and Theorem 8.11 holds over every field with no condition; in particular over F2,
which is where §9 uses it for the degrees 2v.

8.8 The case r = 3, without Pfaffians
For r = 3 (h = 1) the theory collapses to a family with one parameter, and every step is a one-line identity.
This gives a proof of Theorem O at r = 3 that uses nothing from §8.2–§8.7; with §9 it suffices for the degrees
m = 4, 6 and 12. Here T = {−1, 0, 1}, Cm = F[y1, …, ym]/(yi3) and D(a, b) = a2 − ab + b2. The shape of M ∈ Tm
is determined by j(M) := |Σi Mi| (the mark is the parity of m − j), and the interlaced pairs are the sets {j ≤ J}.
For 0 ≤ J ≤ m put ZJ(m) := {M ∈ {−1, 0, 1}m : |Σ Mi| ≤ J}, and define an ideal I(m, J) ⊆ Cm as follows, where P,
P+, P' denote sets of pairwise disjoint pairs of indices and a, b, c distinct indices outside them:
      if J ≡ m (mod 2): I(m, J) := (DP : |P| = (m − J)/2);
      if J ≢ m (mod 2) and J ≥ 1: I(m, J) := ((yb − ya)·DP : |P| = (m − 1 − J)/2; DP+ : |P+| = (m + 1 − J)/2);
      if J = 0 and m is odd: I(m, 0) := (yc2·DP : P a perfect matching of the other indices; Δ(ya, yb, yc)·DP' : P' a
      perfect matching of the other indices).
Theorem 8.15. dimF I(m, J) ≥ |ZJ(m)| for every field F and all 0 ≤ J ≤ m. In particular I(2k + 1, 1) is the ideal
generated by the DP, P ∈ 𝒥, in C2k+1, and |Z1(2k+1)| = N3(2k + 2): this is Theorem O, ≥, at r = 3.
Proof. Induction on m; I(0, 0) = F. For J = m the ideal is the unit ideal and Z is everything. A tail with |Σ| = j'
has 3 completions in ZJ(m) if j' ≤ J − 1, 2 if j' = J ≥ 1, 1 if j' = J + 1 or j' = J = 0, and none otherwise. So |ZJ(m)|
= |ZJ+1(m−1)| + |ZJ(m−1)| + |ZJ−1(m−1)| for J ≥ 1, and |Z0(m)| = |Z1(m−1)| (with ZJ(m−1) everything and I(m − 1, J)
the unit ideal for J ≥ m − 1). By peeling and induction it suffices to show, for the slices of I(m, J):
W2 ⊇ I(m−1, J+1), W1 ⊇ I(m−1, J), W0 ⊇ I(m−1, J−1) for J ≥ 1; W2 ⊇ I(m−1, 1) for J = 0.
Write D(1, b) for D(y1, yb) = y12 − y1 yb + yb2; the sets of pairs below lie in {2, …, m}, and «free» means an in‐
dex of {2, …, m} outside them.
Case I: J ≡ m, 1 ≤ J ≤ m − 2, p := (m − J)/2 ≥ 1; I(m, J) = (DP : |P| = p).
      W2 ⊇ I(m−1, J+1) = (DP' : |P'| = p − 1): there are J + 1 ≥ 2 free indices; for a free b, D(1, b)·DP' ∈ I(m, J)
      has y12-coefficient DP'.
      W1 ⊇ I(m−1, J) = ((yb − ya)DP' : |P'| = p − 1; DP'' : |P''| = p): (D(1, b) − D(1, a))·DP' = (−y1(yb − ya) + yb2
      − ya2)·DP' ∈ I(m, J); and DP'' ∈ I(m, J) does not contain y1.
      W0 ⊇ I(m−1, J−1) = (DP'' : |P''| = p): these are generators of I(m, J) without y1.
Case II: J ≢ m, 1 ≤ J ≤ m − 1, p := (m − 1 − J)/2; I(m, J) = ((yb − ya)DP : |P| = p; DP+ : |P+| = p + 1).
      W2 ⊇ I(m−1, J+1). If p = 0 this is the unit ideal, and D(1, b) ∈ I(m, J) has y12-coefficient 1. If p ≥ 1 its
      generators are (yb − ya)DP' (|P'| = p − 1) and DP (|P| = p); there is a free index c besides a, b in the first
      case and a free b in the second, and D(1, c)(yb − ya)DP', D(1, b)DP lie in I(m, J) with y12-coefficients the
      generators.
      W1 ⊇ I(m−1, J) = (DP : |P| = p): for a free b, (yb − y1)·DP ∈ I(m, J) has y1-coefficient −DP.
      W0 ⊇ I(m−1, J−1). If J ≥ 2, its generators (yb − ya)DP (|P| = p), DP+ (|P+| = p + 1) are generators of I(m,
      J) without y1. If J = 1 (m even), it is I(m − 1, 0) = (yc2 DP, Δ(ya, yb, yc) DP'), and yc2 = y1·(yc − y1) + D(1,
      c), Δ(ya, yb, yc) = D(1, a)(yc − yb) − D(1, b)(yc − ya) + D(1, c)(yb − ya) (in the second identity the coeffi‐
      cients of y12 and of y1 cancel, and the constant term is ya2(yc − yb) − yb2(yc − ya) + yc2(yb − ya) = Δ(ya,
      yb, yc)). So yc2 DP and Δ·DP' lie in I(m, 1) and do not contain y1.
Case III: J = 0. If m is even, I(m, 0) = (DP+ : P+ a perfect matching) and I(m − 1, 1) = (DP' : |P'| = (m − 2)/2):
with the free index b, D(1, b)DP' has y12-coefficient DP'. If m is odd, I(m − 1, 1) = ((yb − ya)DP'; DP+) with P+ a
perfect matching of {2, …, m} and P' one of {2, …, m} ∖ {a, b}: y12·DP+ ∈ I(m, 0), and Δ(y1, ya, yb)·DP' = (ya − y1)
(yb − y1)(yb − ya)DP' ∈ I(m, 0) has y12-coefficient (yb − ya)DP'.
For the last sentence of the theorem: the DP with |P| = k in 2k + 1 variables are the DJ; and deleting the last
entry is a bijection from the (2k+2)-tuples in {−1, 0, 1} of sum 0 onto Z1(2k+1). ∎

8.9 A worked example: r = 3, k = 1
Here m = 3, the three generators are D(y2, y3), D(y1, y3), D(y1, y2) in F[y1, y2, y3]/(yi3), a ring of dimension 27,
and N3(4) = 19: the 4-tuples in {−1, 0, 1} of sum 0 number 1 + 12 + 6. The root is Λ = {((1), 0), (∅, 1)}. The
tails have two coordinates, with shapes in Sh2 = {(∅, 0), ((2), 0), ((1), 1)}, and |Z(∅,0)| = 3, |Z((2),0)| = 2, |Z((1),1)|
= 4 (total 9).

            tail shape   its chain (positions 1, 2, 3)       options in Λ   FΛ   case of Prop. 8.10   slice r − Φ
              (∅, 0)        (∅, 1) · ((1), 0) · ((1), 0)          all       3        (M), δ = 0           0
              ((1), 1)      (∅, 1) · ((1), 0) · ((2), 1)       first two    2        (Z), δ = 1           1
              ((2), 0)      ((1), 0) · ((2), 1) · ((3), 0)       first      1           (R)               2

So Λ0 = Sh2, Λ1 = {(∅, 0), ((1), 1)} and Λ2 = {(∅, 0)}, all interlaced, and |ZΛ| = 9 + 7 + 3 = 19. On the side of
the ideal: W0 ∋ D(y2, y3) (the generator of VΛ2); W1 contains y3 − y2, from D(y1, y2) − D(y1, y3) = −y1(y2 − y3) +
y22 − y32, which is the case (Z) with B0 = {2, 3}, ℓ = 1, t = 0; and W2 ∋ 1, from D(y1, y2). The dimensions of the
three slices are 3, 7 and 9.

9. Even degrees
In this section m ≥ 4 is even, k ≥ 1, n = 2k and N = n + 2, except in §9.2, which also allows the degree 2 and k
≥ 0 (Lemma 9.10 uses it in that generality). Nothing in §2.1 or in Appendix B uses the parity of m: Theorem 0
and Proposition 2.1 hold for every m ≥ 3, and [DS] themselves compute even cells ([DS, Remark 4.4] and
[DS, §5]). So, as for odd m, the conjecture at (k, m) is the equality dimFp (ψ̄ J : J ∈ 𝒥)Fp[G] = |Γ𝒥| for every
prime p dividing m, and ≤ always holds. What changes is everything after §2.1. Inversion on μm ∖ {1} now has
the fixed point −1, so the count is different (§9.1). At the prime 2, the element 2 is not a unit and the coordi‐
nate y = t − t−1 of Lemma 2.3 does not exist: for m = 2v we pass to the ideal of leading forms, which contains
s1 ⋯ s2k+1 times the ideal of Theorem O for the box 2v − 1 in characteristic 2, an isomorphic copy of it in an‐
other ring (§9.2). For the other even degrees the colour reduction of §6 applies at each prime (§9.3), with two
new kinds of block: at an odd prime the cofactor is even, the colour −1 is its own inverse, and its block is the
ideal of Theorem O for the box pv (§9.4); at the prime 2 the block of colour 1 is the problem for the degree 2v
again, and the pair blocks are bipartite ideals with an even box.
In this section the letter r is the odd box of §8, and the cofactor of a prime power in m, which §6 calls r, is
written r'. The count Nr(n) of §8 makes sense for r = 1 (h = 0): N1(n) = 1, the only tuple being (0, …, 0); it is
used in that sense in §9.2 and §9.4.

9.1 The count
Lemma 9.1. For every m ≥ 3, Γ𝒥 is in bijection with the set of N-tuples in μm ∖ {1} that can be split into pairs of
mutually inverse entries. For even m their number is
                                   |Γ𝒥| = Nm−1(N) = N!·[xN] cosh(x)·I0(2x)(m−2)/2.
Proof. The first paragraph of the proof of Lemma 2.2 uses nothing about m: for a ∈ Γ𝒥, with a matching J, put
a0 := (a1 ⋯ an+1)−1; then a0 = ak0−1 ≠ 1, and every pair of J is a pair of inverse entries of (a0, …, an+1).
Conversely a tuple that splits into inverse pairs has product 1, so its entry at 0 is determined by the others,
which lie in Γ𝒥 through the same matching. For even m, inversion on μm ∖ {1} has the single fixed point −1
and (m − 2)/2 orbits {c, c−1}. A tuple splits into inverse pairs if and only if, for every orbit, c and c−1 occur
equally often, and −1 occurs an even number of times. Each orbit contributes the exponential generating
function I0(2x), and the value −1 contributes cosh x. A bijection μm ∖ {1} → T that carries inversion to u ↦ −u
and −1 to 0 (with r = m − 1) identifies these tuples with the tuples counted by Nm−1(N) in §8. ∎
We extend the notation of §1.2: for even m, Qk(m) := Nm−1(2k + 2). So Qk(m) = |Γ𝒥| for every m ≥ 3. The first
values are
                            m\k      1          2           3              4                   5
                              4      19        141        1 107          8 953               73 789
                              6      61       1 001      18 733         375 745          7 858 225
                              8     127       3 301      103 279       3 595 177       133 789 789
                              10    217       7 761      345 465      17 605 249       980 612 161
                              12    331      15 101      876 331      59 415 961      4 481 629 021
                              16    631      41 301     3 529 863    361 612 105      42 214 788 925

For k = 1, Q1(m) = 3m2 − 9m + 7, and the values for k = 2, 3 are those of the polynomials displayed in [DS,
Remark 4.4] for even m (with their δm = 1); for instance 141, 1 001 and 1 107 at (n, m) = (4, 4), (4, 6), (6, 4).
For m = 4 the numbers are the central trinomial coefficients, the constant terms of (x + 1 + x−1)2k+2. In gen‐
eral, for even m the rank Qk(m) + 1 of L(X) is one more than the count Nm−1(2k+2) of Theorem A at the odd
number m − 1 (§10).

9.2 Degree 2v: leading forms
Let q = 2v with v ≥ 1, k ≥ 0, and let F be a field of characteristic 2. Put si := ti + 1. Since tq − 1 = (t + 1)q,
                             F[G] = F[t1, …, t2k+1]/(tiq − 1) = F[s1, …, s2k+1]/(siq) =: B,
a graded ring. Let I := (ψ̄ J : J ∈ 𝒥)B, where ψ̄ J is the image of the generator ψJ of §2 for the degree m = q.
Proposition 9.2. dimF I ≥ Nq−1(2k + 2). Hence, for v ≥ 2 and k ≥ 1, dimF2(ψ̄ J : J ∈ 𝒥)F2[G] = |Γ𝒥|, and H2k(X;
Z)/L(X) is torsion free for the Fermat variety of degree 2v and dimension 2k.
Proof. The ψ̄ J have coefficients in F2, so the dimension does not depend on F; let F = F2.
Step 1: the generators. In F2[u], (u + 1)φ(u) = uq + 1 = (u + 1)q, so φ(u) = (u + 1)q−1. For u = tjtl, u + 1 = (1 +
sj)(1 + sl) + 1 = sj + sl + sjsl. So
                                         ψ̄ J = Πi=0k ski · Πi=1k (sji + ski + sjiski)q−1,
and, as a polynomial, its homogeneous component of lowest degree is LJ := Πi=0k ski · Πi=1k (sji + ski)q−1, of de‐
gree (k + 1) + k(q − 1).
Step 2: leading forms. For 0 ≠ f ∈ B let in(f) be its homogeneous component of lowest degree, and let in(I) be
the span of the in(f), 0 ≠ f ∈ I. Let I≥d be the set of the elements of I with no component of degree less than
d. The map I≥d → Bd that takes the component of degree d has kernel I≥d+1, and its image is in(I) ∩ Bd.
Summing over d, dim in(I) = dim I. Moreover in(I) is an ideal: for a monomial x and f ∈ I, x·in(f) is 0 or
in(xf). Since the ideal (siq) is homogeneous, the image of LJ in B is 0 or in(ψ̄ J). Hence
                                        dim I = dim in(I) ≥ dim (LJ : J ∈ 𝒥)B.
Step 3: the odd box. Put r := q − 1. In F2[a, b], (a + b)·Σu=0q−1 au bq−1−u = aq + bq = (a + b)q, so (a + b)q−1 =
Σu=0q−1 au bq−1−u. Multiply by b and drop bq: b(a + b)q−1 = ab·Σw=0q−2 aw bq−2−w in F2[a, b]/(bq), and the last
sum is the polynomial D(a, b) = Dr(a, b) of §8 over F2, where the signs disappear. The indices k0 and ji, ki (i ≥
1) are 1, …, 2k+1, each once; so LJ = Y·DJ in B, with Y := s1 ⋯ s2k+1 and DJ := Πi=1k D(sji, ski). Multiplication
by Y on B kills exactly the monomials with some exponent at least q − 1, so it induces an injective map
F2[s]/(sir) → B, which carries the ideal (DJ : J ∈ 𝒥)·F2[s]/(sir) onto (LJ : J ∈ 𝒥)B. The former is the ideal of
Theorem O at (k, r, F2).
Conclusion. If q ≥ 4, then r = 2v − 1 ≥ 3 is odd and Theorem O gives dim (LJ)B ≥ Nr(2k + 2). If q = 2, then r =
1, LJ = Y ≠ 0 for every J, and dim (LJ)B = 1 = N1(2k + 2). With Step 2 this is the inequality. For k ≥ 1 and m =
q ≥ 4, Proposition 2.1 and Lemma 9.1 give dim I ≤ |Γ𝒥| = Nq−1(2k + 2), so there is equality at the only prime
dividing m. ∎
Remarks 9.3. (1) The proof shows more: in(I) = (LJ : J ∈ 𝒥)B. The generators ψ̄ J are a standard basis of I for
the filtration by the order of vanishing, in the sense that their leading forms generate the ideal of leading
forms. (2) The inequality of Step 2 is where information could be lost; Theorem O says that none is. The
statement «dimF2(DJ)·F2[s]/(sir) ≥ Nr(2k+2) for r = 2v − 1» is, on its face, stronger than the conjecture at (k,
2v): it is the conjecture together with (1). (3) For q = 2, ψ̄ J = Y for every J (in Step 1, sl(sj + sl + sjsl) = sjsl, be‐
cause sl2 = 0), and I is the socle of B.

9.3 The colour reduction for an even degree
Let p be a prime dividing m, and write m = q·r' with q = pv and p ∤ r'. Put F := F̄ p, F[G] := F[t1, …, tn+1]/(tim −
1) and I := (ψ̄ J : J ∈ 𝒥)F[G], as in §6. By Proposition 2.1 and Lemma 9.1, exactly as in §6,
                                              dimF I ≤ |Γ𝒥| = Qk(m), (9.1)
and the conjecture at (k, m) is equivalent to dimF I ≥ Qk(m) for every prime p dividing m.
Lemma 9.4 (splitting and survival, for every prime). Lemmas 6.1 and 6.2 hold as stated, with r' in the
place of r, for every prime p dividing m and every m ≥ 3.
Proof. The proof of Lemma 6.1 uses that (tr' − 1)q = tm − 1 in characteristic p and that tr' − 1 is separable, be‐
cause r' ≠ 0 in F. The proof of Lemma 6.2 uses the factorisation (u − 1)φ(u) = Πξ ∈ μr'(u − ξ)q in F[u] and the
Frobenius identity (tjtl − cjcl)q = tjqtlq − cjqclq = 0 in Rc. Neither uses the parity of p, of q or of r'. ∎
Blocks (this replaces Lemma 6.3, which uses that the cofactor is odd). Fix a colouring c ∈ μr'n+1, with c0 :=
(c1 ⋯ cn+1)−1 and the colour classes 𝒞ζ ⊆ {0, …, n+1}. The colours equal to their inverses are 1 and, when r' is
even (so p is odd), −1. The other colours split into pairs {ζ, ζ−1}; fix a set ℛ of representatives. The blocks of c
are 𝒞1, 𝒞−1 (empty if r' is odd) and the sets 𝒞ζ ∪ 𝒞ζ−1, ζ ∈ ℛ. A pair {a, b} has cacb = 1 if and only if it lies in 𝒞1,
or it lies in 𝒞−1, or it meets both 𝒞ζ and 𝒞ζ−1 for some ζ ∈ ℛ. So the matchings compatible with c are the choic‐
es of a perfect matching P1 of 𝒞1, a perfect matching P−1 of 𝒞−1 and a bijection σζ : 𝒞ζ → 𝒞ζ−1 for each ζ ∈ ℛ,
made independently; every such choice is an element of 𝒥 (this is where the full family is used), and c is
compatible if |𝒞1| and |𝒞−1| are even and |𝒞ζ| = |𝒞ζ−1| for every ζ. Group the tensor factors of Rc accordingly,
Rc = Rc,1 ⊗ Rc,−1 ⊗ ⊗ζ ∈ ℛ Rc,ζ, and define gP1, gP−1, gσζ and the ideals Ic,1, Ic,−1, Ic,ζ as in §6.3: the products of the
factors of (6.2) that belong to the pairs of one block, and the ideals they generate.
Lemma 9.5 (tensor decomposition). If c is not compatible, πc(I) = 0. If c is compatible, πc(I) = Ic,1 ⊗ Ic,−1 ⊗
⊗ζ ∈ ℛ Ic,ζ, and its dimension is the product of the dimensions of the factors.
Proof. As for Proposition 6.5: by (6.2) the image of a compatible ψ̄ J is a unit times a pure tensor along the
blocks, the choices in the blocks are independent, and Lemma 6.4 applies. ∎
Lemma 9.6 (the count factors). For a compatible colouring c let N1(c) be the number of tuples (wi)i ∈ 𝒞1 in μq
∖ {1} that split into inverse pairs, N−1(c) the number of tuples (wi)i ∈ 𝒞−1 in μq that split into inverse pairs, and
Nζ(c) := Nbal(|𝒞ζ|, q) (§6.6). Then
                                     Qk(m) = |Γ𝒥| = Σc N1(c)·N−1(c)·Πζ ∈ ℛ Nζ(c),
the sum over the compatible colourings. Moreover: N1(c) = (2k')!·[x2k'] I0(2x)(q−1)/2 if p is odd and N1(c) =
Nq−1(2k') if p = 2, where |𝒞1| = 2k'; N−1(c) = Nq(|𝒞−1|); and Nbal(a, q) = Nph(a − 1, q) for a ≥ 1.
Proof. Work with the complex roots of unity, as in Lemma 6.8, and fix an isomorphism of groups between the
complex r'-th roots of unity and those of F; it preserves 1, −1 and inversion, hence the blocks of a colouring
and their sizes. μm = μr' × μq, gi = ciwi for i = 0, …, n+1; gagb = 1 iff cacb = 1 and wawb = 1; and gi ≠ 1 iff (ci, wi)
≠ (1, 1). By Lemma 9.1, Γ𝒥 is in bijection with the N-tuples (g0, …, gn+1) in μm ∖ {1} that split into inverse
pairs, and the colours of such a tuple are (c0, c) with c0 = (c1 ⋯ cn+1)−1. A matching that splits the tuple is
compatible with c, so each of its pairs lies in one block; hence the tuple splits if and only if its restriction to
each block does. On 𝒞1: gi = wi, and the condition is that (wi) lies in μq ∖ {1} and splits into inverse pairs. On
𝒞−1: gi = −wi, which is never 1 because q is odd, and gigl = wiwl; the condition is that (wi) splits into inverse
pairs in μq. On 𝒞ζ ∪ 𝒞ζ−1: every gi ≠ 1, a splitting is a bijection σ : 𝒞ζ → 𝒞ζ−1 with wσ(i) = wi−1, and one exists if
and only if the multiset of the wi, i ∈ 𝒞ζ, equals the multiset of the wl−1, l ∈ 𝒞ζ−1: this is Nbal(|𝒞ζ|, q). The con‐
ditions concern disjoint sets of coordinates, so the count is the product. The closed forms: on μq ∖ {1} inver‐
sion has no fixed point if q is odd (Lemma 2.2) and one fixed point if q is even (Lemma 9.1); on μq, q odd, it
has the single fixed point 1, which gives Nq; and Nbal(a, q) = Nph(a − 1, q) is Lemma 7.2(iii). ∎
(In this count the index 0 is a coordinate like the others. On the side of the ideals it is not: the variable t0 does
not exist, and the block that contains 0 has one variable less. This is the difference between the balanced and
the phantom bipartite ideals, between the two cases of Lemma 6.6, and between the two forms of Lemma
8.12.)

9.4 The blocks
Lemma 9.7 (pair blocks). For every prime p | m and every ζ ∈ ℛ, with a := |𝒞ζ| = |𝒞ζ−1|: Ic,ζ ≅ Ibala if 0 ∉ 𝒞ζ ∪
𝒞ζ−1, and Ic,ζ ≅ Ipha−1 otherwise, with the box q = pv. In both cases dimF Ic,ζ ≥ Nζ(c).
Proof. Lemma 6.7 and its proof hold for every prime p and every r': they use only cs ≠ 1 for the indices s of the
block, and, in the case 0 ∈ 𝒞ζ, the equality of the ideals generated by (x − z)q−1 and by (z − x)q−1, which holds
for every q. The inequality is Theorem C for the box q = pv over F = F̄ p; its hypothesis holds for every prime p,
2 included, because C(q − 1, t) ≡ (−1)t (mod p). For p = 2 this is Theorem C at an even box. ∎
Lemma 9.8 (the block of colour 1 at an odd prime). If p is odd, dimF Ic,1 ≥ N1(c).
Proof. This is Lemma 6.6: §6.4 uses only (ti − 1)q = 0, q = pv and 2 ∈ F×. The number N1(c) of §6.4 is the one
of Lemma 9.6: if 0 ∉ 𝒞1 the two definitions coincide, and if 0 ∈ 𝒞1 a tuple (wi)i ∈ 𝒞1 that splits into inverse
pairs has product 1, so its entry w0 is the one that §6.4 computes from the others, and deleting it is a bijection
between the two sets of tuples. ∎
Lemma 9.9 (the block of colour −1). Let p be odd, r' even and |𝒞−1| = 2a. Then dimF Ic,−1 ≥ Nq(2a) =
N−1(c). More precisely, in suitable coordinates yi with Rc,−1 = F[yi : i ∈ 𝒞−1 ∖ {0}]/(yiq): if 0 ∈ 𝒞−1, Ic,−1 is the ide‐
al (DJ : J ∈ 𝒥) of Theorem O for the box r = q in 2a − 1 variables; if 0 ∉ 𝒞−1, it is the ideal ΣP DP·C2a of Lemma
8.12.
Proof. For i ∈ 𝒞−1 ∖ {0} put ui := −ti. In Rc, (ui − 1)q = −(ti + 1)q = 0, because q is odd. So Lemma 2.3 applies to
ui (it needs (u − 1)q = 0, q = pv and 2 ∈ F×): with yi := ui − ui−1, the factor of i is F[yi]/(yiq), and titl − 1 = uiul
− 1 = (unit)·(yi + yl). Moreover ti − 1 = −(ui + 1) = −(2 + (ui − 1)) is a unit. So in (6.2) a pair {i < l} ⊆ 𝒞−1 ∖
{0} contributes a unit times (yi + yl)q−1, and the pair {0, k0}, if 0 ∈ 𝒞−1, contributes a unit. In characteristic p,
(a + b)q−1 = Σu (−1)u au bq−1−u (proof of Lemma 2.4(i)), which is the polynomial D(a, b) of §8 for r = q.
Hence gP is a unit times the product of the D(yi, yl) over the pairs of P that avoid 0. If 0 ∈ 𝒞−1, the perfect
matchings of 𝒞−1 are the matchings J ∈ 𝒥 of dimension 2(a − 1), and Theorem O gives dim ≥ Nq(2a). If 0 ∉
𝒞−1, the ideal is V{(∅,0)} of level 2a, and Theorem 8.11 with Lemma 8.5 gives the same bound. (For a = 0 the
block is empty, the ideal is F and Nq(0) = 1.) ∎
Lemma 9.10 (the block of colour 1 at the prime 2). Let p = 2 and |𝒞1| = 2k'. Then dimF Ic,1 ≥ Nq−1(2k') =
N1(c).
Proof. Here q = 2v, Rc,1 = F[ti : i ∈ 𝒞1 ∖ {0}]/(tiq − 1), a group algebra, and for u = titl in it, (u − 1)q−1 = φq(u) :=
1 + u + ⋯ + uq−1 (in F[u], (u − 1)φq(u) = uq − 1 = (u − 1)q). For k' = 0 the block is empty and both sides are
1.
0 ∈ 𝒞1. By (6.2), gP = (unit)·(tk0 − 1)·Π (tl − 1)φq(titl), the product over the pairs {i < l} of P that avoid 0, where
k0 is the partner of 0. After the increasing relabelling of 𝒞1 ∖ {0} as {1, …, 2k' − 1}, these are exactly the gener‐
ators ψ̄ J of §2 for the degree q and the dimension 2(k' − 1). So Ic,1 is the ideal of Proposition 9.2 at (k' − 1, q)
over F, and that proposition gives dim ≥ Nq−1(2k').
0 ∉ 𝒞1. Now gP = Π{i<l} ∈ P (tl − 1)φq(titl), over the perfect matchings P of the 2k' ≥ 2 indices of 𝒞1; let ℳ be the
ideal they generate. Let i1 := min 𝒞1, let H' be the group on the other indices, and write each f ∈ Rc,1 as f = Σj
         j                                                                                                              0
∈ Z/q ti1 fj with fj ∈ F[H']. The map θ : ℳ → F[H'], f ↦ f0, is F[H']-linear. If {i1, l} ∈ P, the coefficient of ti1 in
φq(ti1tl) is 1, and no other factor of gP contains ti1; so θ(gP) = (tl − 1)·Π (tl' − 1)φq(ti'tl'), the product over the oth‐
er pairs: the generator of the previous case, with i1 in the role of 0. So θ(ℳ) contains the ideal of the previous
case in 2k' − 1 variables, and dim ℳ ≥ dim θ(ℳ) ≥ Nq−1(2k'). ∎
(Version 11 proved in addition that θ is injective on ℳ. No conclusion uses it: the equality block by block is
Corollary 9.12(i), which follows from the inequalities alone. The proof printed here is the one that was for‐
malized, §14.8.)

9.5 The theorem
Theorem 9.11 (even degrees). Let m ≥ 4 be even and k ≥ 1. Then dimFp Fp[G]/(ψ̄ J : J ∈ 𝒥) = m2k+1 − Qk(m)
for every prime p; the group R/(ψJ : J ∈ 𝒥) is free abelian of rank m2k+1 − Qk(m); and H2k(X; Z)/L(X) is torsion
free for the Fermat variety X of degree m and dimension 2k.
Proof. Let p be a prime dividing m, m = q·r' as in §9.3. By Lemma 9.4 (Lemma 6.1), Lemma 9.5, Lemmas 9.7–
9.10 and Lemma 9.6,
           dimF I = Σc dim πc(I) = Σc dim Ic,1·dim Ic,−1·Πζ dim Ic,ζ ≥ Σc N1(c)·N−1(c)·Πζ Nζ(c) = Qk(m),
the sums over the compatible colourings: the block of colour 1 is Lemma 9.8 if p is odd and Lemma 9.10 if p
= 2; the block of colour −1 (present only if p is odd) is Lemma 9.9; the pair blocks are Lemma 9.7. (If r' = 1,
that is, m = 2v, there is one colouring, and the inequality is Proposition 9.2.) With (9.1), dimF I = Qk(m), and
dimensions do not change from Fp to F. For p ∤ m the equality of the statement is Theorem 0(b). So R/(ψJ : J
∈ 𝒥), whose rank is m2k+1 − Qk(m) by Theorem 0(b) at p = 0, has no p-torsion for any prime p: it is free. No
step of this uses topology. Theorem 0(a) then gives the last assertion. ∎
With Corollary 7.7, this proves the Main Theorem and Main Theorem′ of §1.2 for every m ≥ 3.
Corollary 9.12. (i) In the proof of Theorem 9.11 every block has exactly the dimension of its count: dim Ic,1 =
N1(c), dim Ic,−1 = N−1(c) and dim Ic,ζ = Nζ(c) for every compatible colouring c. (ii) For q = 2v, v ≥ 1, and every a
≥ 0: dimF̄2 Ibala = Nbal(a, q) and dimF̄2 Ipha = Nph(a, q). So Corollary 7.8 holds for p = 2 as well.
Proof. (i) The sum of the products of the dimensions equals the sum of the products of the counts, each di‐
mension is at least its count, and every count of a compatible colouring is positive. (ii) For a = 0 see the proof
of Corollary 7.8. For a ≥ 1 take m := 3q, p = 2, r' = 3, n := 2a, and ζ a primitive cube root of unity. Colour a
coordinates by ζ, a by ζ−1 and one coordinate s by 1: then c0 = 1, 𝒞1 = {0, s}, and the pair block is Ibala. Colour
a + 1 coordinates by ζ and a by ζ−1: then c0 = ζ−1, 𝒞1 = ∅, and the only block is Ipha. Apply (i). ∎

9.6 Two examples
Example 9.13 (quartics). For m = 4 the only prime is 2, q = 4, and r = 3: the proof is Proposition 9.2 and
the elementary Theorem 8.15 over F2; no Pfaffian and no colouring is involved. At k = 1: B = F2[s1, s2,
s3]/(si4), the three leading forms are s1s2s3·(sa2 + sasb + sb2), and the ideal generated by the three polynomials
sa2 + sasb + sb2 in F2[s1, s2, s3]/(si3) has dimension 19 = N3(4) (§8.9). So rank L(X) = 20, the Picard number of
the Fermat quartic surface. In general, for the Fermat quartic of dimension 2k, L(X) is primitive of rank one
more than the central trinomial coefficient of 2k + 2: 20, 142, 1 108, 8 954, ….
Example 9.14 (k = 1, m = 6). Here N = 4, Q1(6) = 61, and both kinds of prime occur. At p = 3 (q = 3, r' = 2)
the colours are ±1, all 8 colourings are compatible, and there are no pair blocks. At p = 2 (q = 2, r' = 3) the
colours are the cube roots of unity, 19 of the 27 colourings are compatible, and every block of colour 1 con‐
tributes 1 (Remark 9.3(3)).
         p = 3: sizes (|𝒞1|, |𝒞−1|)   colourings × N1·N−1   p = 2: sizes (|𝒞1|, |𝒞ζ|, |𝒞ζ−1|)   colourings × N1·Nζ
                   (4, 0)                   1 × 6·1                     (4, 0, 0)                    1 × 1·1
                   (2, 2)                   6 × 2·3                     (2, 1, 1)                    12 × 1·2
                   (0, 4)                   1 × 1·19                    (0, 2, 2)                    6 × 1·6
                   total                6 + 36 + 19 = 61                 total                   1 + 24 + 36 = 61

At p = 3 the blocks of colour 1 are instances of Theorem B at q = 3 (Q1(3) = 6, Q0(3) = 2) and the blocks of
colour −1 are instances of Theorem O at r = 3 (N3(2) = 3, N3(4) = 19). At p = 2 the pair blocks are bipartite
ideals with the box 2 (Nbal(1, 2) = 2, Nbal(2, 2) = 6). (At k = 1 the theorem is Degtyarev's [De14]; the example
only shows the bookkeeping.)
Remarks 9.15. (1) What each degree uses. For m = 4: Theorem 8.15 over F2. For m ≡ 2 (mod 4): at the prime 2,
Theorem C at the box 2 and Remark 9.3(3); at the odd primes p, Theorems B and C at pv and Theorem O at r
= pv. For m = 6 and m = 12 every box that occurs is 2, 3 or 4, and the proof uses, of §8, only §8.8. The bor‐
dered Pfaffians are first needed at m = 8 (r = 7) and m = 10 (r = 5). (2) The list of boxes. Theorem 9.11 uses
Theorem O — or, in Lemma 9.9 when 0 ∉ 𝒞−1, Theorem 8.11 at the second root {(∅, 0)} — exactly at the box‐
es r = pv in characteristic p, for the odd primes p with pv ∥ m, and r = 2v − 1 in characteristic 2, if 2v ∥ m with
v ≥ 2. (3) Subfamilies. As in §6, the proof uses the product structure of the compatible matchings (Lemma
9.5) and the roots of Lemma 8.5 and Lemma 7.2, which need the full family 𝒥; and with one matching re‐
moved the dimension of the ideal of Theorem O drops, also over F2 (§14.9).


10. A different count: Theorem A
For a finite set L of variables over a field F and an odd q ≥ 1, put Iq(L) := (ej(L) : j odd) + (xq : x ∈ L) and
                                           Nq(n) := n!·[yn] ey I0(2y)(q−1)/2.
Theorem A. Let F be a field with char F ≠ 2, let q ≥ 1 be odd, and let |L| = n. Then dimF F[L]/Iq(L) = Nq(n).
The proof is in Appendix A. The lower bound degenerates the ideal of an explicit finite set of points. The up‐
per bound is an induction on n over a family of ideals Kμ(L) indexed by partitions μ; the ideals are built from
the coefficients of one generating function, and each inductive step shifts one index in that function. The
proof uses the characteristic only through 2 ∈ F×, and it uses no relation between q and char F.
Corollary 10.1. Let p be an odd prime and q = pv. For every k ≥ 0, Ak(q) := dim Fp[x0, …, x2k+1]/(E + (x0q, …,
x2k+1q)) = Pk(q), where Pk(q) is the number of points of Fq2k+2 at which every odd elementary symmetric polyno‐
mial vanishes, i.e. the number of Fq-points of ⋃J∈𝒥 V(IJ).
Corollary 10.2. For every odd q and every n, the finitely generated abelian group M := Z[x1, …, xn]/(ej : j odd;
xiq) has rank Nq(n), and its torsion subgroup is a 2-group.
Remarks 10.3. (1) In characteristic 2 the statement fails: at n = 4 the dimensions are 21, 65, 133, 225 for q = 3,
5, 7, 9, against Nq(4) = 19, 61, 127, 217. (2) For q = 3 and all k, Corollary 10.1 was known earlier through
Steinberg modules and tilting theory [Jan, II.4.16], [Mat, Theorem 1(1)], [DCP, Theorems 5.6–5.7]; for k ≤ 4
and all q = 3v it was known through case-by-case arguments in earlier notes of this project. Theorem A con‐
tains all of these, in every characteristic ≠ 2 and for every odd q. As a count, however, Theorem A is not
new: it follows from a theorem of Kostant in characteristic 0 and from a proposition of
Bezrukavnikov, Riche and Rider in odd characteristic (Remark 10.4). (3) Theorem A is not used
anywhere in the proof of the Main Theorem, and it does not imply it (§11).
Remark 10.4 (the centralizer of a regular unipotent element; as a count, Theorem A is not new).
Let q = 2h + 1 ≥ 3, so that h = (q−1)/2 as in §1.9. In this remark and in Remark 10.7 the letters G, T, W, K, ℓ
and u have the meaning that they have in the sources quoted, not that of §2 and §11; 𝒵 is the centralizer de‐
fined in (1).
(1) A dictionary. Let F be a field of characteristic ≠ 2, and let V := F[x]/(xq) with the bilinear form ⟨xa, xb⟩ :=
(−1)a if a + b = q − 1, and 0 otherwise. The form is nondegenerate, and it is symmetric because a + b is even.
Multiplication by x, which we call e, satisfies ⟨e u, v⟩ = −⟨u, e v⟩, and it is nilpotent with a single Jordan block.
An endomorphism of V that commutes with e is a polynomial f(e), and f(e) preserves the form exactly when
f(e)f(−e) = 1; the constant term of this identity gives f(0) = ±1, so the determinant of f(e) is f(0)q = f(0). So the
centralizer of e in SO(V) ≅ SO2h+1 is the commutative unipotent group
                                         𝒵 := { f(e) : f(e)f(−e) = 1, f(0) = 1 }.
For t ∈ F the Cayley transform gt := (1 + te)(1 − te)−1 lies in 𝒵, and for t ≠ 0 it has a single Jordan block: it is a
regular unipotent element of SO(V), and 𝒵 is its centralizer. The ring F[L]/(xq : x ∈ L) is V⊗n, and f(e) ∈ 𝒵 acts
on it as multiplication by Πx∈L f(x). In particular gt acts as E(t)/E(−t), where E(t) := Πx∈L(1 + tx) = Σj ej(L) tj.
Since E(t) − E(−t) = 2 Σj odd ej(L) tj and E(−t) is invertible, the elements E(t)/E(−t) − 1 lie in the ideal (ej(L) : j
odd), and when F is infinite they generate it (they are polynomials in t whose coefficients generate it). For a
general f(e) ∈ 𝒵 put φ := 1 + f; then φ(0) = 2 is a unit, f(x) = φ(x)/φ(−x), and Πx f(x) − 1 = (H − H−)/H− with
H := Πx φ(x) and H− := Πx φ(−x). Here H − H− is twice the sum of the homogeneous components of odd de‐
gree of the symmetric polynomial H, and a symmetric polynomial of odd degree lies in (ej(L) : j odd). Hence,
for F infinite,
                                   F[L]/Iq(L) = V⊗n / ⟨(g − 1)v : g ∈ 𝒵, v ∈ V⊗n⟩,
the coinvariants of the centralizer of a regular unipotent element on the n-th tensor power of the vector rep‐
resentation of SO2h+1. The hypothesis that F is infinite cannot be dropped: over F3 the coinvariants of the fi‐
nite group 𝒵 have dimension 9, 27 and 19 at (q, n) = (3, 3), (3, 4) and (5, 3), against 7, 19 and 13 for the ring
(§14.5). Over a finite field the statement holds for the centralizer as an algebraic group, that is, after extension
of scalars to an infinite field. Since V⊗n is isomorphic to its dual as a representation of 𝒵, the space of coin‐
variants has the dimension of the invariants (V⊗n)𝒵. In characteristic 0 the invariants of 𝒵 are those of its Lie
algebra, which has basis e, e3, …, e2h−1; ej acts as multiplication by the power sum pj(L), and (pj(L) : j odd) =
(ej(L) : j odd) by Newton's identities.
(2) Characteristic 0: Kostant's theorem. Let G be a connected semisimple group over C and W a finite-dimen‐
                                                                                    x
sional rational G-module whose weights lie in the root lattice. Then x ↦ dim W𝔤 is constant on the regular
elements of 𝔤 [Kos]; we quote the statement from [Gin, Proposition 4.2]. At a regular semisimple x the cen‐
                                           x
tralizer 𝔤x is a Cartan subalgebra, and W𝔤 is the zero weight space of W. The weights of V are 0, ±ε1, …, ±εh,
                                                      e
which span the root lattice of so2h+1. So dim (V⊗n)𝔤 is the dimension of the zero weight space of V⊗n: the
number of closed walks of length n on the lattice Zh with steps 0, ±εi, which is Nq(n). So in characteristic 0,
Theorem A follows from Kostant's theorem and (1).
(3) Odd characteristic: a proposition of Bezrukavnikov, Riche and Rider. Let K be an algebraically closed field
of characteristic ℓ, and let G be a connected reductive group over K with simply connected derived subgroup,
such that ℓ is good for G and X*(T)/ZR has no ℓ -torsion (T a maximal torus, R the roots). Then [BRR,
Proposition 2.12]: for every finite-dimensional G-module W which admits a good filtration and every regular
unipotent element u ∈ G, dim WZG(u) = dim WT. We read this statement and its proof in the arXiv version of 4
July 2024; the proof rests on the vanishing theorem and the good filtration of [KLT]. Take G = Spin2h+1 and ℓ
odd. Then ℓ is good for G (the only bad prime of type Bh, h ≥ 2, is 2, and type A1 has none), and X*(T)/ZR ≅
Z/2, so the hypotheses hold. The vector representation V is the Weyl module of highest weight ε1; it is simple
for ℓ ≠ 2, hence tilting ([BRR, proof of Lemma 2.13] for h ≥ 2; for h = 1, G = SL2 and V is the Weyl module of
highest weight 2), and a tensor product of modules with a good filtration has a good filtration [Jan, II.4.21],
[Mat]. The group scheme ZG(u) is smooth [BRR, Proposition 2.2], so its invariants are those of its group of K-
points. The centre of G acts trivially on V, and the image of ZG(u) in SO(V) is the centralizer of the image of
u: if g ∈ SO(V) commutes with it, a lift g̃ satisfies g̃ug̃ −1 = ±u, and −u is not unipotent. Finally (V⊗n)T is the
zero weight space. So for F = K, dim F[L]/Iq(L) = dim (V⊗n)𝒵 = Nq(n); the dimension does not change under
extension of scalars, so the same holds over every field of odd characteristic. So in odd characteristic, too,
Theorem A follows from the literature, through (1): from [BRR, Proposition 2.12]. Corollary 10.2
then follows as in Appendix A.8. Version 9 of this paper said: «we do not know a version of Kostant's theo‐
rem in positive characteristic that gives this». There is one; we had not read it. The count of Theorem A
should be credited to Kostant in characteristic 0 and to [BRR] in odd characteristic. (Logically, the case of
characteristic 0 also follows from the case of one odd prime p: the ring over Q is M ⊗ Q for the abelian group
M of Corollary 10.2, so its dimension is at most that of M ⊗ Fp, and Proposition A.5 gives the lower bound.
We keep the reference to Kostant, whose theorem is the origin of the statement.) A related paper, [Ric], ex‐
tends Kostant's results on the centralizers of regular elements to positive characteristic and to integral coeffi‐
cients; it describes the centralizer group scheme, and we did not find the count in it (we read its introduction
and §2.2, and searched its source). We have not found the dictionary (1), or the statement of Theorem A for
this ring, in the literature. For q = 3 the statement is the one recalled in Remark 10.3(2).
(4) What Appendix A contributes. The proof of Appendix A is a second, independent proof of the count. It is
elementary and self-contained: it uses no algebraic groups, no good filtrations and no Frobenius splitting,
only 2 ∈ F×. It also gives slightly more than the count: to every partition μ with at most h parts, read as the
dominant weight Σ μiεi, it attaches an explicit ideal Kμ(L), with K∅(L) = Iq(L), such that dim F[L]/Kμ(L) is the
multiplicity Nμ(n) of the weight μ in V⊗n (Theorem A.15 and Corollary A.17; computed independently at 178
cells, §14.5), and each row of Kμ(L ∪ z) is the ideal of the corresponding neighbouring weight (Theorem A.12
and Corollary A.17). The lower bound (Proposition A.5) is the classical method of degenerating the ideal of a
finite set of points (the «orbit harmonics» of [GP]).
(5) Computed, not claimed. In the 27 cells (q, n) that we computed (3 ≤ q ≤ 13, n ≤ 9), the Hilbert series of
F[L]/Iq(L) is Σλ cλ·tmn m0λ(t−1), where λ runs over the irreducible constituents Vλ of V⊗n in characteristic 0, cλ
is the multiplicity, and m0λ is Lusztig's t-analogue of the zero weight multiplicity [Lus]; in 56 cases the same
holds for the graded character of the symmetric group (pairs of a cell with q ∈ {3, 5}, n ≤ 6 and a cycle type),
with the multiplicity spaces in place of the cλ. In characteristic 0 this is what the theory of generalized expo‐
nents and of the Brylinski–Kostant filtration predicts [Bry]; for tilting modules in good characteristic see
[Liu]. Of these sources we have read only the introduction of [Liu], and we record the statement as a compu‐
tation (§14.5).
(6) Even q. For even q the form of (1) is alternating, and the same dictionary holds with Sp(V) ≅ Spq: the cen‐
tralizer of the regular unipotent element g1 is {±1} × 𝒵, and −1 acts on V⊗n as (−1)n. The group Spq is simply
connected, every odd prime is good for it, X*(T)/ZR ≅ Z/2, and V is a simple Weyl module in every character‐
istic. So for even n and every field of characteristic ≠ 2, [Kos] and [BRR, Proposition 2.12] give
                             dim F[x1, …, xn]/(e1, e3, …; x1q, …, xnq) = n!·[yn] I0(2y)q/2,
the number of closed walks of length n on the lattice Zq/2 with steps ±εi. (In characteristic 0 the reference to
[Kos] can again be avoided: the dimension over Q is at most the dimension over F3, and Proposition 10.5(iii)
with Corollary 10.6(i), applied to the odd number q + 1, gives the lower bound.) Version 9 listed this as an
open problem; for even n it is a consequence of the literature. For odd n the zero weight space is 0 and the
proposition says nothing about the invariants of 𝒵 alone. In 8 cells (q, n) with q ∈ {2, 4, 6, 8}, of both parities
of n, the dimension in characteristics 3, 5 and 7 equals the dimension in characteristic 0, and for odd n it is
the number of such walks from 0 to ε1; in characteristic 2 it is larger in all 8 cells. The case of odd n is not
proved (§15).
Proposition 10.5 (the invariant copairing). Let F be a field, r ≥ 2, N = 2s ≥ 2, and Cr := F[x1, …, xN]/(x1r,
…, xNr). Put Dr(a, b) := Σu=0r−1 (−1)u au br−1−u, and for a perfect matching J of {1, …, N} put Dr,J := Π{a<b} ∈ J
Dr(xa, xb) and IJ := (xa + xb : {a,b} ∈ J). Then:
      (i) (xa + xb)·Dr(xa, xb) = 0 in Cr, and annCr(Dr,J) = IJ Cr;
      (ii) dimF ΣJ Dr,J Cr = dimF F[x1, …, xN]/⋂J (IJ + (xir));
      (iii) ej · Dr,J = 0 for every odd j. So ΣJ Dr,J Cr ⊆ annCr(ej : j odd), and dimF ΣJ Dr,J Cr ≤ dimF F[x1, …,
      xN]/((ej : j odd) + (xir)).
Proof. (i) The product (a + b)·Σu (−1)u au br−1−u telescopes to br + (−1)r−1ar, which is 0 in the box. In
F[a,b]/(ar, br), multiplication by Dr(a,b) therefore factors through the quotient by (a + b), which is F[b]/(br),
of dimension r. The elements Dr(a,b)·bi, 0 ≤ i ≤ r−1, are non-zero (the term u = r − 1 survives) and homoge‐
neous of different degrees, so the image has dimension at least r. Hence the annihilator of Dr(a,b) is exactly
(a + b). The ring Cr is the tensor product, over the pairs of J, of such rings, and Dr,J is the tensor product of
the elements Dr; the kernel of a tensor product of linear maps is the sum of the kernels in each factor, which
gives ann(Dr,J) = IJ Cr. (ii) Let ⟨f, g⟩ be the coefficient of Πi xir−1 in fg; as in the proof of Proposition 2.6, the
pairing is non-degenerate, dim ann(I) = dim Cr − dim I for every ideal I, and the annihilator of a sum of
ideals is the intersection of their annihilators. So dim ΣJ Dr,JCr = dim Cr − dim ⋂J IJ Cr. (iii) By (i), xa Dr,J =
−xb Dr,J for each pair, so Πi(1 + xi t)·Dr,J = Π{a<b}∈J(1 − xb2 t2)·Dr,J, which is even in t. The last inequality is dim
ann(I) = dim Cr − dim I for I = (ej : j odd)Cr. ∎
Corollary 10.6 (Theorem B at 2k + 2 variables; both ends of the sandwich). Let q ≥ 3 be odd, k ≥ 0, N =
2k + 2, and r = q − 1.
      (i) For every field F, dimF ΣJ Dq−1,J Cq−1 = Qk(q).
      (ii) Modulo [BRR, Proposition 2.12]: if char F ≠ 2, then ΣJ Dq−1,J Cq−1 = annCq−1(ej : j odd), ⋂J (IJ +
      (xiq−1)) = (ej : j odd) + (xiq−1), and dimF F[x1, …, xN]/((ej : j odd) + (xiq−1)) = Qk(q). So both inequalities
      of Proposition 11.1 are equalities.
Proof. (i) Write the variables as x0, y1, …, y2k+1, and let C be the ring of Theorem B in the yi. Every element f of
ℳ := ΣJ Dq−1,JCq−1 is killed by e1 = x0 + σ, σ := Σi yi, by Proposition 10.5(iii). Write f = Σu=0r−1 x0u fu with fu ∈
C; comparing the coefficients of x0u in x0 f = −σf gives fu−1 = −σ fu for u ≥ 1. So f is determined by fr−1, and
the linear map θ : ℳ → C, f ↦ fr−1, is injective. If k0 is the partner of 0 in J, the coefficient of x0r−1 in Dq−1(x0,
yk0) is (−1)r−1, so θ(Dq−1,J·g) = ±DJ·g for g ∈ C, with DJ as in §2.3. Moreover x0·Dq−1,J = −yk0Dq−1,J, so ℳ = ΣJ
Dq−1,JC. Hence θ(ℳ) = (DJ : J ∈ 𝒥)C, and dim ℳ = Qk(q) by Theorem B. (ii) By Proposition 10.5(iii) and (i),
ℳ is a subspace of ann(ej : j odd), of dimension Qk(q); so dim F[x]/((ej : j odd) + (xiq−1)) ≥ Qk(q) over every
field. If char F is odd, this dimension is N!·[yN] I0(2y)(q−1)/2 = Qk(q) by Remark 10.4(6), which rests on [BRR,
Proposition 2.12]. If char F = 0, the ring is obtained by extension of scalars from the finitely generated
abelian group Z[x]/((ej : j odd) + (xiq−1)), so its dimension is the rank of this group, which is at most the di‐
mension of its reduction modulo 3, that is, Qk(q); so [Kos] is not needed. In both cases ℳ and ann(ej : j odd)
have the same dimension, so they are equal. Taking annihilators, ⋂J IJ Cq−1 = (ej : j odd)Cq−1. ∎
Remark 10.7 (what this says). (1) Invariant tensors. Let Vr := F[x]/(xr) with the form ⟨xa, xb⟩ = (−1)a [a + b = r
− 1], which is symmetric for odd r and alternating for even r. Then Dr(a, b) = Σu xu ⊗ (−1)u xr−1−u is the ele‐
ment of Vr ⊗ Vr dual to the form, so every Dr,J is a tensor of Vr⊗N invariant under the whole group of isome‐
tries (Spr for even r, Or for odd r). The ring Cr acts on Vr⊗N through the N commuting copies of e, one in each
factor. In these terms, Corollary 10.6 reads: Theorem B says that the invariant tensors Dq−1,J of Spq−1
generate, under the commuting copies of a regular nilpotent element, a space of the dimension of
the zero weight space of V⊗(2k+2), over every field; in characteristic ≠ 2, and modulo [BRR,
Proposition 2.12], that space is the whole space of invariants of the centralizer of a regular unipo‐
tent element (of the centralizer as an algebraic group; equivalently, over an infinite field). By Proposition
2.5 and Theorem 0, for degree pv and k ≥ 1, Conjecture 1.2 of [DS] is equivalent to the first half of this state‐
ment (the dimension count) over Fp, for Sppv−1. We did not find this formulation in the literature, and we do
not know whether Theorem B in odd characteristic can be derived from the theory of modules with a good
filtration.
(2) Characteristic 0. There the equality ΣJ Dr,JCr = annCr(ej : j odd) can be derived from classical invariant the‐
ory, for both parities of r and even N. We give the argument as a sketch; we have not read its sources in the
original, and nothing in this paper uses it. Let G be SOr for odd r and Spr for even r, with Lie algebra 𝔤, and
let W := Vr⊗N. By Kostant's theorem the evaluation at a regular nilpotent e maps the G-equivariant polynomi‐
                                         e
al maps 𝔤 → W onto the invariants WG of the centralizer of e in G [Kos]. These are the invariants of 𝔤e: for
SOr the group Ge is connected, and for Spr it is the product of {±1} and a connected group, and −1 acts trivial‐
ly on W because N is even. By the first fundamental theorem, the equivariant maps 𝔤 → W of degree d are
spanned by the complete contractions of Vr⊗N ⊗ 𝔤⊗d with the form (for odd r, Or = {±1} × SOr, and −1 acts
trivially on this tensor power, whose degree N + 2d is even; so the invariants of SOr in it are those of Or).
Evaluated at e, a contraction is a product, over the pairs {a, b} of some matching, of elements xaj·Dr(xa, xb),
times traces of powers of e, which vanish. Over the rationals the dimension of ΣJ Dr,JCr is therefore that of
the zero weight space; for even r this agrees with Theorem B over Q.
(3) The odd box. For odd r the corresponding statement is Theorem O, proved in §8: dimF ΣJ Dr,J Cr = Nr(N)
over every field (Corollary 8.13). Version 10 of this paper stated it as an open problem. With Theorem A it
gives the equality of the two ideals in characteristic ≠ 2 (Corollary 10.8). In characteristic 2 the two ideals dif‐
fer: their colengths are 19 and 21 at (r, N) = (3, 4). The statement cannot follow from Theorem A by an argu‐
ment that is valid for subfamilies of matchings: for 14 of the 15 matchings at r = 3, k = 2, over F3, the sum
count is 141, the number of points, and the intersection count is 140 (computed in the earlier notes of this
project [Rep]; recomputed in §14.9).
Corollary 10.8 (the odd box; both ends of the sandwich). Let r ≥ 3 be odd, k ≥ 0, N = 2k + 2, and let F be
a field of characteristic ≠ 2. Then, in the notation of Proposition 10.5,
ΣJ Dr,J Cr = annCr(ej : j odd) and ⋂J (IJ + (xir)) = (ej : j odd) + (xir),
and both quotients F[x1, …, xN]/⋂J (IJ + (xir)) and F[x1, …, xN]/((ej : j odd) + (xir)) have dimension Nr(N). In
particular, for an odd prime p and q = pv: dim Fp[x0, …, x2k+1]/⋂J ∈ 𝒥 (IJ + (xiq)) = Pk(q) = Ak(q).
Proof. By Proposition 10.5(iii), ΣJ Dr,J Cr ⊆ annCr(ej : j odd), and the dimension of the right-hand side is dim
F[x]/((ej : j odd) + (xir)) = Nr(N) by Theorem A. By Corollary 8.13 the left-hand side has dimension Nr(N) too;
so they are equal. Take annihilators in Cr, with the pairing of the proof of Proposition 10.5(ii), for which dim
ann(I) = dim Cr − dim I, so that ann(ann(I)) = I for every ideal I: by Proposition 10.5(i), ann(ΣJ Dr,J Cr) = ⋂J
IJ Cr, and ann(ann((ej : j odd)Cr)) = (ej : j odd)Cr. The last sentence is the case F = Fp, r = q, with Corollary
10.1. ∎
Unlike Corollary 10.6(ii), this does not depend on [BRR]: Theorem A is proved in Appendix A. In the lan‐
guage of Remarks 10.4 and 10.7(1): for odd r and char F ≠ 2, the tensors Dr,J, which are invariant under the
orthogonal group of the form on Vr, generate, under the commuting copies of a regular nilpotent element,
the whole space of invariants of the centralizer of a regular unipotent element of SOr in Vr⊗N (of the central‐
izer as an algebraic group; equivalently, over an infinite field). And the two counts of §1.6 and §11.1, the sum
count Ak(q) and the intersection count at the odd box, do coincide for the full family of matchings; they are
both different from Conjecture 1.2 at the degree q, which lives at the even box q − 1 (§11), and they are the
count of Conjecture 1.2 at the even degree q + 1 (Lemma 9.1).



11. Why the full family is needed

11.1 Two counts
Let p be an odd prime, q = pv, S := Fp[x0, …, x2k+1], N = 2k+2 and IJ := (xa + xb : {a,b} ∈ J). The ideal E vanish‐
es on ⋃J V(IJ): a point lies there iff its multiset of coordinates is closed under negation iff Πi(1 + xi t) is even
in t (unique factorisation in F̄ p[t]). By Proposition 2.6, (S) says
                                     bk(q−1) := dim S/⋂J∈𝒥(IJ + (xiq−1)) = Qk(q),
an intersection at the even box q − 1, counting the points of the union of the matching spaces on the zero-
free grid TN. Corollary 10.1 says dim S/(E + (xiq)) = Pk(q), a sum at the odd box q, counting points on the
grid with 0.
Proposition 11.1 (the sandwich). bk(q−1) ≤ Qk(q) ≤ dim S/(E + (xiq−1)).
Proof. Dimensions do not change under extension of scalars, so we may work over Fq. Let Γ ⊆ TN be the set of
zero-free points of ⋃J V(IJ); |Γ| = Qk(q) by Lemma 2.2, and S/I(Γ) has dimension |Γ|. We use Lemma 5.8′
(with N variables). The ideal I'' := E + (xiq−1 − 1) is contained in I(Γ), and gr(I'') ⊇ E + (xiq−1) because E is ho‐
mogeneous; so dim S/(E + (xiq−1)) ≥ dim S/gr(I'') = dim S/I'' ≥ |Γ|, the right-hand inequality. For each J, the
ideal of the finite set V(IJ) ∩ TN is IJ + (xiq−1 − 1): modulo IJ one free variable per pair remains, and xq−1 − 1 is
separable with roots exactly T. Its gr contains IJ + (xiq−1), and is equal to it, since both quotients have dimen‐
sion (q−1)k+1. Finally I(Γ) = ⋂J I(V(IJ) ∩ TN), and gr of an intersection is contained in the intersection of the
gr's, so gr(I(Γ)) ⊆ ⋂J (IJ + (xiq−1)), which gives the left-hand inequality. ∎
So the Main Theorem is the left equality. It is not a consequence of Corollary 10.1, which is an equality of
the right-hand kind at a different box. The right-hand inequality is in fact an equality too: it is the analogue
of Theorem A at the even box q − 1, which follows from [BRR, Proposition 2.12] (Remark 10.4(6)). So the
two ideals ⋂J (IJ + (xiq−1)) and E + (xiq−1) coincide (Corollary 10.6(ii)). This does not make the Main
Theorem a consequence of a count of the kind of Theorem A: the count gives the right-hand equality only,
and the equality of the two ideals is what the Main Theorem adds to it.

11.2 A counterexample for subfamilies
Proposition 2.1 and Proposition 2.5 hold for every non-empty K ⊆ 𝒥. So, for a subfamily, LK(X) is primitive iff
dim(DJ : J ∈ K)C = |ΓK|.
Fact 11.2 (computed; not used in any proof). Let q = 9, k = 2, and let K := 𝒥 ∖ {[[0,1],[2,3],[4,5]], [[0,2],
[1,5],[3,4]]} (13 of the 15 matchings). Then dim(DJ : J ∈ K)C = 4730 < 4736 = |ΓK|. So H4(X)/LK(X) has 3-tor‐
sion for the Fermat fourfold of degree 9. For the same K, the sum-type count holds: with EK := ⋂J∈K IJ, dim
S/(EK + (xi9)) = 7089, which is the number of F9-points of ⋃J∈K V(IJ).
The value 4730 was obtained by four independent routes (§14): the literal form of [DS] in the ring F3[G] (dim
F3[G]/(ψ̄ J : J ∈ K) = 54 319 = 95 − 4730, against 95 − |ΓK| = 54 313), the y-form of §2, the intersection form of
Proposition 2.6, and an engine written separately (a kernel basis D·yai per pair, exact rank modulo 3 degree by
degree). |ΓK| = 4736 was obtained by direct enumeration.
At q = 3, k = 2, the analogue of (S) fails for 800 of the 32 767 non-empty subfamilies (worst deficit 2, e.g. 18 <
20), while every subfamily satisfies ≤. So 3-torsion for subfamilies occurs already for the cubic fourfold.
Consequences.
      Any argument that proves the analogue of (S) for every subfamily proves a false statement. A proof of
      the Main Theorem must use a property of the full family 𝒥; in §5 this property enters at the root (Lem‐
      ma 5.2), and in §6–§7 through the product structure of the compatible matchings (Proposition 6.5) and
      the roots of Lemma 7.2.
      A = P cannot imply the Main Theorem through any argument that is valid for subfamilies.
      This is the natural first guess, and it is the error described in §1.8.

12. The integral Hodge conjecture
In this section k ≥ 1, n = 2k, m ≥ 3, and X ⊂ Pn+1 is the Fermat variety of degree m.

12.1 Classical facts
      (H1) Integral cohomology. H*(X; Z) is torsion free and vanishes in odd degrees. By the Lefschetz hy‐
      perplane theorem [Mil, §7], H2j(X; Z) = Z·hj for 2j < n, where h is the hyperplane class, and H2l(X; Z) →
      H2l(Pn+1; Z) ≅ Z is an isomorphism for 2l < n.
      (H2) Hodge classes. Through Poincaré duality we call a class in Hn(X; Z) a Hodge class if its dual lies
      in Hk,k(X), and write Hdg(X) ⊆ Hn(X; Z) for the group of Hodge classes. It is the set of integral classes
      on which every class of Fk+1Hn(X, C) integrates to zero, so it is a primitive subgroup. The class of an al‐
      gebraic subvariety of dimension k is a Hodge class, so L(X) ⊆ Hdg(X). The class PX of Appendix B, Step
      3 (the dual of hk) is a Hodge class, and Hn(X; Q) = Hnprim(X; Q) ⊕ Q·hk.
      (H3) Characters [SK], [Shi79], [Ran, Proposition 1.7]. Let 𝔄 be the set of α = (a0, …, an+1) ∈ (Z/m
      ∖ {0})n+2 with a0 + ⋯ + an+1 = 0, and for α ∈ 𝔄 put |α| := Σi ⟨ai⟩/m, where ⟨a⟩ ∈ {1, …, m−1} is the rep‐
      resentative of a. Under the action of μmn+2 on the coordinates, Hnprim(X; C) = ⊕α∈𝔄 V(α) with every
      V(α) of dimension one, and (Hk,k(X) ∩ Hnprim(X; Q)) ⊗ C = ⊕α∈𝔅 V(α), where 𝔅 := {α ∈ 𝔄 : |tα| = k + 1
      for every t ∈ (Z/m)×} is the set of Hodge characters. So rank Hdg(X) = |𝔅| + 1. An element α ∈ 𝔄 is of
      pair type if the multiset {a0, …, an+1} splits into pairs {a, −a}; we write 𝔇 ⊆ 𝔄 for the set of these.
      (H4) Prime degree [Ran, Proposition 1.8(i)], based on [KO]. If m is prime, every Hodge character
      is of pair type. We include a short proof, so that (H4) does not depend on [Ran] or [KO].
      (H4′) Degree 4. If m = 4, every Hodge character is of pair type. The proof is three lines, given below;
      it is also the case m = 4 of (H5).
      (H5) Aoki's theorem [Aok, Theorem A, p. 24]. 𝔅 = 𝔇 if and only if m is a prime or 4, or every prime
      divisor of m is greater than n + 2. (Aoki's sets 𝔄nm, 𝔅nm, 𝔇nm [Aok, §0] are the sets 𝔄, 𝔅, 𝔇 above, with
      ai ∈ {1, …, m−1} and Σ ai ≡ 0 (mod m); the proof of his Theorem A is in [Aok, §7], pp. 41–43, and the
      erratum [Aok] concerns only his Theorem B.) For odd m and even n, «greater than n + 2» means «at
      least 2k + 3». For even m and n ≥ 2, the conditions hold only for m = 4.
Proof of (H4). Let m = p and α ∈ 𝔅, and for c ∈ Fp× let N(c) be the number of i with ai = c. Then |tα| = Σc N(c)
⟨tc⟩/p = k + 1 for every t ∈ Fp×. Let χ be an odd character of Fp×, that is, χ(−1) = −1. Multiply by χ(t) and sum
over t. The constant contributes 0, and the substitution u = tc gives B1,χ · Σc N(c)χ̄(c) = 0, where B1,χ = (1/p)
Σu=1p−1 χ(u)u. An odd character is non-principal, hence primitive with conductor p since p is prime; so B1,χ ≠
0, by the classical formula L(1, χ̄) = πi τ(χ̄) B1,χ/p together with L(1, χ̄) ≠ 0 [Was, Theorem 4.9]. Hence Σc
N(c)χ̄(c) = 0 for every odd χ. The function f(c) = N(c) − N(−c) is odd, so Σc f(c)χ̄(c) equals 2Σc N(c)χ̄(c) = 0 for
odd χ, and vanishes trivially for even χ. By Fourier inversion on Fp×, f = 0. Since p is odd, c ≠ −c, and the en‐
tries split into pairs {c, −c}. ∎ (We also checked numerically that B1,χ ≠ 0 for all 2 090 odd characters of the
odd primes p < 200.)
Proof of (H4′). Let m = 4, α ∈ 𝔅, and let N(c) be the number of i with ai = c, for c = 1, 2, 3. Then N(1) + N(2)
+ N(3) = 2k + 2 and |α| = (N(1) + 2N(2) + 3N(3))/4 = k + 1. Subtracting twice the first equation from four
times the second, N(3) − N(1) = 0. Then N(2) = 2k + 2 − 2N(1) is even. So the entries split into pairs {1, 3}
and {2, 2}. ∎

12.2 Pair-type characters
Lemma 12.1. (i) Every pair-type α ∈ 𝔄 is a Hodge character: 𝔇 ⊆ 𝔅. (ii) |𝔇| = Qk(m).
Proof. (i) For t ∈ (Z/m)× and a ≠ 0, ta ≠ 0 and ⟨ta⟩ + ⟨−ta⟩ = m. Summing over the k + 1 pairs, |tα| = k + 1.
(ii) Identify Z/m with μm by a ↦ e2πia/m. The pair-type elements of 𝔄 become the N-tuples in μm ∖ {1} that
split into pairs of inverse entries; the condition Σ ai = 0 is then automatic. By Lemma 2.2 for odd m and
Lemma 9.1 for even m, there are Qk(m) of them. ∎

12.3 Proof of Corollary H
By (H3), (H2), Theorem 0(c) and Lemmas 2.2, 9.1 and 12.1, rank Hdg(X) = |𝔅| + 1 and rank L(X) = |Γ𝒥| + 1 =
|𝔇| + 1, with L(X) ⊆ Hdg(X) and 𝔇 ⊆ 𝔅.
(ii). Hdg(X)/L(X) is a subgroup of Hn(X; Z)/L(X), which is finitely generated and torsion free by the Main
Theorem; so Hdg(X)/L(X) is free, of rank |𝔅| − |𝔇| = |𝔅| − Qk(m). It is zero iff 𝔅 = 𝔇, which by (H5) happens
iff m is prime, or m = 4, or every prime factor of m is at least 2k + 3. (For prime m and for m = 4, (H4) and
(H4′) give 𝔅 = 𝔇 without appeal to [Aok].)
(i). Under the hypothesis of (i), Hdg(X) = L(X) by (ii). (Only the primitivity of L(X) is used here; that Hdg(X)
is primitive is not needed.) For the other degrees, by (H1) (the Lefschetz hyperplane theorem) and Poincaré
duality: odd degrees vanish; for 2j < n, H2j(X; Z) is generated by hj, the class of the intersection of X with j
general hyperplanes; for 2j > n, H2j(X; Z) ≅ H2l(X; Z) ≅ Z with l = n − j < k, and it is generated by the class of
any linear subspace Pl ⊆ X, whose image in H2l(Pn+1; Z) is a generator. Such subspaces exist, since X contains
the k-spaces LJ,β. So every integral Hodge class of X, in every degree, is the class of an algebraic cycle with in‐
teger coefficients. ∎
Remark 12.2. (1) Under the hypothesis of (i), Corollary H says more than the integral Hodge conjecture for
X: the integral Hodge classes of middle degree are generated by the (2k+1)!!·mk+1 standard k-spaces alone.
(2) In the same degrees, the elementary divisors and the discriminant of the lattice of integral Hodge classes
of middle degree, computed in [AMV] for small (n, m), are those of the lattice generated by the standard spa‐
ces. For k = 1 and m = p ≥ 5 prime, closed forms are given by the author's Watermark and Double Ladder
theorems [WDL], which are cited and not proved here (both are proved for every such prime and verified in
                                                                2                                      2
Lean 4 in [WDL]; they have not been refereed): |disc| = p3(p−3) , and the discriminant group is (Z/p)3p −24p+59
× (Z/p2)3p−16 for p ≥ 7 and (Z/5)10 × Z/25 for p = 5. They agree with the surface rows m = 5, 7, 11, 13 of
[AMV, Table 1]. For k ≥ 2 and m = p prime, the sequel note [WED] gives a formula for |disc Hdg(X)| through
the Hilbert function of the ring of Theorem A at the even box p − 1, modulo [BRR, Proposition 2.12] (with‐
out it, as a lower bound); the elementary divisors are open (§15).
Remark 12.3 (the degrees excluded by (i)). When m is neither prime nor 4 and has a prime factor p ≤ 2k
+ 1, Corollary H(ii) says that L(X) is a primitive sublattice of Hdg(X) of corank |𝔅| − Qk(m) > 0, so the stan‐
dard linear cycles do not generate the integral Hodge classes, and other algebraic cycles would be needed (for
k = 1 they exist, by the Lefschetz (1,1) theorem); we do not claim that any particular construction supplies
them. Counting 𝔅 directly (§14), |𝔅| − Qk(m) is 48, 2 880 and 152 880 for m = 9 and k = 1, 2, 3; it is 192 for (k,
m) = (1, 27), 624 for (1, 81), 288 for (1, 15), 432 for (1, 21), 528 for (1, 45), 2 880 for (2, 25), 4 320 for (2, 35) and
45 360 for (2, 15); and it is 0 for (k, m) = (1, 25), (1, 35), (1, 49), (1, 55), (1, 77), (1, 121), as (H5) predicts. For
even m: |𝔅| − Qk(m) is 24, 750 and 19 432 for m = 6 and k = 1, 2, 3; 48, 2 580 and 116 592 for m = 8; 144 and
12 120 for m = 10 and k = 1, 2; 312 and 37 890 for m = 12; and it is 0 for m = 4 and k ≤ 5, as (H4′) says. (At k
= 1 the ranks |𝔅| + 1 are 20, 86, 176, 362, 644 for m = 4, 6, 8, 10, 12, the Picard numbers of these Fermat sur‐
faces.) For m = 9 the count has a closed form.
Lemma 12.4 (m = 9). For m = 9 and every k ≥ 1, |𝔅| = C(2k+2, k+1)3. Hence Hdg(X)/L(X) is free of rank
C(2k+2, k+1)3 − Qk(9) (48, 2 880, 152 880, 8 064 000 for k = 1, 2, 3, 4).
Proof. Since (Z/9)× = {±1, ±2, ±4} and ⟨−x⟩ = 9 − ⟨x⟩, the condition |tα| = k + 1 for t implies it for −t, and α ∈
𝔄 is a Hodge character iff Σi δ(ai) = 0, where δ(a) := (2⟨a⟩ − 9, 2⟨2a⟩ − 9, 2⟨4a⟩ − 9) ∈ Z3 (note Σi 9 = 9N =
18(k + 1)). Let s(a) ∈ {±1}3 be the vector of signs of δ(a). Then δ(1) = (−7, −5, −1), δ(2) = (−5, −1, 7), δ(4) =
(−1, 7, 5), δ(3) = (−3, 3, −3), and δ(−a) = −δ(a); so a ↦ s(a) is a bijection from Z/9 ∖ {0} onto {±1}3, and δ(a)
= M·s(a) for the matrix M with rows (4, 2, 1), (−1, 4, 2), (−2, −1, 4) (check on a = 1, 2, 4, 3, then use δ(−a) =
−δ(a)). As det M = 81 ≠ 0, Σi δ(ai) = 0 iff Σi s(ai) = 0 in Z3, i.e. iff each of the three coordinates of the sign vec‐
tors is balanced over the N entries. The condition Σ ai = 0 follows from Σ⟨ai⟩ = 9(k + 1). So |𝔅| is the number
of N-tuples of sign vectors with three balanced coordinates, C(N, N/2)3. ∎
(The direct counts 216, 8 000, 343 000 at k = 1, 2, 3 suggested the lemma; the direct count at k = 4, 16 003 008
= 2523, agrees with it, §14.)

13. Partial Fermat varieties
In this section m ≥ 3 and 0 ≤ s ≤ d. We prove Corollary W of §1.4. For d = 0 both statements are trivial (X and
W0 are sets of m points), so let d ≥ 1.
Proof of Corollary W(ii). Let X be the Fermat variety of dimension 2d, R = Z[t1, …, t2d+1]/(tim − 1), and K :=
𝒥(s, d). A matching J ∈ K is J = J' ⊔ {{2i, 2i+1} : s < i ≤ d} with J' a matching of {0, …, 2s+1}, and J' ↦ J is a bi‐
jection from 𝒥 in dimension 2s onto K. By §2,
                                    ψJ = ψJ' · Πi=s+1d gi, gi := (t2i+1 − 1)·φ(t2it2i+1),
where ψJ' is the generator of [DS] in dimension 2s, a polynomial in t1, …, t2s+1. Let p be a prime and F := Fp.
The ring R ⊗ F is the tensor product of F[t1, …, t2s+1]/(tim − 1) and the rings F[t2i, t2i+1]/(t2im − 1, t2i+1m − 1), i
> s. By Lemma 6.4 (which holds over any field), the ideal generated by the ψ̄ J, J ∈ K, is I2s ⊗ ⊗i>s (ḡi), where
I2s is the ideal of Conjecture 1.2 in dimension 2s, and its dimension is the product of the dimensions. In F[t2i,
t2i+1]/(tm − 1), put u := t2it2i+1; the ring is F[u, t2i+1]/(um − 1, t2i+1m − 1), and ḡi = (t2i+1 − 1)·φ(u). The ideal
(φ(u)) of F[u]/(um − 1) is the line spanned by φ(u), since uφ(u) = φ(u); the ideal (t − 1) of F[t]/(tm − 1) has di‐
mension m − 1. By Lemma 6.4 again, dim(ḡi) = m − 1, over every field. Hence
                                      dimFp(ψ̄ J : J ∈ K) = dimFp I2s · (m − 1)d−s.
By the Main Theorem in dimension 2s and Proposition 2.1, dimFp I2s = |Γ𝒥(2s)| = Qs(m) for every prime p di‐
viding m (for s = 0, I0 = (t1 − 1) has dimension m − 1 = Q0(m) directly). On the other hand |ΓK| = Qs(m)(m −
1)d−s: by [DS, Definition 1.3], a tuple (a1, …, a2d+1) lies in ΓK iff (a1, …, a2s+1) ∈ Γ𝒥(2s) and each pair (a2i, a2i+1),
i > s, is (a, a−1) with a ≠ 1. So dimFp(ψ̄ J : J ∈ K) = |ΓK| at every p | m, and by Proposition 2.1 (which holds for
every non-empty K ⊆ 𝒥) H2d(X)/LK(X) is torsion free. Its rank is |ΓK| + 1 by Theorem 0(c). ∎
Proof of Corollary W(i). Consider the family of hypersurfaces f0(z0, z1) + ⋯ + fd(z2d, z2d+1) = 0 with f0 = ⋯ =
fs = xm + ym and fs+1, …, fd binary forms of degree m without repeated factors. By Euler's identity its members
are smooth; its parameter space is the complement of a discriminant hypersurface in an affine space, hence
connected; it contains X (all fi = xm + ym) and Ws; and along it the m roots of each fi, i > s, move continuously.
The path must stay in this subfamily: the spaces LJ,β whose first 2s + 2 coordinates are paired by a matching
other than {0,1}, …, {2s, 2s+1} involve f0, …, fs and need not deform if these forms move. By [DS, §4.6], along
such a path from the Fermat variety X to Ws, the subspaces LJ,β with J ∈ 𝒥(s, d) deform to the d-spaces of Ws,
and parallel transport (constant with respect to the Gauss–Manin connection) carries L𝒥(s,d)(X) onto the sub‐
group of H2d(Ws; Z) generated by these d-spaces; transport preserves rank and primitivity. So (i) follows from
(ii), with the same rank Qs(m)(m−1)d−s + 1. ([DS, §4.6] reach the same point through the module 𝔐̄𝒥s(2d) =
𝔐̄𝒥(2s)(2s) ⊗Z S̄(s, d) of their Theorem 1.1(d), which is free «if and only if Conjecture 1.2 holds for Fermat va‐
rieties of dimension 2s».) ∎
Remarks 13.1. (1) We have read the argument of [DS, §4.6] in the original and made the choice of the path ex‐
plicit, but we have not re-derived the transport statement (the continuity of the family of subspaces and the
invariance of primitivity under parallel transport), on which Corollary W(i) depends; part (ii) is proved above
from Theorem 0, Proposition 2.1, Lemma 6.4 and the Main Theorem. (2) The rank in (ii), Qs(m)(m − 1)d−s +
1, was also checked by enumerating ΓK from [DS, Definition 1.3] at m = 3, 5 and d ≤ 3 (14 cells; the enumera‐
tion is in the repository [Rep]). (3) We make no claim about the Hodge classes of Ws: its rational Hodge class‐
es are not computed here, so Corollary W is a primitivity statement only.

puter. The computations below are corroboration: a proof that has never been confronted with data has not
been tested. Every run was made inside a memory and time watchdog. All engines and logs are in the reposi‐
tory [Rep]. Main Theorem′ and Theorem O have, in addition, been verified by a proof assistant for every de‐
gree (§14.8). The computations for the odd box and the even degrees are in §14.9.

14.2 The ingredients of §5
      Every down-set, pure combinatorics. All ≼-down-sets of Parm (not a sample) at (q, m) = (3; 4, 6, 8,
      12), (9; 3, …, 11), (27; 4, 5, 8, 9), (81; 4, 7), (243; 6), up to 940 down-sets per cell: P1, P2 and P3 hold,
      with 0 failures. The counts #{M : λ(M) = λ} were obtained by a class-by-class recursion, a route differ‐
      ent from the fibres, with the check Σλ = (q−1)m. P1 and (5.1) were also checked by brute force over all
      tails at seven cells up to (27, 4) (238 257 tests), and P2 on all comparable pairs with h up to 60 and m ≤
      12.
