14.6 Independent readings, and their limits
The proofs of §2, §5 and Appendix A were checked in a first pass, then re-derived cold, step by step, by a sep‐
arate reader with its own code, before this paper was written. An earlier version of this paper (for m = 3v)
was then read cold by three further readers, independently, each with a list of attacks and its own engines.
None found a fatal error, a gap or a mathematical error; all their findings were of presentation, and they are
incorporated here. One of them audited the proofs inside [DS] of the results collected in Theorem 0, from the
source of arXiv:1405.4683v3, and found them sound for every m ≥ 3 and every even n ≥ 2; the Picard number
of the cubic surface independently confirms the signs of [DS, Theorem 2.2] (with 1 + t in place of 1 − t in ψJ
the rank at (n, m) = (2, 3) would be 20, not 7).
The version for odd prime powers was read cold by a further reader of the same system and, for the first time,
by AI systems of two other providers (ChatGPT, OpenAI; Gemini, Google). None found a fatal error. Two of
them found, independently, that one sentence in the proof of Theorem 5.9, and the same phrase twice in the
proof of Proposition 11.1, was literally false: an initial ideal is a monomial ideal and does not contain the
non-monomial generators. One classified this as a gap and the other as an error of wording, and both gave
the argument used here (Lemma 5.8′). Two readers also pointed out Theorem 5.9′ and Corollary H. ChatGPT
then read the Hodge part of the version with Corollary H (§1.3, §8 and §11 there) and found one error, a nov‐
elty claim contradicted by the cells of [DS, §5]; it is corrected here (§1.3).
The part of this version on the degrees that are not prime powers (§6–§7) was constructed by one instance of
the same system, with its own engines, and then audited cold by another, which re-derived every step, in‐
cluding the nine cases of Proposition 7.4 and the three constructions of Proposition 7.5, compared the colour
reduction with the author's earlier block decomposition [Rep], and ran separate engines with predictions
recorded before the runs (§14.3–§14.4). The new sections (§6, §7, §12, §13) were then read cold by a further
instance, which re-derived them and recomputed the counts with its own code; it found no fatal error, no gap
and no mathematical error, and its findings of presentation are incorporated. A further cold reader then read
the whole of §6, §7, §12, §13 and the chain of the Main Theorem, with eleven section-referees and adversari‐
al verifiers of its own. It recomputed the Fermat fourfolds of degree 15 and 21 at both primes from the literal
generators of [DS], colouring by colouring, and checked Theorem 7.6 at 117 down-sets of 11 cells and
Theorem C at 10 new root cells. It found no fatal error and no gap in the proofs; one error of self-description
(the upper bound of Proposition 2.1 is algebraic, which is now stated in §1.2 as Main Theorem′); one sen‐
tence missing in the deformation argument of Corollary W(i), now supplied; and items of presentation, in‐
corporated. ChatGPT (OpenAI) then read the previous version (v7) cold, attacking the new parts with its
own computations and with the source of [DS]. It found no fatal error, no gap and no mathematical error; its
one point of presentation is incorporated here (the proof of Lemma 6.8).
Version 11: the odd box and the even degrees (§8–§9). They were found and proved on 2 October 2026 by
one instance of the system (the constructor), working alone in one session from a written mission, with its
own engines. The mission had been written by the auditing instance, which had first measured, in seven
cells with m = 2v, that the leading forms of the generators span an ideal of the right dimension, and, in eigh‐
teen cells, that the count at the odd box holds. The two documents of the constructor were then audited cold
by the auditing instance: every step of what is now §8 and §9 was written again in its own words and marked,
the translation was re-read in the original of [DS] for even m, §7 was re-read with an even q, and gates were
run with code of its own (§14.9). It found no error and no gap, and four points of presentation. The docu‐
ments were then read cold by a third instance, which had only those two documents, version 10 of this paper
and the source of [DS], and no access to the work of the other two. It wrote its verdict item by item (29 items)
before running any computation, and then made 19 logged runs with its own code, with 14 predictions
recorded in advance (§14.9). Its verdict was «holds», with no error and no gap, and 17 points of presentation,
which are incorporated here; among them: that §7 should be stated for every q, so that the even box is a case
of the theorem and not a re-reading of its proof; the labelling of the rows in the proof of Lemma 8.4; and the
observation that Lemma 8.7 holds for more border sets than it states. Two of its own negative controls did not
behave as it had predicted — one was badly conceived, and the other could not fire and was replaced by one
that does — and it says so. Its session record shows that it used no other agent and opened nothing outside
its folder. The auditing instance graded the report, checked eight of its hand computations and repeated six
of its runs with its scripts, with identical output. The text of §8 and §9 printed here was written by the audit‐
ing instance from those documents and readings, and it differs from them in notation and in three places of
substance: the upper bound of Theorem O is proved here without Theorem A (Corollary 8.13); the count of
§9 treats the index 0 as an ordinary coordinate (Lemma 9.6), which removes the per-colouring upper bound
that the constructor had proved with p-adic integers; and Corollary 10.8 is new. The limits of this part of
the record: the three instances are of the same system; the cold reader computed no cell of the literal ring of
[DS] beyond those already known; the printed text of §8–§9, as distinct from the documents it was written
from, was at first re-read and gated by its writer only (§14.9, last item; it was then read cold, see the next para‐
graph); and nothing in §8–§9 was formalized (it now is, §14.8). The documents, the audit, the report of the
cold reader, its grading and all the code are in [Rep], record/even-degrees/.
The printed text of version 11, and version 12. The printed text of version 11 was read cold on 2 October
2026 by a further instance of the same system, which had the paper, the original of [DS] and the pages of
[Aok] that it needed, and nothing else. It wrote its verdict item by item before running anything (37 items:
§1, §2, Appendix B, §5, §6, all of §7, §8 and §9, Corollary 10.8, §12, §13 and §16), re-derived every step of the
chain for even m by hand, and made logged runs with its own code, among them the dimension of the literal
ideal of [DS] in 16 cells (k, m, p) with p | m. Its verdict was «holds», with no error and no gap; its 18 notes of
presentation and three observations (some patterns are zero; the border of Lemma 8.7 for ℓ = 0; the odd-box
formula at an even box) are incorporated in this version. It did not read Appendix A or §4, did not re-prove
the topology or (H1)–(H3), and did not open the pdf; Corollary 10.8 is in its reading «holds, given Theorem
A». Its session record shows that it used no other agent and opened nothing outside its folder. The auditing
instance graded the report, checked its hand computations and repeated six of its runs with its scripts, with
identical output. In the present version, the proof of §8.5 (Lemmas 8.7′, 8.8 and Corollary 8.9) and the upper
bound of Corollary 8.13 are new in the writing: they are the proofs that were formalized, and the Lean kernel
checks them (§14.8), but no reader other than their writer has read the printed text. Report, grading and
code: [Rep], record/cold-readings/.
The limits of this record must be stated plainly.
      All these readers are AI systems (Claude, Anthropic, for all but four readings; ChatGPT for three of
      them and Gemini for one; see the note at the end). They worked separately and without access to each
      other's code, but they are not independent in the way that different human experts are.
      No human expert has refereed this work.
      Pham's theorem [Ph] is not re-proved here, and neither are the intersection numbers [DS,
      Theorem 2.2]; Appendix B reduces Theorem 0 to these two results.
      The numbering of [DS] was checked against arXiv:1405.4683v3, not against the printed version.
      Versions 9 and 10. The new material of versions 9 and 10 (Remark 10.4, Proposition 10.5, Corollary
      10.6 and Remark 10.7) and the supplementary note [Note] were read cold on 1 October 2026 by a sepa‐
      rate instance of the same system, working alone and with its own code. Its verdict was «holds», with
      one gap (the inequality of Remark 10.7(3) in characteristic 2, repaired there), three errors (a false sen‐
      tence in the sketch of Remark 10.7(2); the hypothesis of [Gin, Proposition 4.2], which is stated for
      semisimple groups; one imprecise identification in the note) and thirteen points of presentation. One
      of these was an under-claim, which became Corollary A.17; another was the observation that the dic‐
      tionary of Remark 10.4(1) fails for the points of the centralizer over a finite field. All the findings are
      incorporated, with the texts and the arguments that the reader proposed; in particular the argument of
      Corollary A.17 is the reader's. The reader re-derived Proposition 10.5 and Corollary 10.6 by hand,
      checked them at 14 cells in characteristics 2, 3, 5 and 1000003, and checked the quotation and the hy‐
      potheses of [BRR, Proposition 2.12] against the arXiv source; it did not re-audit §2–§7 or the proofs of
      Appendix A, which the earlier readings covered. Report, grading and code: [Rep], record/cold-read‐
      ings/12_internal-reader_on-v10/. Kostant's theorem is quoted from [Gin, Proposition 4.2]; [Kos]
      was not read in the original. [BRR, §2.1–§2.6] and the introduction and §2.2 of [Ric] were read in the
      arXiv sources; [KLT], [Bry] and [Lus] were not read.
      The Lean proof covers Main Theorem′ and Theorem O (§14.8), for every degree; it does not cover
      the topology, Theorem A or Corollaries H and W. Its proofs were written by an AI system and are
      checked by the Lean kernel; that the Lean statements say what the paper says is an audit, set out line
      by line in [LC, §1.2–§1.3], which a reader can repeat on about fifteen short definitions.

14.7 A referee's guide
The joints deserve attention in this order.
    1. Proposition 5.6 (P2) and Proposition 7.4, the only case analyses in the proof that are not one-line
       identities. A sceptical reader should try long, very uneven partitions at q = 3 (h = 1) and q = 9 (h = 4),
       where lengths hit the cap ℓ ≤ h, and, for Proposition 7.4, the cases AR and MR, where a removal of one
       pair of partitions is compared with an option of another kind.
    2. The translation (§2): the conventions of [DS] (j0 = 0, the variable t0 never appears, N = 2k+2) and
       Lemma 2.4.
    3. The colour reduction (§6): Proposition 6.5 (the surviving generators are pure tensors; this is where
       the full family is used, as Lemma 5.2 is in §5) and Lemma 6.8, where the index 0 couples the blocks
       only through w0; and Corollary 7.8, whose proof passes through the sum over the colourings.
    4. The coverage formulas (5.1) and (7.1), and Propositions 5.8 and 7.5, which have no slack (Re‐
       mark 5.10(2), §14.4).
    5. Theorem 0 and Appendix B. Only Theorem 0(a), (c) and (d) are topological; Theorem 0(b), which car‐
       ries every upper bound of the paper, is a Chinese-remainder count.
    6. Corollary H: the classical input (H3) and Aoki's theorem (H5) of §12.1, which are quoted and not re-
       proved here; (H4) is proved in §12.1.
    7. The odd box (§8): Lemma 8.4, part (D2), in the case νj = 1; Lemma 8.7′: the identity (8.6), the two op‐
       erations of Lemma 8.8 and the powers of ζ in Corollary 8.9 (all of them checked in Lean, §14.8); and,
       in Proposition 8.10, the case (M) with δ = 1, where two Pfaffians are combined so that the terms with
       D−(y1, ·) cancel, and the case (Z) with δ = 1, where Lemma 8.7 is used.
    8. The even degrees (§9): Step 2 of Proposition 9.2 (the leading forms); §7 read with an even q (the only
       use of the field is C(q − 1, r − 1) ≠ 0 in case (γ) of Proposition 7.5); Lemma 9.9 (the substitution u =
       −t) and Lemma 9.10 (the map θ); and Lemma 9.6, where the index 0 is a coordinate of the count but
       not of the ideals.

14.8 A machine-checked proof of Main Theorem′ and of Theorem O
Main Theorem′ has been proved, for every degree, in the proof assistant Lean 4 [dMU] with the Mathlib li‐
brary [Mlib]; the certificate is [LC]. The final theorem, EvenAll.mainTheorem', states: for every m ≥ 1 and
every k ≥ 0, R/(ψJ : J ∈ 𝒥) is a free abelian group of rank m2k+1 − Qk(m), and for every field F, dimF F[G]/(ψ̄ J : J
∈ 𝒥) = m2k+1 − Qk(m) and dimF (ψ̄ J : J ∈ 𝒥) = Qk(m). This is slightly more general than Main Theorem′ as
stated in §1.2 (m ≥ 3, k ≥ 1, prime fields). In Lean, Qk(m) is defined by the formulas of §1.2, with the parity
split, and lemmas identify it with the numbers of tuples of Lemmas 2.2 and 9.1 and with |Γ𝒥|. Theorem O is
proved with equality over every field and with the freeness over Z (OddEquality.D1, OddEquality.D2DJ; for
the ideal ℳ of Lemma 8.12, OddEquality.D2M).
      What was formalized. For odd m (version 1 of the certificate, 29 September 2026): Lemma 2.2 and
      the translation of §2 in its local form (Lemmas 2.3, 2.4 and Proposition 2.5); Theorem 5.3 and the in‐
      duction of §5; the colour reduction of §6; Theorems 7.6 and C; and the upper bound [DS, Claim 4.3]
      (Theorem 0(b), Appendix B, Step 6), which is algebraic. For every degree, in twenty further pieces: §7
      for every box q ≥ 2, and at q = pv in characteristic p, 2 included; Lemma 9.1 and the upper bound for
      even m; all of §8 — the shapes and interlaced pairs, Lemmas 8.3–8.5, the Pfaffians and bordered
      Pfaffians of §8.3 over any commutative ring with the signs given there, Lemmas 8.7, 8.7′ and 8.8 and
      Corollary 8.9 in the form printed in §8.5, Definition 8.6, the seven cases of Proposition 8.10, Theorem
      8.11, Theorem O, Lemma 8.12 in the direction used and Corollary 8.13 — and Theorem 8.15; and all of
      §9, with Lemmas 9.4–9.10 for every prime and every cofactor. Theorem 4.1, Theorems 5.9 and 5.9′,
      Corollaries 7.8 and 9.12, Theorem 8.15 and the equality in Theorem O were formalized too, although
      Main Theorem′ does not need them.
      How. The Lean proofs were written by the AI system Aristotle (Harmonic), in 49 pieces (29 for the odd
      degrees, 20 for the rest), each a short text written from this paper. Every returned file was compiled
      again on the author's machine (Lean v4.28.0, Mathlib at the tag v4.28.0) and audited against the paper
      before the next piece was sent: the difference with the previous project (no piece changed a file of an
      earlier one), a search for forbidden constructs, the build, the statements as Lean prints them, and their
      axioms. Before a piece was sent, its statements were tested by exact computation, with negative con‐
      trols (33 731 checks for the twenty pieces of the even degrees, with no failure).
      Result. 233 files, 41 243 lines, 1 859 theorems and lemmas. There is no sorry, admit, native_decide or
      added axiom, and every main theorem depends only on the axioms propext, Classical.choice and
      Quot.sound. A rebuild from source on the author's machine compiled 229 of the 233 modules; the oth‐
      er four — two modules of numeric checks, whose evaluation by the kernel needs more memory than
      the cap of the local builds, and the two modules that only import them — compiled on Aristotle's ma‐
      chines, and no theorem used elsewhere is in them. A program that collects the declarations used by
      the final theorem finds 4 412 of them, in 180 of the 233 modules.
      What is not formalized. Theorem 0(a), (c) and (d): the topology of [DS] that identifies the torsion of
      R/(ψJ) with that of H2k(X; Z)/L(X) and gives the rank of L(X). Mathlib has no singular homology of
      complex Fermat varieties. So the Main Theorem, as a statement about homology, is machine-checked
      only in its algebraic form. Theorem A, Corollaries H and W, §11, the graded refinements of Theorems
      B and O, and the computations of §14.1–§14.5 and §14.9 are not formalized either.
      Where. The certificate [LC] gives the Lean statements and definitions verbatim, compares them with
      this paper line by line, and lists the trust base, the data of every piece and how to reproduce the build.
      The Lean project, the pieces, the checks and every log are in [Rep], folder lean/.

14.9 The odd box and the even degrees

ed before the runs. None of these computations is used in a proof.

15. Open problems
  1. Equality for every interlaced pair. Prove dim VΛ = |ZΛ| for every interlaced pair Λ of Theorem 8.11
     (it is proved at the two roots, Corollary 8.13, and measured in every cell we computed, §14.9), and de‐
     scribe Cm/VΛ as an associated graded ring, as in the Complement to Theorem 5.9. The evaluation argu‐
     ment of Theorem 5.9 does not apply as it stands: on a grid with 0 the polynomial Dr(a, b) does not van‐
     ish off the pairs b = −a. (In version 10 this item was «Even degrees»; it is now Theorem 9.11.)
  2. The remaining integral Hodge classes. When m is neither prime nor 4 and has a prime factor at
     most 2k + 1, find generators of Hdg(X) beyond L(X). By Corollary H(ii) the missing part is a free group
     of rank |𝔅| − Qk(m) (Remark 12.3, Lemma 12.4), and the integral question is whether algebraic cycles
     fill it. For k = 1 they do, by the Lefschetz (1,1) theorem; the question is for k ≥ 2. The rational Hodge
     conjecture for these X is known in many cases [Shi79], [Aok], [Jum].
  3. Elementary divisors. For m = p prime and k ≥ 2, compute the elementary divisors of Hdg(X) = L(X);
     [AMV, Table 1] gives the data for small cells. For k = 1 they are the author's Watermark and Double
     Ladder theorems [WDL]; for every k, the discriminant is given by the sequel note [WED], modulo
     [BRR, Proposition 2.12] (Remark 12.2).
  4. A direct proof of Corollary 7.8. Prove dimF̄ p Ibala ≤ Nbal(a, q) on the bipartite block itself, without
     passing through the sum over the colourings of §6; since the dimension over Q is larger (Remark
     7.9(2)), such a proof must use the characteristic. Equality dim VΛ = |ZΛ| for every down-set of Theorem
     7.6, measured at every down-set we tested (§14.4) and never contradicted, would give it.
  5. Representation theory. The Specht module route at q = 3 (Remark 4.2(3)), and more generally a rep‐
     resentation-theoretic description of the rings Cm/VΛ (Complement to Theorem 5.9) and of the bipartite
     quotients.
  6. Theorem A for even q and odd n. Prove that for every field of characteristic ≠ 2, every even q and
     every odd n, dim F[x1, …, xn]/(e1, e3, …; x1q, …, xnq) is the number of walks of length n on the lattice Zq/2
     with steps ±εi from 0 to ε1. For even n the corresponding statement (closed walks) follows from [BRR,
     Proposition 2.12] (Remark 10.4(6)); for odd n the weights are not in the root lattice, and the statement
     is computed in a few cells only. The proof of Theorem A uses the value 0 of the grid, which an even q
     does not have.
  7. Around the odd box. Problem 7 of version 10 is Theorem O. What remains: (a) derive Theorem B and
     Theorem O in positive characteristic from the theory of modules with a good filtration, as [BRR] derive
     the count of Theorem A; (b) Theorem C over an arbitrary field: in the cells where a binomial coeffi‐
     cient C(q − 1, t) vanishes that we computed (q = 4 over F3, q = 6 over F2), the inequality of Theorem
     7.6 still holds at every down-set, although the proof of Proposition 7.5 does not apply (§14.9); (c)
     Lemma 8.7′ gives the membership for any exponents in its case (a), and in its case (b) with the border
     yr−1, which cannot be dropped (§8.5, Remark (3)): describe all the border sets for which it holds.
  8. What is not formalized. Main Theorem′ and Theorem O are formalized for every degree (§14.8; this
     was problem 8 of version 11). Formalize the topology of Theorem 0 (Mathlib has no singular homology
     of complex Fermat varieties), Theorem A, Corollaries H and W, and the graded refinements of
     Theorems B and O.
  9. The leading forms. By Remark 9.3(1) the generators ψ̄ J at m = 2v are a standard basis for the order fil‐
     tration. Is the same true of the generators at an odd prime power, for the filtration by the powers of the
     augmentation ideal, and does it give a proof of Theorem B that avoids the coordinate y = t − t−1?

16. Exact status
    PROVED, with no topology: Main Theorem′ — for every m ≥ 3 and every k ≥ 1, the group R/(ψJ : J
    ∈ 𝒥) of [DS] is free abelian of rank m2k+1 − Qk(m). For odd m it uses §5–§7; for even m, §6 (Lemmas
    6.1, 6.2, 6.4 and 6.7, at every prime, the prime 2 included), §7, §8, §9 and, at the odd primes, §5; in both
    cases [DS, Claim 4.3], a Chinese-remainder count re-proved in Appendix B, Step 6.
    MACHINE-CHECKED in Lean 4 with Mathlib (§14.8), for every degree: Main Theorem′, for
    every m ≥ 1 and every k ≥ 0, over Z and over every field; and Theorem O, with equality over every field
    and the freeness over Z. No sorry, and no axiom beyond propext, Classical.choice, Quot.sound. Not for‐
    malized: the topology of Theorem 0(a), (c) and (d), Theorem A, Corollaries H and W, and the graded
    refinements of Theorems B and O.
    PROVED, modulo Pham's theorem [Ph] and the intersection numbers [DS, Theorem 2.2]: the
    Main Theorem, i.e. Conjecture 1.2 of Degtyarev–Shimada for the Fermat varieties of every degree m ≥
    3 and every even dimension. Through Theorem 0 it uses [DS, Theorem 1.1(a), Claim 4.3, Theorem 1.4,
    Corollary 1.5], which Appendix B re-derives from those two results and classical topology, for every m
    ≥ 3.
    PROVED, modulo in addition the standard topological facts (H1)–(H2), the classical descrip‐
    tion of Hodge classes (H3) [SK], [Shi79], [Ran], and Aoki's theorem (H5) [Aok] (for prime m
    and for m = 4, (H4) and (H4′) replace (H5); the proof of (H4) uses the formula for B1,χ and
    Dirichlet's theorem L(1, χ) ≠ 0 [Was]): Corollary H — for every m ≥ 3, L(X) is a primitive sublattice
    of Hdg(X) with free quotient of rank |𝔅| − Qk(m), and the integral Hodge conjecture holds for X when
    m is prime, or m = 4, or every prime factor of m is at least 2k + 3. In the prime cells computed in [DS,
    §5], in the cells (k, m) = (2, 4), (3, 4) computed there, and in the cell (2, 4) of [AMV], it was already
    known (§1.3).
    PROVED, modulo the same two results as the Main Theorem: Corollary W(ii), for every m ≥ 3,
    and, modulo in addition the transport argument of [DS, §4.6], Corollary W(i).
    PROVED: Theorem B (the dimension count, with equality over every field and for every odd q, and
    the freeness over Z), Theorems 5.3, 5.9 and 5.9′, Theorem 4.1; the colour reduction (Proposition 6.9);
    Theorem C and Theorem 7.6, for every integer q ≥ 2 and every field in which the binomial coefficients
    C(q − 1, t) are non-zero; Corollary 7.8; Lemma 12.4.
    PROVED: Theorem O (the count at the odd box, with equality over every field and for every odd r ≥ 3,
    and the freeness over Z), Theorem 8.11, Lemmas 8.7 and 8.7′, Theorem 8.15; Proposition 9.2, Theorem
    9.11 and Corollary 9.12 (in Proposition 9.2 and Theorem 9.11, the last clause, that H2k(X; Z)/L(X) is tor‐
    sion free, is modulo the same two results as the Main Theorem).
      PROVED: Theorem A, for every field of characteristic ≠ 2, every odd q and every n, with Corollaries
      10.1 and 10.2. It is a different statement from the Main Theorem. As a count it is not new: it follows
      from Kostant's theorem [Kos] in characteristic 0 and from [BRR, Proposition 2.12] in odd characteris‐
      tic, through the dictionary of Remark 10.4. What this paper adds is that dictionary and the elementary

      A.17).

Acknowledgements and use of AI
The mathematics of this paper was developed with extensive use of Claude, an AI system made by
Anthropic, working under the author's direction in separate roles: instances that proposed and wrote proofs,
instances that re-derived them cold and ran their own checks, and instances that read the finished text as ref‐
erees. The previous versions were also read by AI systems of two other providers (ChatGPT, OpenAI; Gemini,
Google). The degrees that are not prime powers (§6–§7) were constructed by one instance and audited cold
by another. The even degrees and the odd box (§8–§9) were constructed by one instance in a single session,
from a mission written by an auditing instance that had first measured the leading-form inequality of §9.2 in
seven cells; they were then audited cold by that auditing instance, step by step and with its own code, and
read cold by a third instance, which had no access to the work of the other two (§14.6). The printed text of
version 11 was read cold by a further instance (§14.6). The Lean 4 proofs of Main Theorem′ and of Theorem
O (§14.8) were written by Aristotle, an AI system made by Harmonic, from pieces written from this paper,
and were compiled and audited on the author's machine. The author directed the project and takes responsi‐
bility for the content.
The work took more than two and a half months. It went through several hundred written working sessions,
recorded more than three hundred unsuccessful routes together with the reason each one failed, and pro‐
duced more than two hundred standalone write-ups of intermediate results. The full record — the engines
and their logs, the audits and the cold readings, the dead routes, and the history of the project — is public at
github.com/tretoef-estrella/chaise-longue-theorem [Rep]. Every result of the author on which this pa‐
per relies or which it cites — the earlier block decomposition quoted in §6, the Watermark and Double
Ladder theorems quoted in §12, and the computer verdicts quoted in §1.3 and §14 — is deposited in that
repository, together with every engine and log behind §14 and the Lean project of §14.8.



Appendix A. Proof of Theorem A
This appendix is independent of §2–§5. Its symbols are local: here L is a finite set of variables, A and B are
subsets of L, C is a set of field elements (Lemma A.4), J is an ideal (Proposition A.5), Ra(G) are the «rows» of
Lemma A.6, Kμ(L) are ideals, and Θ is a generating function (it has nothing to do with ΓK of [DS]).

A.0 Conventions
      F is a field with 2 ∈ F×, q ≥ 1 is odd, and h = (q−1)/2.
      For X ⊆ L, EX(t) := Πx∈X(1 + xt) and ej(X) := [tj]EX(t), so e0 = 1 and ej = 0 for j < 0 or j > |X|. Iq(L) :=
      (ej(L) : j odd) + boxL with boxL := (xq : x ∈ L).
      [tD] of a power series is 0 when D < 0.
      Id(2y) := Σb≥0 y2b+d/(b!(b+d)!) for d ≥ 0, and Nq(n) := n!·[yn] ey I0(2y)h.
      A partition is a finite multiset of positive integers μ1 ≥ ⋯ ≥ μℓ, with ℓ = ℓ(μ) and |μ| = Σμi. For w ≥ 0,
      βw(μ) := Σi max(μi − w, 0) (the number of boxes of the Young diagram to the right of column w), and
      cw(μ) := βw(μ) − βw+1(μ) = #{i : μi ≥ w+1}. So β0(μ) = |μ|, c0(μ) = ℓ(μ), and cw(μ) ≤ ℓ(μ) for all w.
Strategy. Adjoin one variable z to L. Every ideal G of F[L ∪ z] containing zq has q rows R0(G) ⊆ ⋯ ⊆ Rq−1(G),
and dim F[L∪z]/G = Σa dim F[L]/Ra(G) (Lemma A.6). The count satisfies the matching recursion Nμ(n+1) =
Σa Nchilda(μ)(n) (Lemma A.3), which is the derivative of a product of Bessel functions. So it suffices to attach to
each μ an ideal Kμ(L) with K∅(L) = Iq(L) such that every row of Kμ(L ∪ z) contains the ideal of the corre‐
sponding child (Theorem A.12). The ideals come from one generating function Θ, and adjoining z to one of
three sets shifts an index of Θ in a controlled way (Lemma A.8). This is the same peeling idea as §5.1, with
the box q in place of q − 1.

A.1 The count and the dictionary
For a partition μ with ℓ(μ) ≤ h, put Fμ(y) := ey · Πi=1ℓ Iμi(2y) · I0(2y)h−ℓ and Nμ(n) := n!·[yn]Fμ(y), so that Nq(n)
= N∅(n).
Lemma A.1. For d ≥ 1, (d/dy) Id(2y) = Id−1(2y) + Id+1(2y), and (d/dy) I0(2y) = 2 I1(2y).
Proof. Termwise, with (2b+d) = b + (b+d):
             Σb (2b+d) y2b+d−1/(b!(b+d)!) = Σb≥1 y2b+d−1/((b−1)!(b+d)!) + Σb y2b+d−1/(b!(b+d−1)!).
The first sum is Id+1(2y) (substitute b ↦ b+1). The second is Id−1(2y) if d ≥ 1, and for d = 0 it is again I1(2y). ∎
Definition A.2 (the dictionary). Let ℓ = ℓ(μ) ≤ h. For a ∈ {0, …, q−1}, childa(μ) is the partition:
         lower, 0 ≤ a ≤ ℓ−1: μ with one copy of μa+1 replaced by μa+1 − 1; parts are lowered largest first, and
         a part 0 is deleted;
         value-0, a = ℓ: μ;
         new-class, ℓ+1 ≤ a ≤ q−ℓ−1: μ ∪ (1);
         raise, a = q−j with 1 ≤ j ≤ ℓ: μ with one copy of μj replaced by μj + 1; row q−1 raises the largest part.
New-class rows exist iff ℓ+1 ≤ q−ℓ−1, i.e. iff ℓ + 1 ≤ h. Hence every child has at most h parts.
Lemma A.3. Nμ(0) = [μ = ∅], and Nμ(n+1) = Σa=0q−1 Nchilda(μ)(n) for every n ≥ 0.
Proof. The first claim holds because Fμ(0) = [μ = ∅]. For the second, Nμ(n+1) = n!·[yn]Fμ'. By the product
rule and Lemma A.1, Fμ' is a sum of:
         Fμ, from ey;
         for each i, F of μ with μi replaced by μi − 1, plus F of μ with μi replaced by μi + 1 (a part 0 is a factor I0);
         2(h−ℓ) copies of Fμ∪(1), from I0h−ℓ.
These are exactly the ℓ + 1 + 2(h−ℓ) + ℓ = q children of Definition A.2. ∎
Lemma A.4. Let C be a set of q elements of a field of characteristic ≠ 2 with 0 ∈ C = −C. Then Nq(n) is the
number of c ∈ Cn such that #{i : ci = v} = #{i : ci = −v} for every v ∈ C ∖ {0}.
Proof. C ∖ {0} splits into h pairs {v, −v} with v ≠ −v. By the exponential formula:
         the zero coordinates contribute the factor ey;
         each pair with b coordinates equal to v and b equal to −v contributes Σb y2b/(b!·b!) = I0(2y). ∎

A.2 The lower bound
Proposition A.5. dimF F[L]/Iq(L) ≥ Nq(|L|).
Proof.
Reduction to a large field. Let F' be an infinite extension of F. The dimension is unchanged by extension of
scalars, because F'[L]/Iq(L)F'[L] = (F[L]/Iq(L)) ⊗F F'. Choose c1, …, ch ∈ F'× with ci ≠ ±cj for i ≠ j, and put C
:= {0, ±c1, …, ±ch}. These are q distinct elements, since c ≠ −c for c ≠ 0.
A radical ideal with the right count. Let f(x) := Πc∈C(x − c) and J := (ej(L) : j odd) + (f(x) : x ∈ L).
                                                                          L
         By the Chinese remainder theorem, F'[L]/(f(x) : x ∈ L) ≅ F'C , a product of copies of F'.
         Every quotient of a finite product of fields by an ideal is the product over a subset of the factors. Hence
         F'[L]/J ≅ F'Z, where Z is the set of c ∈ CL at which every odd ej vanishes.
         For such c, all odd ej(c) vanish iff Πx(1 + cx t) is an even polynomial, i.e. Πx(1+cx t) = Πx(1 − cx t).
         By unique factorisation in F'[t], this holds iff the multiset {cx ≠ 0} is closed under negation.
         So dim F'[L]/J = |Z| = Nq(n) by Lemma A.4.
Degeneration. Let in(J) be the span of the top-degree forms of the elements of J.
         (ej : j odd) ⊆ in(J), since the ej are homogeneous.
         xq ∈ in(J), since xq is the top form of f(x).
Hence Iq(L) ⊆ in(J). For a degree-compatible monomial order, LM(g) = LM(top(g)). Moreover, for fixed d the
non-zero top forms of degree-d elements of J form, with 0, a vector space. So LM(J) = LM(in(J)), and by
Macaulay's basis theorem dim F'[L]/in(J) = dim F'[L]/J. Therefore
dim F'[L]/Iq(L) ≥ dim F'[L]/in(J) = Nq(n). ∎
A.3 Rows
Lemma A.6. Let z ∉ L, and let G ⊆ F[L ∪ z] be an ideal with zq ∈ G. For 0 ≤ a ≤ q−1 put
                                     Ra(G) := {f ∈ F[L] : za f ∈ G + za+1F[L∪z]}.
Then:
         each Ra(G) is an ideal of F[L];
         Ra(G) ⊆ Ra+1(G);
         dimF F[L∪z]/G = Σa=0q−1 dimF F[L]/Ra(G), in N ∪ {∞}.
Proof.
         Ideal: Ra(G) is an additive group, and for g ∈ F[L] we have za gf = g·za f.
         Monotone: multiplying za f ∈ G + (za+1) by z gives za+1 f ∈ G + (za+2).
         Dimension: put Ga := G + zaF[L∪z]. Then G0 = F[L∪z] and Gq = G. The map F[L] → Ga/Ga+1, f ↦ za
         f, is surjective because zaF[L∪z] = zaF[L] + za+1F[L∪z], and its kernel is Ra(G). Summing over a gives
         the formula. ∎

A.4 The generating function
Fix disjoint A, B ⊆ L. Put:
         P := L ∖ (A ∪ B);
         r := |A|;
         w := |B| − |A|, the imbalance.
For a ∈ A let φa(t) := (1 − at)/(1 + at) = 1 + Σi≥1 2(−1)i ai ti.
Definition A.7. For s ∈ Z, let DL(A,B;s) := r(q−1) + |P| + 1 − s and
                                  ΘsL(A;B) := [tDL(A,B;s)] EP(−t)·Πa∈A φa(t) ∈ F[L].
We call s the level.
Remark. With HA;B(t) := Πa∈A(1+at)−1Πb∈B(1−bt)−1 one has EL(−t)HA;B(t) = EP(−t)Πa∈Aφa(t): the factors of B
cancel. So B enters Θ only through P, that is, through the degree D.
Lemma A.8 (the three moves, and vanishing). Let z ∉ L and L+ := L ∪ {z}. For disjoint A, B ⊆ L and s ∈
Z:
         (P) ΘsL+(A;B) = Θs−1L(A;B) − z·ΘsL(A;B);
         (B) ΘsL+(A;B∪{z}) = ΘsL(A;B);
         (A) ΘsL+(A∪{z};B) = Θs−q+1L(A;B) + Σi≥1 2(−1)i zi·Θs−q+1+iL(A;B), a finite sum;
         (V) if s ≤ 0, then ΘsL(A;B) ∈ boxA, and ΘsL(A;B) = 0 if A = ∅.
Proof.
         (P) In L+, z lies in the set P+ = P ∪ {z}, and EP+(−t) = EP(−t)(1 − zt). Since DL+(A,B;s) = DL(A,B;s−1)
         and DL+(A,B;s) − 1 = DL(A,B;s), the identity follows.
         (B) Neither P nor D changes.
         (A) One more factor φz(t), and DL+(A∪z,B;s) = DL(A,B;s) + (q−1). The coefficient of zi ti in φz is 1 for i
         = 0 and 2(−1)i for i ≥ 1. Finally, level u of (A;B) in L has degree DL(A,B;s) + (q−1) − i exactly when u =
         s − q + 1 + i.
         (V) ΘsL(A;B) = Σp (−1)p ep(P)·[tD−p]ΠA φa. The coefficient of tm in ΠA φa is homogeneous of degree m
         in the r letters of A. For s ≤ 0 every p ≤ |P| gives D − p ≥ r(q−1)+1. So every monomial has some expo‐
      nent ≥ q when r ≥ 1, and [tD−p]1 = 0 when r = 0. ∎
Lemma A.9 (descent). For a ≥ 0:
                             za·ΘsL(A;B) = Θs−aL+(A;B∪{z}) − Σi=1a zi−1·Θs−a+iL+(A;B).
Proof. The case a = 0 is (B). By (P) and (B), z·ΘsL(A;B) = Θs−1L+(A;B∪z) − ΘsL+(A;B), and applying (B) again to
the first term gives zΘsL = Θs−1L(A;B) − ΘsL+(A;B). Hence
                                     zaΘsL = za−1Θs−1L(A;B) − za−1ΘsL+(A;B),
and the formula follows by induction on a. ∎
Lemma A.10 (the odd identity). Let |A| = |B| = r, let c ∈ Z, M := DL(A,B;c), and hm := [tm]HA;B(t). For every
ℓ ≥ 0,
                    zℓ+1Θc+1L(A;B) ≡ Ω − ½·zℓ·((−1)M ΘcL(B;A) − ΘcL(A;B)) mod zℓ+2F[L+],
where Ω := zℓ Σj odd ej(L+)hM−j − zℓ+1Σj odd ej(L+)hM−1−j lies in (ej(L+) : j odd).
Proof. Put αm := [tm]EL(t)HA;B(t) and βm := [tm]EL(−t)HA;B(t). By definition and the Remark after Definition
A.7, βM = ΘcL(A;B) and βM−1 = Θc+1L(A;B).
Next, EL(t)HA;B(t) = EP(t)ΠB(1+bt)/(1−bt), which at t = −u is EL(−u)HB;A(u). Since |A| = |B|, the degree M
corresponds to the same level c for (B;A), so αM = (−1)M ΘcL(B;A).
Let Xm := [tm]O(t)HA;B(t) with O(t) := Σj odd ej(L+)tj = ½(EL+(t) − EL+(−t)), so that Ω = zℓXM − zℓ+1XM−1. From
EL+(±t) = EL(±t)(1 ± zt):
                                        Xm = ½(αm + zαm−1 − βm + zβm−1).
Hence
                             Ω = ½zℓ(αM − βM) + zℓ+1βM−1 − ½zℓ+2(αM−2 + βM−2).
Rearranging gives the claim. ∎

A.5 The ideals
Definition A.11. For a partition μ with ℓ(μ) ≤ h and a finite set L,
                 Kμ(L) := Iq(L) + ( ΘsL(A;B) : A, B ⊆ L disjoint, w = |B| − |A| ≥ 0, 1 ≤ s ≤ βw(μ) ).
Remarks.
    1. K∅(L) = Iq(L), since βw(∅) = 0.
    2. If μ ≠ ∅ and L = ∅, then Θ1∅(∅;∅) = [t0]1 = 1, so Kμ(∅) = F.
    3. For A = ∅, the generators are ±e|S|+1−s(S) with S = L ∖ B, allowed for s ≤ βn−|S|(μ). For S ⊊ L this is
       Tanisaki's rule for the partition μ ∪ (1n−|μ|) of n [Ta], [GP]; for S = L it adds the ej(L) with j ≥ n − |μ| +
       1.
    4. The case s = 1, A ≠ ∅ gives ±2r (ΠA aq−1)·ΠP x modulo box.
    5. The generators with A ≠ ∅ and higher s are not suggested by Tanisaki's rule. They were first found by
       computation, as the corrections needed for μ = (1,1), (1,1,1) and (2,2), and the generating function Θ
       organises them.

A.6 The row inclusions
Theorem A.12. Let μ be a partition with ℓ := ℓ(μ) ≤ h, L a finite set, z ∉ L and K+ := Kμ(L ∪ z). For every a ∈
{0, …, q−1}:
                                                  Ra(K+) ⊇ Kchilda(μ)(L).
Lemma A.13. Let A, B ⊆ L be disjoint with w := |B|−|A| ≥ 0.
         (i) If s ≥ 1 and s + 1 ≤ βw(μ), then ΘsL(A;B) ∈ R0(K+).
         (ii) If s ∈ Z, s ≤ βw(μ), a ≥ 0 and s − a ≤ βw+1(μ), then za·ΘsL(A;B) ∈ K+. (For s ≤ 0 this holds already by
         (V).)
         (iii) Iq(L) ⊆ R0(K+).
Proof.
         (i) Θs+1L+(A;B) is a generator of K+: its imbalance is w and its level s+1 ≤ βw(μ). By (P) it equals
         ΘsL(A;B) − zΘs+1L(A;B).
         (ii) Use Lemma A.9.
                 The first term has imbalance w+1 and level s − a ≤ βw+1(μ).
                 The terms of the sum have imbalance w and levels in [s−a+1, s] ⊆ (−∞, βw(μ)].
                 Each term is a generator of K+ if its level is ≥ 1, and lies in box by (V) otherwise.
         (iii) ej(L) = ej(L+) − z·ej−1(L), and xq ∈ K+ for x ∈ L. ∎
Proof of Theorem A.12. By Lemma A.6 the rows increase, so it suffices to place every generator of Kchilda(μ)(L)
in the row a assigned by the dictionary (or in an earlier row). Generators from Iq(L) are handled by (iii). So
consider a child generator ΘsL(A;B) with w = |B|−|A| ≥ 0 and 1 ≤ s ≤ βw(child). Recall βw − βw+1 = cw ≤ ℓ.
Lower, 0 ≤ a ≤ ℓ−1. Here βw(child) = βw(μ) − [μa+1 > w].
         If μa+1 > w, then s + 1 ≤ βw(μ), and (i) applies.
         If μa+1 ≤ w, then all parts > w are among μ1, …, μa (parts are ordered decreasingly), so cw(μ) ≤ a. Hence
         s − a ≤ βw(μ) − cw(μ) = βw+1(μ), and (ii) gives zaΘsL ∈ K+.
Value-0, a = ℓ. Here s ≤ βw(μ) and s − ℓ ≤ βw(μ) − cw(μ) = βw+1(μ), so (ii) applies.
New-class, ℓ+1 ≤ a ≤ q−ℓ−1. Here βw(μ ∪ (1)) = βw(μ) + [w = 0].
         Every generator other than (w, s) = (0, |μ|+1) is a generator of Kμ(L), hence lies in row ℓ ⊆ row a by the
         value-0 case.
         For w = 0 (so |A| = |B|) and s = |μ| + 1, apply Lemma A.10 with c = |μ|:
               Ω ∈ K+;
               by (ii) with a = ℓ, both zℓΘ|μ|L(A;B) and zℓΘ|μ|L(B;A) lie in K+. The pairs (A;B) and (B;A) have im‐
               balance 0; |μ| = β0(μ); and |μ| − ℓ = β0 − c0 = β1(μ).
               Hence zℓ+1Θ|μ|+1L(A;B) ∈ K+ + (zℓ+2), i.e. the generator lies in Rℓ+1 ⊆ Ra.
Raise, a = q − j, 1 ≤ j ≤ ℓ. Here βw(child) = βw(μ) + [μj ≥ w], and q − j ≥ q − ℓ ≥ ℓ + 1 because 2ℓ ≤ q−1.
         Old levels, s ≤ βw(μ): s − (q−j) ≤ βw(μ) − ℓ − 1 < βw+1(μ), so (ii) applies.
         New level at w = 0, s = |μ| + 1: the new-class argument places it in Rℓ+1 ⊆ Rq−j.
         New level at w ≥ 1 (only when μj ≥ w): s = t := βw(μ) + 1. Put s' := βw−1(μ) and consider Π := Θs'L+(A ∪
         {z}; B).
                 Π has imbalance w − 1 ≥ 0 and level s' ≥ cw−1(μ) ≥ j ≥ 1, so it is a generator of K+.
                 By (A), Π = Σi≥0 κi zi ΘuiL(A;B), with ui := s' − q + 1 + i, κ0 = 1 and κi = 2(−1)i for i ≥ 1, all units.
                 Put i* := q − cw−1(μ). Then ui* = βw(μ) + 1 = t, and i* ≤ q − j because μ1, …, μj ≥ w gives cw−1(μ) ≥
                 j.
                 For i < i*: ui ≤ βw(μ), and ui − i = s' − q + 1 = βw+1(μ) + cw(μ) + cw−1(μ) − (q−1) ≤ βw+1(μ), since
                 cw + cw−1 ≤ 2ℓ ≤ q−1. So zi ΘuiL(A;B) ∈ K+ by (ii), or lies in box if ui ≤ 0. (This includes i = 0,
             which matters when s' ≥ q.)
             Therefore κi* zi*ΘtL(A;B) ∈ K+ + (zi*+1). Since κi* is a unit, ΘtL(A;B) ∈ Ri* ⊆ Rq−j. ∎
Remark A.14 (the order of the lower rows is used). The lower case uses «largest part first». With the opposite
order, the inclusion fails already for μ = (3,1), q = 9, |L| = 5: 65 generators of the child (3) lie outside row 0.
The count of Lemma A.3 does not see the order; the inclusions do.

A.7 Proof of Theorem A
Theorem A.15. For every n ≥ 0, every set L of n variables and every partition μ with ℓ(μ) ≤ h: dimF F[L]/Kμ(L)
≤ Nμ(n).
Proof. Induction on n.
Base, n = 0. F[∅] = F. For μ = ∅, K∅(∅) = 0 and N∅(0) = 1. For μ ≠ ∅, Kμ(∅) = F (Remark 2 after Definition
A.11) and Nμ(0) = 0.
Step. Let |L| = n and z ∉ L. Then zq ∈ Kμ(L∪z), and:
                      dim F[L∪z]/Kμ(L∪z) = Σa=0q−1 dim F[L]/Ra(Kμ(L∪z)) (Lemma A.6)
                                   ≤ Σa dim F[L]/Kchilda(μ)(L) (Theorem A.12)
                             ≤ Σa Nchilda(μ)(n) (induction; every child has ≤ h parts)
                                           = Nμ(n+1) (Lemma A.3). ∎
Proof of Theorem A. Take μ = ∅ in Theorem A.15: K∅(L) = Iq(L), so dim F[L]/Iq(L) ≤ Nq(n). Combine with
Proposition A.5. ∎

A.8 Proofs of Corollaries 10.1 and 10.2
Corollary 10.1. Take F = Fp, q = pv, n = 2k+2. Then Ak(q) = Nq(2k+2) by Theorem A. Also Nq(2k+2) = Pk(q):
in the proof of Proposition A.5 with F' = F̄ p and the set of values C = Fq ⊂ F', the set Z is {x ∈ Fq2k+2 : ej(x) =
0, j odd}. ∎
Corollary 10.2. M is spanned by the monomials with exponents < q, so it is finitely generated. Write M ≅ Zρ
⊕ T with T finite. For a field F, M ⊗ F ≅ F[L]/Iq(L), by right exactness of ⊗. So:
      ρ = dimQ = Nq(n);
      for an odd prime p, dimFp = ρ + (number of cyclic p-primary summands of T) = Nq(n).
Hence T has no odd primary part. ∎
Remark A.16. In characteristic 2 the statement fails, and the proof uses ½ in exactly one place (Lemma A.10).
The measured dimensions at n = 4 are 21, 65, 133, 225 for q = 3, 5, 7, 9, against Nq(4) = 19, 61, 127, 217.

A.9 Equality for every partition
Corollary A.17. For every partition μ with ℓ(μ) ≤ h, every finite set L and every z ∉ L:
      dimF F[L]/Kμ(L) = Nμ(|L|);
      Ra(Kμ(L ∪ z)) = Kchilda(μ)(L) for every a ∈ {0, …, q−1}.
Proof. Let P(μ) be the statement «dimF F[L]/Kμ(L) = Nμ(|L|) for every finite set L». P(∅) is Theorem A.
Suppose P(μ), and let |L| = n and z ∉ L. In the chain of the proof of Theorem A.15,
   dim F[L∪z]/Kμ(L∪z) = Σa dim F[L]/Ra(Kμ(L∪z)) ≤ Σa dim F[L]/Kchilda(μ)(L) ≤ Σa Nchilda(μ)(n) = Nμ(n+1),
the two ends are equal by P(μ), and all the numbers are finite. The first inequality is a sum of the inequalities
dim F[L]/Ra ≤ dim F[L]/Kchilda(μ)(L) given by the inclusions of Theorem A.12, and the second is a sum of the
inequalities of Theorem A.15; so each of them is an equality. Hence dim F[L]/Kchilda(μ)(L) = Nchilda(μ)(n) for
every a and every L, which is P(childa(μ)), and Ra(Kμ(L∪z)) = Kchilda(μ)(L), because one ideal contains the oth‐
er and they have the same finite codimension. So P(μ) implies P(ν) for every child ν of μ, and the second
claim for μ.
It remains to see that every partition ν ≠ ∅ with ℓ(ν) ≤ h is a child of a partition μ with ℓ(μ) ≤ h and |μ| = |ν|
− 1; then P(ν) follows by induction on |ν|. Let μ be ν with its smallest part lowered by 1 (and deleted if it be‐
comes 0). If the smallest part of ν is 1, then ν = μ ∪ (1) and ℓ(μ) + 1 = ℓ(ν) ≤ h, so ν is a new-class child of μ
(Definition A.2). Otherwise ℓ(μ) = ℓ(ν) =: ℓ and ν is μ with μℓ replaced by μℓ + 1: the raise child a = q − ℓ. ∎
So the ideals Kμ(L) are graded models of all the weight spaces of V⊗n, and the q rows of Kμ(L ∪ z) are exactly
the ideals of the q neighbouring weights. We proved Theorem A.15 as an upper bound, which is all that
Theorem A needs; that the bound is an equality was pointed out by the cold reader of this version (§14.6).

Appendix B. Theorem 0, modulo Pham's theorem and the intersection
numbers
This appendix proves Theorem 0 for n = 2k ≥ 2, m ≥ 3 and ∅ ≠ K ⊆ 𝒥. It follows [DS, §4] closely, with every
step written out. We use, without proof:
      (T1) Classical topology [Mil, §7]. H*(X) and H*(Y0) are torsion free, where Y0 := X ∩ {z0 = 0} is the
      Fermat hypersurface of dimension n − 1 in Pn; the intersection form ⟨ , ⟩ on Hn(X) is unimodular; and
      by the Lefschetz hyperplane theorem Hj(Y0) → Hj(Pn) is an isomorphism for j < n − 1. We also use
      three standard facts: the Gysin map Hn(X) → Hn−2(Y0) sends the class of a cycle meeting Y0 transver‐
      sally to the class of the intersection; two complex submanifolds of complementary dimension meeting
      transversally in one point have intersection number +1; and the action of G by holomorphic automor‐
      phisms preserves the intersection form and is compatible with Poincaré–Lefschetz duality [DS, §2].
      (T2) Pham's theorem [Ph], [DS, Theorem 2.1]. Let S be the Pham cycle of [DS, §2], an n-sphere in
      X ∖ Y0, and let G act on Hn(X ∖ Y0). The map R → Hn(X ∖ Y0), 1 ↦ [S], is surjective.
      (T3) The intersection numbers [DS, Theorem 2.2]. Fix η := eπi/m, and put s(η) := 1, s(η−1) := −1,
      and s(β) := 0 for the other m-th roots β of −1. Then ⟨LJ,β, S⟩ = sgn(σJ)·Πi=0k s(βi), where σJ is the permu‐
      tation (0, 1, …, n+1) ↦ (j0, k0, …, jk, kk).
The group G, generated by γi : zi ↦ ζzi (ζ := e2πi/m, i = 1, …, n+1), acts on X by holomorphic automorphisms;
we identify R with its group ring through ti ↦ γi. For an R-module M put M∨ := HomZ(M, Z) with the contra‐
gredient action (g·φ)(x) = φ(g−1x).
Step 1 (the Pham image is primitive, of corank 1). Let V := Im(Hn(X ∖ Y0) → Hn(X)). The cohomology se‐
quence of the pair (X, Y0), with Poincaré–Lefschetz duality, gives an exact sequence of R-modules Hn(X ∖ Y0)
→ Hn(X) → Hn−2(Y0) [DS, §2]. The second map is the Gysin map, which sends the class of a cycle meeting Y0
transversally to the class of the intersection. By (T1), Hn−2(Y0) ≅ Hn−2(Pn) ≅ Z (note n − 2 < n − 1), so
Hn(X)/V embeds in Z: V is primitive. The map Hn(X) → Z is non-zero: a standard space LJ,β meets Y0 transver‐
sally in the linear (k−1)-space LJ,β ∩ {z0 = 0} (on LJ,β, zk0 = β0 z0, and z0 is a non-zero linear form). The inter‐
section is transverse: at a point x, with H := {z0 = 0}, TxY0 = TxX ∩ TxH, so TxLJ,β ∩ TxY0 = TxLJ,β ∩ TxH, of di‐
mension k − 1. Its class generates Hn−2(Y0), since its image in Hn−2(Pn) is the class of a linear (k−1)-space.
Hence rank V = rank Hn(X) − 1. By (T2), R → V, 1 ↦ [S], is surjective.
Step 2 (duals of surjections). On R put [tν, tν'] := δν,ν' (ν, ν' ∈ (Z/m)n+1). This form is unimodular and G-in‐
variant, so x ↦ [x, ·] is an isomorphism R ≅ R∨ of R-modules. Let f : R → M be a surjective homomorphism of
R-modules. Then f∨ : M∨ → R∨ ≅ R is injective, its image is the ideal Ker(f)⊥ := {x ∈ R : [x, y] = 0 for all y ∈ Ker
f}, and R/Ker(f)⊥ is torsion free.2 Indeed, an element of the image vanishes on Ker f; conversely, if x ∈ Ker(f)⊥,
then [x, ·] vanishes on Ker f and factors through R/Ker f ≅ M, so it lies in the image. Finally, if c x ∈ Ker(f)⊥
with 0 ≠ c ∈ Z, then c[x, y] = 0 for y ∈ Ker f, so x ∈ Ker(f)⊥.
2 As stated in [DS, §4.2], this description of the image of f∨ requires the additional hypothesis that M/Im f be torsion free (for instance, f
surjective); this hypothesis holds in the case where [DS] apply it, the surjection of Step 1, so the result we use is not affected.

Apply this to the surjection f : R → V of Step 1. We obtain an ideal V∨ ⊆ R with R/V∨ torsion free. Explicitly,
the composite Hn(X) ≅ Hn(X)∨ → V∨ ⊆ R, where the first map is the (G-invariant) intersection form and the
second is restriction, sends τ to the element rτ with [rτ, tν] = ⟨τ, f(tν)⟩, that is
                                                       rτ = Σν ∈ (Z/m)n+1 ⟨τ, γν[S]⟩ tν.
Step 3 (the kernel is generated by the class of a linear section, which lies in LK). The restriction
Hn(X)∨ → V∨ is surjective, because V is a direct summand of Hn(X) (a primitive subgroup of a free group); so
Hn(X) → V∨ is surjective with kernel V⊥ (orthogonal for ⟨ , ⟩), which is primitive of rank 1. Let PX := [X ∩ Λ]
for a linear subspace Λ ⊂ Pn+1 of dimension k + 1 meeting X properly; this class does not depend on Λ, since
[X ∩ Λ] = hk ∩ [X] whenever the intersection is proper. Taking Λ ⊂ {z0 = 0} shows PX ⊥ V, since every class of
V is carried by a cycle in X ∖ Y0. And ⟨PX, [L]⟩ = 1 for a standard space L, since PX is the k-th power of the hy‐
perplane class cut on X and L is a linear space of dimension k. So PX is primitive and V⊥ = Z·PX. Now fix J ∈
K and β with βm = −1, and let ΛJ := {zki = β zji : i = 1, …, k}, of dimension k + 1. On ΛJ one has zjim + zkim = 0
for i ≥ 1, so X ∩ ΛJ = {z0m + zk0m = 0} ∩ ΛJ, the reduced union of the m standard spaces LJ,(β0, β, …, β), β0m = −1.
Hence PX ∈ LK(X), and therefore
                                        Hn(X)/LK(X) ≅ V∨/L'K, rank LK(X) = rank L'K + 1,
where L'K ⊆ V∨ ⊆ R is the image of LK(X). Since the maps are R-linear and LK(X) is an R-submodule, L'K is an
ideal of R.
Step 4 (the image of LJ is generated by ψJ). For ν ∈ (Z/m)n+1 write γν := Π γiνi and put ν0 := 0. Since γν
multiplies zi by ζνi, one has (γν)−1(LJ,β) = LJ,β' with β'i = ζνj − νk βi. For fixed J these translates exhaust all β
                                                                                i    i


(choose νji = 0 and adjust νki), so L{J}(X) is the R-module generated by [LJ,η], η := (η, …, η). By Step 2 and (T3),
its image is ψ'J := Σν aν tν with
                                  aν = ⟨LJ,η, γν[S]⟩ = ⟨(γν)−1LJ,η, S⟩ = sgn(σJ)·Πi=0k e(νki − νji),
where e(c) := s(ζ−cη), that is e(0) = 1, e(1) = −1 (because ζ−1η = η−1), and e(c) = 0 otherwise. The indices k0
and ji, ki (i ≥ 1) are 1, …, n+1, each once, so the sum factorises, and Σc e(c)tc = 1 − t and Σc,c' e(c' − c)tjc tkc' = (1
− tk)Σc (tjtk)c = (1 − tk)φ(tjtk) give
                                         ψ'J = sgn(σJ)·(1 − tk0)·Πi=1k(1 − tki)φ(tjitki) = ±ψJ.
Hence L'K = (ψJ : J ∈ K).
Step 5 (torsion transfer): Theorem 0(a). The exact sequence 0 → V∨/L'K → R/L'K → R/V∨ → 0 has a tor‐
sion-free cokernel, so every torsion element of R/L'K lies in V∨/L'K, and the torsion subgroups of V∨/L'K and
R/L'K = AK coincide. With Step 3, Tors(Hn(X)/LK(X)) ≅ Tors(AK).
Step 6: Theorem 0(b)–(d). (b) If char Kp = p with p = 0 or p ∤ m, then tm − 1 is separable over Kp, so R ⊗ Kp
        n+1
≅ Kpμm is the ring of functions on μmn+1, and AK ⊗ Kp is the ring of functions on the common zeros of the
ψJ, J ∈ K. For b ∈ μm, φ(b) = m ≠ 0 if b = 1 and φ(b) = 0 otherwise. So ψJ(a) ≠ 0 iff aki ≠ 1 for all i and ajiaki =
1 for i ≥ 1; since then aji = aki−1 ≠ 1, this says exactly a ∈ ΓJ. Hence dim(AK ⊗ Kp) = mn+1 − |ΓK|. (c) By Step 3
and (b) with p = 0, rank LK(X) = rank L'K + 1 = (mn+1 − rank AK) + 1 = |ΓK| + 1. (d) For p ∤ m, (b) with Kp =
F̄ p gives dimFp(AK ⊗ Fp) = rank AK, so AK has no p-torsion, and by (a) neither has Hn(X)/LK(X). ∎
